the commission has already adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. it is a clean and cheap source of energy that can be used very quickly across the eu by technologies that are already available and widespread. this is why reducing consumption and making our economy more energy efficient should be one of the eu's main strategic priorities. savings are also an integral part of the strategy to ensure europe's long-term energy security. it's been proven that every one percent improvement in energy efficiency will bring about a much greater decrease in our dependence on imports. for natural gas, this is a decrease of up to 2% in its import volume. and only if a very ambitious target is adopted will the current level of import dependency be maintained. otherwise, if the 30% target were to be adopted, our dependence would rise to over 70%. energy efficiency is also the future of our economic competitiveness and our economic development. we should clearly reject the view that efficiency is an obstacle to economic growth, that european policy in this area will only require hundreds of billions of euros of private and public spending without any significant economic or social benefits. in my opinion, the opposite is true. if the 40% energy efficiency target is approved, we will open the way to gdp growth and employment. in contrast, a target of 35% would lead to significantly lower results, even by 1,5% of gdp. even lower results are expected by models for targets set at even lower levels. similarly, a higher target will have an impact on lower international fuel prices, on the european price of electricity and, not least, on the price of emission allowances. the result will not be, as some have claimed, that ambitious energy saving commitments will reduce the competitiveness of european industry and have an overall negative impact on it. the european commission had all this data at its disposal. she worked with them to calculate the scenarios. she was aware of all the opportunities and benefits that energy efficiency offers. however, it ended up choosing one of the least ambitious 2030 targets of all possible models. by setting a 30% target, maintaining a number of exceptions and flexibility in article 7 and in many other areas, it has unfortunately not met the expectations that the coming decade brings. so i have to ask: what happened to the 'energy efficiency first' principle? so, what, we're just left with blank forms? because the facts clearly show that even 40% of the target can be achieved at an acceptable cost. and on the contrary, it will create new opportunities for construction, for the development of energy services, because the potential for energy savings is far from being fully exploited. in industry, less than half of the potential has already been used up, but in the case of buildings, for example, 80% of the potential is still untapped. it is in buildings that significant changes can be achieved at reasonable cost. by accelerating the renovation of european buildings, we will not only achieve long-term reductions in energy consumption, but also contribute to addressing the most pressing issues related to energy poverty. today, millions of europeans have great difficulty in paying for heat, energy or gas. very often, they also live in apartments that are not properly heated or that are unhealthy because of poor heating and ventilation. in doing so, each additional percentage of energy efficiency improvements will help these households to pay their energy bills. in conclusion, i would like to emphasise that by signing the paris climate agreement, we committed ourselves to taking measures that will lead to a significant reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, so that there is no increase in average temperature by two degrees celsius. energy savings are expected to make a significant contribution to this. however, the proposal put forward by the european commission is far from this. great results require great ambition. i believe we will be ambitious enough to achieve the same results in the case of energy efficiency.
in december last year, we voted in the itre committee for another major part of the so-called winter package. i won't deny that i could imagine a completely different conclusion. from the outset, the discussions have been very difficult to find a common compromise that would limit the ambition of the commission proposal, which drastically exceeds the position of the member states, because this will be a problem in the institutional negotiations. i know from experience that every objective must have economically justifiable costs, and i am completely lacking in these for the 40% energy efficiency target that was voted for. such a dramatic intervention in the energy and heating markets will require billions of investments not only from citizens and businesses but also from the state budget. i fear that the final bill for electricity and heat will not be paid by the citizens themselves, but by the parliament. energy is one of the key sectors affecting the competitiveness of the eu as a whole. if we override the setting of mandatory parameters for efficiency, renewable resources and the like, we can do very serious damage to the economies of the member states in the future.
the question posed by the blue card. i would like to ask the honourable member, since you speak so beautifully about the energy poverty of the socialists and the number of people in the eu who suffer from energy poverty, why did the socialist group not support the legal definition in the text, but only the very vague definition in the recital? because we are the legal definition in the text, which we could use in the subsequent negotiations, as the gue/ngl proposed. you socialists have just used something vague, which can be interpreted in any way.
mr president, commissioner, colleagues, you know this is not a committee meeting, so i will not speak on one or two issues at the expense of the complexity of other issues which are equally vital. i would say that this is definitely one of the most important proposals, a key proposal, that we have under this mandate. yes, it is also true that the european parliament, in comparison to the council, has done a fairly good job, but let me make two remarks. one is, yes ambition is important, but there are two things which are even more important: one is that we do something that is not doable, and the second is that we might be ambitious in some parts of this parliament, but we reach a fragmented result which sends an inadequate political message to the other institutions, primarily the council. i regret on some of the issues we were unable to maintain a broader coalition due to issues which are difficult to comprehend. so let us do a serious vote and let us send out a message and create a good basis for negotiations, because this is key and we do have to be ambitious and realistic.
at this point, i would normally thank the governance reporters for their great work and urge you to support the report. but we are faced with a document that lacks any deeper relationship and balance with the documents it is necessarily intended to cover. the current governance concept has given up on addressing the issues of overlapping policies and documents, such as the interaction of energy efficiency with the ets, which is exactly the problem it was intended to address. the document included only a vague definition of energy poverty, which is afflicting more and more people in the eu. the regulation contains an unrealistic linear trajectory for achieving objectives which not only do not reflect the economic capabilities of the states, but even the elementary laws of physics. instead of being a balancing instrument to achieve the objectives set out in other legislation, this regulation has been misused to push through the rapporteur's ambitious targets, which are intended to follow exactly the same trajectory as the emission reduction targets in the automotive sector. for that reason, i do not intend to support this report in any way.
i would very much appreciate a useful speech by commissioner cañete. we have heard a number of arguments which demonstrate the importance of the directive under consideration. i think we're all aware of it. we recognise that reducing consumption is essential to ensure the long-term competitiveness of our economy and is the key to reducing co2 emissions. it's also the most effective solution to energy poverty. for these reasons, we should adopt very ambitious measures to promote energy efficiency. the energy committee did so last december. it is shown that accepting a 40% commitment will have relatively large benefits for the european economy and businesses despite the high costs. in the case of buildings, we know that this is the sector where there is a huge potential for savings, of which we have only used 20% so far. we also need a very strong article 7 because the amount of derogations and flexibility that member states use then only brings about 0.75% of the savings instead of the expected 1.5%. i would also like to thank professor gierek and all the other rapporteurs for the very useful and very high-quality work they have done in preparing the report of the committee on energy and i would like to invite all colleagues to support this opinion of the committee on energy, research and innovation, because this directive is very important for the integration of the energy union as a whole.

in writing. this directive follows up on directive 2009/208/ec and the october 2014 european council decision, which envisaged a minimum of 27% renewable energy in the eu in 2030. the ep has asked the commission to raise this target to 30%, with renewables accounting for only 24.3% of consumption in 2030 if current trends continue. the directive provides for the promotion of renewable sources by appropriate state aid schemes and other measures, including cross-border expansion. the increase in renewable energy sources in cooling and heating is also foreseen. in transport, a harmonised approach is required in view of international trade in biofuels. in this context, the energy union governance proposal is an important coordination element. this includes planning where member states formulate national energy and climate plans, reporting and monitoring, with member states reporting on the progress of their national plans. the commission shall, by means of implementing acts, lay down the rules for the application of this regulation and the rules for the application of the delegated and implementing acts referred to in article 4 of regulation (eu) no 1290/2013. the commission will assess the impact of the aid on the union's energy supply and demand in the light of the conclusions of the european council in copenhagen on 26 and 27 june 2016. regardless of the reality of the estimates or targets, it is clear that without a carbon tax, these ambitious targets are not very realistic.
i strongly welcome the ambitious revised proposal for a directive of the european parliament and of the council on the promotion of the use of energy from renewable sources. i firmly believe that the european union will learn from its past mistakes and will not allow its policies in this area to continue to contribute to the deforestation of rainforests worldwide, the taking of land, the destruction of biodiversity and the devastation of local communities. in particular, i support the proposal that the contribution from biofuels and bioliquids produced from palm oil will be 0% from 2021. this proposal reflects the resolution on palm oil and rainforest destruction of 4 april 2017, in which the european parliament called on the commission to adopt measures to phase out the use of vegetable oils, including palm oil, which cause deforestation, in biofuels, by 2020, at the latest. on the other hand, i cannot agree with amendments nos 296 and 346 which require that biofuels, bioliquids and biomass fuels produced from forest biomass do not include straw and coal tar. the commission considers that the proposed measures would be in line with the principles of the union's sustainable forest management strategy, which is based on the principle of sustainable forest management. i believe that the directive should continue to allow the treatment of wood which is damaged in this way.

the report finds that climate change, in particular weather fluctuations, droughts and floods, negatively affect more the lives of those who are in some way threatened by poverty, illiteracy or whose dignity is not taken seriously enough in society. so it's mostly women. this report highlights important facts that we should consider when we seek solutions, such as the fact that women are the ones who suffer the most from all the climate fluctuations, because they are the ones who are the ones who are the ones who are the ones who are the ones who are running the household and looking after the household. on average, women worldwide carry out more than 70% of all water-related activities in areas affected by climate change. 70% of all women in the agricultural sector work there, but women are largely excluded from decision-making on economic or environmental policy. the fact that 781 million people under the age of 15 are illiterate, and two-thirds of them are women, also shows that there is a need to engage in this issue.
i want to share with you an experience and some information. the european anti-fraud office (olaf) is an impartial investigative body and its activities may involve high-level policy. this is the situation in hungary and also in the czech republic, where the project of prime minister babiš's "cats' nest" is under discussion. i have no doubt that olaf's investigations are independent and professional. that is why, in the case of politicians, speed of investigation and transparency are in the public interest. in this particular case, there was no reason to withhold the final report, which was eventually obtained and published by the media anyway. the olaf report shows that fraud has occurred. it will be a test for the rule of law in the czech republic, whether everyone is equal before the law. and lessons for national and european union authorities to be more transparent next time. at last, madame the president, allow me to share with you some personal information. today, i am the happy grandmother of a grandson named charles and i hope he will be happy in the european union we are now building.

i was shocked to read at the end of last year in the scientific journal science that the dead zones in our oceans, seas and especially coastal areas have quadrupled and increased. the dead zones are caused by lack of oxygen. i think we have to be able to fight this, that we even have the means and the tools to do so. we need to work better with the farmers, the local farmers, the local businesses, who can take simple measures to prevent these pollutants from entering coastal areas. mr faria's report describes very well what this loss of biodiversity means. we have the problem of emissions from shipping, which can go up by 50 percent, but even as much as 250 percent, these are all challenges that we face and we really need to address them. i was therefore happy to support this report by mr farii, who is dealing with it, i think, with honour.

on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, i would like to say to mr lange that although we usually agree in the committee on international trade on many things, this will not be one of them: eu trade agreements should not be extended that far beyond the scope of what we can call ‘trade policy instruments’. i have the feeling that what we can see here is a clear tendency to call for sanctions in trade agreements we are negotiating with third countries, and we in the ecr group oppose that approach. we worry that applying sanctions would have a counterproductive effect on how much our partners would be willing to commit in the scope of the tsd chapters. again, there are definitely limits to which we can use trade policy for some achievements in the field of human rights or environmental protection. there is no doubt that tsd chapters must be better implemented, but there are other instruments, other options, that need to be further explored. first of all, an introduction of measurable targets defining clear procedures on how to deal with complaints and how to launch government consultations. thirdly, to recognise that each trade partnership is a different one and that civil society takes different forms in different trade partners. last, but not least, we should also make better use of our own resources – our delegations, chambers of commerce contacts, member states’ embassies and all diplomatic channels. we have to use our eu soft power through the instrument of tsd chapters, and the only way i see that this can be achieved through trade policy is on the basis of cooperation and continuous dialogue. we have to treat our trading parties as partners and not threaten them with sanctions.

i would like to explain why i voted in favour of the renewable energy directive. i am in favour of increasing the share of renewable energy to 35%, as a binding target for the eu as a whole, but with flexibility for the national states. renewable energy is a good way to combat climate change and air pollution. of course, they are also opportunities for economic development and innovation. but sufficient flexibility should be left to member states. i also voted for the extension of the directive to aviation, because we know that aviation is one of the major polluters. i, on the other hand, did not advocate a too strict approach to biofuels. i'm all for bringing the first generation of biofuels to the level of 2017, which is a limitation for the future, but it doesn't negatively impact the investment that's already been made. the ep therefore adopted a proposal that is realistic and allows for a good bargaining position.
so let me tell you what the vote on the draft energy efficiency directive has finally come to. i voted in favour of the proposal because i consider energy saving to be a suitable tool for improving the environment. but progress cannot be faster than research and innovation. i also strongly support flexibility in setting and assessing the achievement of national targets in this area. i could not support raising the fixed mandatory target to 40%, which fortunately did not pass, and i did not vote for raising the target and for extending the renovation obligation to all public buildings. i consider that too big an impact on the state budgets. since a number of good amendments were tabled, i was able to vote in favour of the draft directive in the end.
i would like to comment on the last of the three proposals. it is a draft regulation on the governance of the energy union, and we voted on it last, because it actually closed the voting on the three pieces of legislation relating to the energy package. unfortunately, i was unable to endorse this draft regulation. in general, of course, i support a flexible approach for individual countries, and i therefore expected that this would be taken more into account when voting on amendments, for example, i did not support strict deadlines for national energy and climate strategies. i voted against the binding nature of national energy efficiency targets. i just think we need to leave a little more flexibility here. unfortunately, this third legislative proposal, the regulation, does not correspond fully to the two previous ones, which allowed for greater flexibility and rationality.

for the alde group. i would like to thank in particular the rapporteur, mrs tomcova, for her efforts and time devoted to this report. i really appreciate her efforts. we can speak many fine words about the youth employment initiative and argue with numbers and statistics. but for me, the main goal is quite simple, we have to teach young people to work. working is normal. that's my message to the graduates of the schools that the initiative is designed for. although the programmes financed by the initiative do not replace working hours, they make a lot of sense. i welcome the efforts to transform the initiative into a more sustainable financial instrument to tackle unemployment in the post-2020 period. the negotiations within the multiannual financial framework will ensure the necessary continuity of the programme. member states should not only make full use of the initiative's possibilities, but also invest themselves in promoting labour mobility, the potential of which remains untapped. the eu's workforce is about 243 million people, but only 8.5 million of them work within europe abroad. young people are the most willing to move for work. let's support them! for the group gue/ngl i also thank the two commissioners who have come here today to present the material we have all been waiting for, but i must say that the strategy presented to us leaves me with mixed feelings. on the one hand, i very much welcome the european commission's plans to work to ensure that all plastic packaging placed on the eu internal market is either reusable or easily recyclable by 2030, and that more than half of all plastic waste is recyclable by the same year. the single internal market for plastics is the right way forward, but it will require robust investment. we're facing a global plastic packaging crisis right now, and we don't have time to wait until 2030 and solve it by moving our plastic waste from china, where they don't want it, to africa. the situation in our oceans is unsustainable in the long run, and not just because of microplastics. it is therefore essential that ambitious legislation be put forward as soon as possible, on the basis of the strategy, by the commission itself. they must drastically reduce the consumption of both plastic items and packaging. we've done without them before, we'll have to do without them in the future. i'm not sure that we can rely on industry's voluntary commitments alone. after several studies of biodegradable plastics conducted in the czech republic, which found that in most cases these do not decompose even after two years in the soil, i am skeptical about them. we should start verifying what we're proposing, not letting ourselves be pampered by the industry over what they're proposing.
for the ppe group. allow me to thank all my colleagues on the juri committee, and in particular our rapporteur, mr tadeusz zwiefko, for the work they have done in the preparation of this report. i want to assure all citizens that we are here today to discuss a new draft of important legislation that affects many citizens. we register around 16 million international families in the eu. unfortunately, we also register 140,000 international divorces a year and, worst of all, 1,800 cases of child abduction by a single parent a year. the regulation governing jurisdiction, recognition and enforcement of decisions in matrimonial and parental matters has been in force, with the exception of denmark, since 2005. however, experience has shown that the existing wording can be improved, especially for the benefit of children. it is also necessary to ensure that this regulation only improves procedural rules. substantive law, such as the definition of marriage, is not covered by this regulation. subsidiarity is respected. the regulation also applies only to so-called crossborder cases, i.e. cases where each spouse is a national of another country. it is in the interest of juri and of this whole parliament that this legislation should be adopted because it is in the best interests of the child, and i can say on behalf of the juri committee that we have dealt with this issue, this aspect of the new proposal, very carefully.

in writing. the higher mobility of people in the eu also leads to a higher number of marriages between people of different nationalities. these marriages are not without conflict, separation and an increase in cross-border litigation. it is therefore appropriate to facilitate both the enforcement of judicial decisions given in another member state and the mutual recognition of such decisions. i support the proposal for a revision of the brussels ii regulation because it adapts the existing rules to this new reality. i also welcome the compulsory consultation of the child in cases where the child is able to express his or her opinion on the matter under consideration. i believe that this obligation will help the court to reach a decision that is in the best interests of the child. in this regard, there is a need to strengthen mutual trust between member states by recognising such interviews of children between member states. the abolition of the procedure for the declaration of enforceability of judgments in all areas covered by the regulation will also contribute to strengthening this mutual trust and to shortening the whole of the judicial procedure. i also consider it useful for the proceedings on international child abduction to be conducted by specialised courts in each member state. this specialisation should increase the quality of those decisions and, in fact, shorten the time limits for their issuance.
for the group gue/ngl. first of all, allow me to thank and congratulate the rapporteur on his work. the marrakesh treaty will give more than 300 million blind, visually impaired and dyslexic people access to books and other printed materials, which are currently hampered by the copyright problems mentioned by ms wikström. people who are blind, visually impaired or have reading difficulties continue to face barriers to accessing books and other printed material. the need to increase the number of works and other subject matter in accessible formats such as braille, audiobooks and enlarged fonts has finally been recognised at international level. this will enable the persons and organisations concerned to make accessible format copies of the works and to distribute them throughout the eu and in third countries that are contracting parties. we will increase their access to education and their ability to participate fully in life.
education and full participation of blind, visually impaired or reading-impaired people in society are also linked to the accessibility of books, textbooks and other printed works. yet today, only 5% of them are published in accessible formats, and in developing countries, almost none are published at all. accessible books are often produced at the expense of disability organisations, which cannot provide them across borders because of copyright. the marrakesh treaty, which removes these obstacles, has already been ratified by 33 countries around the world and it is very important that the eu countries join them as soon as possible. more than 30 million europeans with severe visual impairments, who have had very limited or no access to books and other printed material because of copyright, have been waiting too long for the directive to come into force.
i am convinced that persons who are blind, visually impaired or have other reading difficulties continue to face many obstacles in accessing books and other printed material. i agree with my predecessors that, unfortunately, one of the obstacles has been the lax approach of the european institutions. he may not have been lax, but he did delay access to these cultural assets for this particular group of people. i am therefore very pleased that he has advanced the ratification process so that we can now conclude it. i would very much like to see us learn from this example, which we are currently dealing with here, so that the next time the union's institutions and bodies will act in the best interests of these specific individuals, they will do so as quickly as possible and as efficiently as possible.

for the group gue/ngl. first of all, i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done on the report. the report does have some positive elements, such as the recognition of the importance of regulated professions for the service or innovation sectors. but i have a problem with the basic principle of the proposed regulation. i consider that the obligation imposed on member states to notify the commission of the changes they have made to their national legislation in the field of regulated professions is directly contrary to the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. after all, each country must be able to determine for itself what requirements and qualifications are required for the exercise of a particular profession. regulation of certain professions is necessary to ensure the protection of the public interest, a high level of consumer protection and a high quality of service. while i support the system of mutual recognition of diplomas and qualifications, i believe that the regulation of the conditions for the exercise of regulated professions must remain entirely within the competence of the member states. i do not want the commission to dictate to czech candidates for the post of lawyer the length of concipient practice or to prescribe the need for education for czech doctors, etc. i will advise my political group to abstain from voting on the motion. madam president, 71% of european gdp and 68% of total employment come from services. regulated professionals account for 22% of all workers. with such numbers, it is shocking and unacceptable that, in many member states, the free movement of services is still not respected and promoted. it is equally shocking that member states, instead of moving towards harmonised regulations on professionals, continue to invent new national barriers to prevent free movement and the equal treatment of all eu citizens. i am here to tell you that a czech professional is just as good as a german, french or any other professional. we must deregulate where restrictions are unjustified and harmonise where they are. we cannot continue to allow economic protectionism within the single market.
recognition of professional qualifications is one way of promoting mobility and competitiveness within the eu. the 2013 revision of the directive was a step in the right direction. the obligation for member states to notify the commission of the list of their regulated professions and the possibility for member states to assess each other's national requirements also contribute to the removal of unjustified obstacles. in order to remove barriers, it is also important to make the professions in different countries more comparable. improving the database of all regulated professions would help to achieve this. the obligation on the state to assess the proportionality of the requirements it imposes before adopting a new regulation is also useful. however, this assessment is carried out differently in the member states. to effectively remove excessive requirements and prevent protectionism, it is necessary to ensure the same practice in all countries. i therefore support the proposal for a directive to establish a uniform framework for assessing the adequacy of national requirements. the common framework should help ensure that the proposed regulation is not a mere formality. at the same time, this would strengthen confidence between member states. i would also welcome the extension of the professional card beyond the current 5 professions for which it applies, to keep pace with the development of digital technologies and to remove administrative barriers.
if we are to continue to break down barriers in the internal market, one area must be occupational mobility. a common procedure at union level should remove disproportionate obstacles while guaranteeing a high quality of service. professional regulation is an important tool for protecting the public interest, patient safety and consumer protection. i agree that transparency and comparability of qualifications for regulated professions is essential and that the main benchmark for comparison should be quality of service rather than mere numbers. all stakeholders, with the social partners at the forefront, should be more closely involved in consultations on professional regulation. only in this way can we strengthen the trust which is absolutely essential for the functioning of the internal market. however, in addition to training and skills development, we also think about how to ensure that professionals have the right conditions to pursue their profession in the context of labour mobility and without it, i.e. on the domestic labour market.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i had the honour to chair the negotiations on this file on behalf of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, and i can truly thank ms thun for all her work and all her efforts. this regulation is a good first step. no longer can disneyland say that the french can get cheaper tickets than czechs. this regulation fixes this. no longer can a company redirect customers without their consent. if i ask for the german website, i want a german website, not a czech one. this regulation fixes this. no longer can a company refuse because a credit card was issued in another country. if i go shopping in austria, my visa card is just as good as an austrian visa card. this regulation fixes this. this said, we must address the elephant in the room: copyrighted content. let’s be honest, this regulation will not fix the problem of blocked access to movies, tv, software or even e—books. it will not fix the first item that people think of when they hear the word geo—blocking and we must continue to move towards the end of this type of geo—blocking. we must end this artificial barrier in the digital single market.
mr president, the prohibition of discrimination on grounds of nationality is already laid down in the services directive. we need to adapt internal market rules to today's digital reality and say no to blocking. the regulation will enable citizens to buy goods electronically on fair terms, book accommodation, use services across the eu and finally, without distinction of origin. the proposal is, in my view, reasonably balanced. consumers should have access to goods and services under the same conditions, but this will not impose unreasonable costs on traders, nor will it automatically impose an obligation to comply with the law of the consumer's country, nor an obligation to deliver goods throughout the eu. i think it is right that the regulation will not apply to services supplied electronically and works that are protected by copyright, such as books, music and others, and that should be the case until we resolve the fair funding of authors.
mr president, discrimination against consumers on the basis of their nationality or country of residence is unfortunately a daily reality in the european union's internal market, and not just in the online environment. limited choice, different payment terms, unjustified price differences, but also different quality of the same goods at first sight are unpleasant experiences for many consumers. the geo-blocking regulation will limit some of these discriminatory practices and make it easier for consumers to access a range of goods and services. this is a step in the right direction and i would like to thank in particular the rapporteur, mrs róża gräfin von thun und hohenstein. the work to complete a fair internal market, where consumers have equal access to goods and services but also where consumers are not knowingly and intentionally misled, certainly does not end with its adoption. i trust that the commission will take this into account when preparing the package of measures for the new consumer policy, which we are eagerly awaiting.

madam president, i would like to explain why i supported the regulation which ended up with a large majority of 557 meps. i would also like to thank mrs róża gräfin von thun und hohenstein, who has called for the adoption of this report. the regulation is intended to enable and facilitate citizens to buy goods and services at a distance throughout the european union. a clear prohibition of geo-blocking is proposed, specifying the conditions under which a redirection may occur and what cannot be considered discriminatory. the prohibition of blocking and related prohibitions will apply only to certain goods and services for the time being. electronically supplied services protected by copyright are therefore excluded. i agree with this restriction. the conditions should be set so that the extension of access to audiovisual works does not come at the expense of copyright.
madam president, i have supported this outcome of the trilateral negotiations. it was certainly very difficult to negotiate a compromise, but the result is a directive that aims to deliver on the commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 43% by 2030. it's a compromise that is acceptable. it's ambitious, but it's also achievable. it contains measures to prevent so-called carbon leakage and the shifting of investments to other countries. keeping the reduction coefficient at 2,2% is also a better solution than increasing it. i also consider important in the compromise that has been reached that the most efficient industries will have more free allowances. this is a very important measure that promotes innovation and modernisation, which also increases overall competitiveness.
i believe, madam president, that the report on the acceleration of clean energy innovation, which has been well received by mr buzek, is a good response by the european parliament to the commission's communication, because it has enriched the commission's communication with some important aspects. i consider the greatest contribution of this report to be the emphasis on a comprehensive strategy for the export of sustainable and clean energy technologies. the european union is ahead of the rest of the world in this area and there is a demand for these technologies. their exports therefore have great economic potential which our industry can exploit. i also strongly agree with the emphasis in this report on clean energy innovation from the bottom up, from citizens and municipalities. in europe and the czech republic, there are many successful examples of households and entire communities that are energy self-sufficient and do not pollute the environment. we should continue to encourage this approach.
madam chairman, female circumcision is a barbaric custom. today, there are some 140 million women worldwide who have undergone this shameful and dangerous operation. about 15 years ago, when i was a filmmaker living in kenya, i was commissioned to make a film about female circumcision. i did a 90-minute documentary with a small crew in a samburu village. it's called josephine's african story. men were not allowed to circumcise themselves. here's to our female cameraman whose professionalism has been able to drown out the shock she's been subjected to. josephine's 12-year-old samburu girl lay on the hammer outside the barn, crying in pain and humiliation. her own surgery made her a dull razor. it took about five minutes. then the women carried josephine to a flower-decorated hut. meanwhile, the men danced nearby and celebrated the occasion by drinking tea with milk. why am i telling you all this, ladies and gentlemen? in europe, the superstition prevails that only muslims practice this medieval custom in africa. it's not true. josephine was a christian, her parents were christians, the old lady with the razor was a christian. like the rest of the samburu, they were christianized decades ago by italian missionaries. the barbarity of female circumcision is not conditional on confession. it has nothing to do with faith. it's a manifestation of primitive common law.
mr president, if we are to discuss the western balkan countries and their ambitions to join the european union, we must first of all bear in mind that serbia is a key country in the region. the persistent efforts of a number of european union politicians to weaken the state have borne fruit. instead of yugoslavia and albania, there are two small member states croatia and slovenia. then there are albania, serbia, bosnia and herzegovina, with a structure that strongly resembles a confederation, montenegro, which separated from the rest of yugoslavia only by a very special voting mechanism in a referendum, and macedonia, which, moreover, cannot even be called macedonia. all non-eu countries have complex religious and ethnic situations, and all have an association treaty with the eu, as well as variously-conceived treaties on the free movement of persons. the eu's policy is to subordinate the foreign policy of these states to its own ideas, and the convulsive attempt to tear the province of kosovo from serbia is despicable. it is time to take clear positive steps towards the western balkan states, which is why i welcome this discussion.

madam president, the attack on afrin has reopened a serious dilemma. a nato member state, our military ally, is committing violence against the kurdish national minority on its own territory and against kurds on foreign soil, in syria and iraq. the dilemma is not only holding back the resolution of the middle east conflict, but also the continuation of the eu's constructive relations with turkey. it also threatens the eu-turkey refugee agreement. the resulting dilemma takes on absurd to picturesque proportions. the united states, turkey's strongest nato ally, is dropping munitions and weapons on the military units of the ypg armed forces, who are now fighting turkey in rojava and afrin. the us air force has directly undertaken air training of ypg ground operations. furthermore, unlike turkey, the united states does not consider the ypg to be terrorists. solving the dilemma of who our ally is in afrin requires a great deal of diplomatic skill. but it also requires the ability to answer the question of whether turkey's brutal human rights violations are acceptable at the cost of weakening the military alliance. i recently visited rojava and the kurdish ypg. i saw how bravely the kurds captured isis's capital city of raqqa, how bravely they started to rebuild the city of kobani, which was destroyed by isis. they are allies of our american allies. we can't throw them overboard just to maintain the status quo between nato and turkey.

madam president, inequality is nothing new. but we have to be concerned that in times of economic growth, inequality doesn't decrease. for example, while digitalisation creates new opportunities, it is necessary to invest in and develop new skills to take advantage of them. then there's a growing gulf between those who take advantage of new opportunities and those who just can't do it without help. that's why we need to talk about inclusion, about quality education for all. it is more difficult for young people to find a place on the labour market if they have no experience. new, non-standard forms of work such as crowdworking, while allowing flexibility to work for those who otherwise would not be able to work, also create a category of second-class workers without social protection. the solution to inequality is not to introduce a so-called guaranteed income. one of the reasons is that it lacks the motivating element. at the european union level, programmes that address the issue of access to education are helping. reducing inequality means, first and foremost, increasing opportunities. and we have the opportunity to strengthen the erasmus+ programme, which is still exclusive, there are still very few young people who get to participate in this programme. so if we want to reduce inequality, we also need to increase the budget for erasmus+, which allows for funding projects to modernise education, emphasising apprenticeships and practical and entrepreneurial skills.
in writing. the eurozone crisis, brexit, the refugee crisis and other crises of recent years have exposed the eu's weaknesses and reduced our citizens' trust in the eu. this trust needs to be restored in time before serious irreversible damage occurs. i believe that the reform of the electoral legislation should contribute to a greater interest in voting, to a greater participation. the amendments proposed are aimed at increasing transparency and identification with european political parties and their candidates. if we want to reduce the democratic deficit, then we must strive for greater voter turnout and understanding of the european elections. for example, in the czech republic, the voter turnout in the 2014 european parliament elections was only 19.5%, which is more than two thirds lower than in the last elections to the czech chamber of deputies (60.84%). i'm sure other states have similar problems. i agree with the proposals to make european parties more visible, such as the proposal to include the logo of european parties on the ballot paper. the awareness of european parties in the member states is very limited and there is therefore a need for european parties to be more visible and to penetrate the consciousness of voters.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. – mr president, let me start by saying that the european investment bank (eib) is the institution that helped us overcome post—crisis problems and it has for sure a firm place in our future. in doing so, there are two issues that i want to highlight from the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ). first of all, all public support – even from the development bank – is not free of risk. here, especially, i am talking about the risk of crowding—out private financing. we, as well as the eib, must be fully aware of this risk and try to minimise it. secondly, the eib is a bank. banking is about money and risks; about finding the right balance. too low a risk means limited impact. too high a risk can lead to losses in capital. too narrow a focus means that we will be missing some targets. too many mandates at the same time can lead to fragmentation and to high costs. we should keep finding the right balance and finding the trade—offs, to be focused and efficient in the work and the eib. this is the way to ensure that the eib will serve good purposes for the future.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, this has been a very interesting debate and also very encouraging for me, because we have to focus more on the support of civil society and ngos, not only in the member states, which have been mentioned here, but in the whole of europe. in real democracy, people are encouraged and invited to participate in decision-making. a healthy, self-confident society welcomes people to have a say. in a fake democracy, people are invited once every four years to come and cast their vote, to elect, and to be silent in between. this is the real threat to democracy, and i think that it is our role to – fight against it is maybe too activist – but to be very vigilant, to monitor the situation and to give support where it is needed, because silencing and shrinking civil society is one of the parameters of populism. populists hate civil society because they love a strong state and, unfortunately, in part of our society there is now the demand for an ever-stronger state, because there are people who promise they will do everything to facilitate things for you, to guarantee you a happy life. they want everything but critical thinking from the people. unfortunately, online and digital development is not helping. we should analyse this phenomenon more, and what it means, because when i speak to the courageous people of the ngos, they say hate speech online is something which is censoring and silencing them. it is not only journalists, but also a large number of people working for ngos, publicly-known people, who are affected by hatred online. it is a factor which we should think about and maybe do something about. there is also the factor of societal changes, which we see in relation to the development of the digital area. i have nothing against digital development. there are many positive things in this respect, but what we see is that the populists like this sphere because they have an easily manipulated crowd. again, this is something which goes directly against civil society, and i think that we should analyse the reasons for shrinking space for civil society, react appropriately and find remedies. i do not think that the only possible remedy we have in hand is infringement against a state where we see that the law is deviating from how the legislation should look. of course, we are monitoring the situation in hungary and in poland. today we discussed romania. we are monitoring some other member states where there are worrying developments – not only in the east of the eu; i must make this clear. maybe the approach of the commission might be seen as pretty legalistic, that we wait for a bad law to be enacted before we act, but we have to be very careful here and maybe trust more the people in europe that there are enough democrats who will not be silenced so easily. we have to be careful and we have to support civil society by means other than infringements directed against the laws in some member states. how can we do it? the fund – i am very much for creating such a fund. we are now launching a feasibility study where we want to map the various funding instruments in the member states, to fill the gap and not to duplicate or do something which would not be systemic and transparent enough. i also have an expert meeting, and i invite those who are interested in this issue to participate in this debate, because i think that it should be our common work. let me conclude by speaking about big money, which is also now in debates such as the multiannual financial framework (mff) after 2020. here we have a great chance to open better and easier possibilities for the ngos to get funding. i am not so sure about opening the special chapter in mff which was, i think, the proposal of mr boni. i think that we should deliberately and thoroughly analyse the possibility of opening the space for ngos, who in many cases substitute the tasks of the state while being miserably paid. we have to change this situation. we see one year of financing, so the ngos invest 90% of their initiatives in collecting or seeking money, instead of doing the core thing. we also have to look into the rules for financing. we will hear more and more about the need to simplify the rules for the new mff. whenever i hear politicians, including myself, speaking about simplification, i start to fear we will complicate it more. now we have to manage to simplify the rules, especially for the small-scale projects, not to allow again a system where you have to invest as much effort into getting eur 100 000 as eur 5 million. the system and the rules must be proportionate. eu funding is my hobby. i could continue, but i will spare you. thank you very much, and let us work together to stop the shrinking of space for civil society. it is vital for europe and it is vital for all democrats in europe.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, first of all i would like to respond to the question from ms sophia in ‘t veld. i would like to underline that i speak on behalf of the presidency of the council and therefore in the name of all its members, and for this reason it is not for the presidency to comment on particular subjects related to the member states. but what i can underline is that the council shares the view of the european parliament that civil society plays a vital role in our societies, and – as you said – it is an essential part of the system of the checks and balances. in 2017, the council encouraged the member states to promote eu citizenship and the values and rights attached to it, involving as far as possible civil society – it was underlined. secondly, i would like also to conclude by saying that an empowered and resilient civil society in all its diversity is a crucial component and voice, as mentioned by some of you, of any democracy. ensuring that civil society has sufficient space to operate in support and protection of fundamental rights in the european union remains a common endeavour for the european institutions and the member states.

for the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, when it comes to human rights defenders, both men and women deserve our support. there is no gender difference in their bravery, their effort, or the suffering they often endure. i want to talk about women human rights defenders because they are naturally more vulnerable physically and psychologically. and they're also more sensitive to certain topics. it's no coincidence that the first pakistani woman to win a nobel prize was a young malala in 2014, who almost lost her life defending the right of girls to education at the age of 15. she saw education as a defense against radical ideas. in 2013, she was awarded the sakharov prize by the european parliament. while we do not have to look for women activists and human rights defenders in third countries, there are much more serious problems that women are facing outside the eu. as a successful example of their struggle, i would cite saudi arabia. thanks to the efforts of women, there are relaxations in laws, such as in guardianship. women no longer have to ask for their husband's permission and approval when they go to the doctor. in fact, from june of this year, women will be able to drive themselves. they fought it out. i see the call for civil society as a necessity. it can also be helpful in this regard by monitoring and promoting concrete examples of human rights defenders. mr president, let me mention one other human rights defender who cannot be overlooked, and that is mother teresa. she didn't talk about human rights, but she gave her whole life to them.
before proceeding with catch-the-eye, i must say that i am glad to chair the session on this item, although i regret that i could not be in the chamber – and, indeed, take the floor – because i think that the defence of human rights among women is something special, as it is so very difficult. also, as vice-president responsible for human rights, i would love to speak on the item and pay tribute not just to those whose names have been mentioned but also to many, many others. catch—the—eye procedure
member of the commission, on behalf of the vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, this has been a very useful exchange, and i thank the honourable members for their interventions. from this debate, i conclude that we all recognise the vital importance of women human rights defenders and share common commitments to help protect them and promote their work. we are living in extremely difficult times for civil society and human rights defenders all around the world. we are collectively facing a situation where the space for civil society is shrinking. however, let me be clear that the european union will never leave human rights defenders alone, and we will continue to work together with you and civil society to defend human rights defenders around the world. may i close by signalling once again my appreciation of parliament’s role in keeping this important issue on the agenda.

mr president, commissioner, hundreds of petitions to the european parliament, a number of national initiatives, at least one oral question every year. that shows the urgency of the problem. professor merrow, a chronobiologist, told us here in the european parliament hearing that 20% of the population has health problems, and 80% of the population doesn't believe that 20% have health problems. another professor of chronobiology, till roenneberg of munich, tells us that the social jet lag that this time change causes leads to an increase in smoking, an increase in alcohol and caffeine consumption, depression, obesity, type 2 diabetes, metabolic problems. it is estimated that both direct and indirect costs amount to up to 1% of gdp. the uncertainty is why the regime still exists. energy savings have not occurred and there is even no evidence of increased sports activity due to daylight saving time. i am glad that you have stated that the commission is revising this directive, which means that mr beleta's amendments are superfluous. on behalf of the 20% i ask the commission to take legislative action.
madam president, we have a heated debate at home and in the european parliament about the time change, but citizens should know that we do not spend more than an hour on this in our plenary session. because we're spending a lot more time on economic, social and security issues. but time-shifting is a burning issue for a large number of citizens, and it is our duty to address their challenge. so i think we need to look at the fact that energy savings have not been demonstrated, but that the time change has an impact on the health of citizens. of course, there are people who want to spend more time outdoors in the summer doing leisure activities, and i'm one of those people, because i also like to play tennis until nine o'clock in the evening in the summer, but nobody gives me that time in the morning. so i am in favour of making health the main argument and supporting a resolution calling for the abolition of time and calling on the commission to act.
for the alde group. madam president, i find it unacceptable that an organisation which defends human rights should face attacks, prosecutions, imprisonment or, in the extreme, death for its work. this is especially true if the organization is dedicated to honoring the memory of victims of crimes committed by the communist regime. in the case of chechnya, it is striking in this regard how often representatives of civil society are accused, for example, of drug offences. it is our duty to draw attention to this situation and it is appropriate to demand the immediate release of mr titiyev and all other political prisoners, whether they are detained in chechnya or in russia. it will soon be 30 years since the first war in chechnya, and sadly, people in this country continue to live in fear and repression because of their beliefs.

madam president, the situation of human rights defenders in russia is getting worse. we must remember the memorial, which has a distinguished record of human rights work. russia needs to know that we are following the case of oyub titiev and will hold accountable all those who have so outrageously intimidated and persecuted mr. titiev and the memorial organization. we strongly urge his immediate release, the withdrawal of the false charges and an investigation into all forms of intimidation that memorial is subjected to in chechnya and elsewhere. we have nowhere to retreat, these are the lives of specific brave people, and even if we leave the value debate about human rights, the statistics directly show that democracy and human rights are directly linked to well-being. so ordinary russians should clean up their own messes in their own interests.
madam president, i too join my colleagues in objecting to the fundamental inadmissibility of the death penalty and in citing those specific cases. i'd like to focus my minute on something else, and that's the role of diplomacy. i think it is within the diplomatic possibilities of the european union institutions to put substantial pressure not only on the egyptian government but also to open up this issue of the inadmissibility of the death penalty in the international arena. unfortunately, we do not have many partners in the international arena on this issue, and i think it would be appropriate for the european union to take this issue forward in a fundamental way. and then i would also emphasize a certain theory of conditionality for the continuation of cooperation and support. i think that in this fundamental demand, which is being made across all political groups, we must also support those states which show some willingness to cooperate on the question of the inadmissibility of the death penalty. and then it should be reflected in the support we give them.
madam president, it is estimated that there are some 30,000 children living in orphanages in haiti. but what few people know is that 80 percent of these children have at least one parent. what's the reason for the kids being there? the main reasons are economic. this country, as mentioned here, has been through an earthquake. it's been hit by two major hurricanes in the past year, and a lot of parents just don't have the money to take care of their kids. and here is our task as meps, europeans, the european union, to appeal to the haitian authorities. first of all, the government to start doing something about this situation. the commission has also provided information on the situation of the union industry in the prc, including the findings of the investigation. and so i'm glad that we have a very powerful commissioner here who can do something about this situation, and i believe that he will help the haitian people to change this situation.
madam president, i would like to say that it is a vicious circle of poverty, corruption, lack of education, lack of paid work, tragic health care and, in fact, a dysfunctional state that is in ruins as a result of multiple natural disasters. of course, slavery is fundamentally unacceptable, whatever it is called, but in haiti, there seems to be some direct connection between poverty and slavery. i agree with mrs luck that the government is responsible for this situation, but i am not sure whether this should lead us to impose sanctions or even to open the question, because those who will be affected by these sanctions will be the children in the first place. and i think the only way to do that is to really be more vigilant about the money that we send to haiti, to work with independent ngos on this issue, and to really be more vigilant about that money, because that's the only way we can actually bring some benefit to those groups.
mr president, i have always been a supporter of turkey, including its attempt to join the european union, but i have to say that even i am gradually changing my mind because what is going on in turkey these days is simply crossing a red line. we see that gradually democracy in this country is being curbed. we see political pressure on free media, on the judiciary, on state administration, on the school system and universities. we see various strange conspiracy theories being spread around and we see also a gradual departure from the secular character of the state. i think that it is time, therefore, to call particularly on the ruling party to stop this and therefore such a resolution is fully justifiable, and i supported it.
mr. president, the situation in venezuela is serious, it deserves our attention, it deserves our criticism. we have, after all, commented on the state of human rights and democracy in various countries. as for venezuela, we have here an exemplary example of the collapse of real socialism, or the latin american version of it. the collapse of the nationalized economy and the collapse of central management. and we also have an example of a certain hypocrisy, especially on the left of the political spectrum of this parliament, which, as i remember, was very critical of the situation in colombia, because it was a center-right government, so colombia was bombarded from different political positions. there was an attempt to block even the negotiation or ratification of the free trade agreement with colombia and now all of a sudden, when we have a similar case here in venezuela, the european left is silent in this parliament. i think it's a little hypocritical to have to measure everyone with the same yardstick.
thank you, although it was not on venezuela.

mr president, i have supported the approach of the part of the european parliament which thinks it is good to introduce only one time, so that there is no alternation between summer and winter time. i believe that in a time when even the experts are clearly saying that time-shifting has no economic benefits, to maintain time-shifting at a time when it is causing problems for certain groups of the european population, the elderly, the sick, the young, i think it is losing its meaning. i therefore think it is good that we have adopted a resolution today, albeit in a softer form, and it would be good for the european commission to deal with this and to really preserve in europe at a certain point in time only one time that will not cause problems for any group of our citizens and will not even cause problems for certain species of farm animals.
mr president, i am usually very critical of the european union, but on this subject i must say that the european parliament adopted a resolution today which actually opens the way to deregulation on the subject of the changeover of summer and winter time. interestingly, russia abolished the summer-winter time seven years ago. if we hurry, and i understand that in the european union everything takes longer, our delay against russia will not be so great. if we agree that the changeover should be abolished, then i am very much in favour of abolishing winter time. to keep summer time permanently for the european union.
mr. chairman, i would like to remind you that i first encountered this initiative at a simple baker's in a small village near chrudimi. his name was pecka, the baker. and that's when petr pithart promised him in his election campaign that if he supported him in the second round, he would push for this change in the summer and winter time. unfortunately, that's not what happened. then peter schiller started promoting it and it eventually made it into our program. i don't think today's vote is going to be crucial in any way. but i think it's a good feeling to just change the established routine of changing summer and winter time, which has no economic or other significance. and i think it doesn't matter if it's summer or winter time. i think that for those of us who voted for this resolution, the thing that bothers us most is that the time is changing. and hopefully, we'll live to see that cancellation.

in writing. – the european council discussed important topics for its future seeking to find common ground, acceptable for all. we shouldn’t be divided on issues where we should be united. tackling effects of the migration crisis is our common endeavour. we have to allocate resources in the financial framework which would help us to tackle migratory challenges. these resources, nevertheless, shouldn’t be unduly mixed with other european issues, such as cohesion policy and structural funds in general. these have been created to underpin a strong european economy and cohesion between member states within the single market. creating undue linkages with other sectorial agenda would compromise their effectiveness and added value. this does not mean conditionalities shouldn’t be put in place. the rule of law is a basic prerequisite not only for participating in the european project but also in the common market, and they could be included in one way or another in close linkage with the spending of the budget. this does not mean either that we shouldn’t fight to have resources to address migratory pressures. on the contrary, these are needed. but resources to address such pressures should stay transparently separated to be able to withstand migratory challenges effectively and in a united way.
the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the commission decision to activate article 7(1) teu as regards the situation in poland (2018/2541(rsp)). i would like to inform you that there will be no catch-the-eye procedure and no blue cards will be accepted.
today we discussed the commission's proposal to activate article 7 of the eu treaty against poland. i would stress that this article has never been used before in the history of the eu and could be a dangerous precedent. i think that the situation in poland and the ongoing reforms, for example in the judicial system, may not be to our liking and are probably not very democratic either. but to threaten a member state with the loss of its voting rights in the council or with even stricter sanctions for its national reforms is wrong at a time when the eu is in deep crisis and we need to be united. although i don't identify with all the changes and reforms that are taking place in poland, i couldn't support the proposal.
i congratulate my colleague pilar ayus and the shadow rapporteurs on the result, because the text presented is in many respects clearer and more consistent than the text of the current regulation. i am satisfied that the maximum quantities of sugar and sweeteners that can be added to spirit drinks are fixed, as the maximum sugar content was not fixed in the original regulation and this is clearly a significant improvement. from the point of view of my country, i am particularly satisfied that the exemption from the production of plum has been maintained, because the plum produced in the czech republic with the addition of alcohol has remained in the draft regulation in the same wording as the existing one, which is clearly the right decision. i would recommend voting against amendments 27, 28. we are of the opinion that the status quo is the possibility of using beer alcohol in alcoholic beverages and spirits, i.e. it is better not to expand the raw materials from which the alcohol can be produced.
so after 10 years, we have a new regulation on the table that concerns spirits, and i appreciate the changes that simplify the rules. i think it is right that the ethyl alcohol produced in the production of non-alcoholic beer should also be used in the production of spirits. the rules need to be adapted to the new reality, and that is the increased production of non-alcoholic beer. there is no substantive reason why producers should be restricted in the disposal of residual ethyl alcohol. that is why, unlike some of my colleagues, i support the amendments that make it possible. exports could also be jeopardised by a change in the language regime, which would restrict the use of a geographical indication for third countries for exports in the language of those third countries. i will therefore also support the amendment in this area and the protection of the traditional recipe for producers and consumers should also apply. the positive thing for the czech republic is that the conditions for plum will not change.

the next item is the report by frank engel, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the situation of fundamental rights in the eu in 2016 (2017/2125(ini)) (a8—0025/2018).
member of the commission. first, let me say hello to our countrymen who are, according to my information, on the balcony, good evening, good health. yes, i am a politician after several years. but to be serious, for this is a very serious topic that we are going to discuss here today. first of all, let me start my contribution by strongly condemning the shocking murder of two innocent young people, one of whom was a brave journalist. this is not just an attack on people, it is an attack on freedom of speech and on democracy and our values, and i strongly believe that the slovak authorities, perhaps in cooperation with the european agencies, europol and eurojust, will find and punish the perpetrators so that we can see that justice works and justice lives in slovakia and in the european union. now let me come back to the report and start by thanking the rapporteur, mr engel, for drafting it. this is a very comprehensive report which touches upon a number of issues that are very relevant to the current context. first, let me highlight the crucial importance of upholding eu values, including the rule of law and the rights enshrined in the charter of fundamental rights. i assure you that the commission is using all the means at its disposal to promote and uphold the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights. this includes the application of the rule of law framework and the launching of infringement proceedings when needed, as well as triggering the mechanisms of article 7 of the treaty where necessary. in line with its communication on the enforcement of eu law, the commission has begun to pursue a strategic approach with regard to infringement proceedings, in particular when respect for the eu charter of fundamental rights is at stake. the commission has initiated infringement proceedings to fight a key challenge, the segregation of roma children in education, which is explicitly raised in the report. the commission has mobilised and aligned its targeted and mainstreamed policy and funding instruments to promote inclusive reforms of public policies. on the rule of law, as announced in the 2018 work programme, the commission will, towards the end of this year, present an initiative to strengthen its enforcement within the eu. secondly, you have raised the issue of the shrinking space for civil society in europe. within the last month, the commission has strongly affirmed the importance it attaches to the existence of a vibrant and free civil society and to regular and transparent engagement with civil society. we are monitoring developments at national level and will not hesitate to act when measures are taken in violation of member states' obligations under the treaties and the charter of fundamental rights just as we did in hungary's case in december 2017, when we initiated proceedings in the court of justice concerning the hungarian law on foreign-funded ngos. we are also considering how to further support and empower civil society actors to help them perform their role and build resilience, encompassing that subject within the broader discussion on the next multiannual financial framework. third and finally, the report very much underlines the need to take action against racism, intolerance, disinformation and toxic narratives, which are shrinking the space for well-informed democratic discussions in the public sphere and polarizing our societies. we couldn't agree more. the commission is leading the work on countering the increasingly worrying phenomenon of illegal online hate speech. just this morning we have adopted a recommendation on measures to effectively tackle illegal content online. in this proposal we strike the right balance between the need to take effective measures against illegal content and ensuring that we can address the special challenges of terrorist content without stifling innovation or the exploitation of business opportunities offered in the online world. the recommendation recognises that the need for measures may vary depending on the types of illegal content and combines the need for effective notice and action procedures with strong safeguards on over-removal and caution in areas where contextualisation is needed to determine the illegality of content when automated means of detection are used without human intervention. the particular needs and urgencies relate to tackling terrorist content. the recommendation sends a strong signal to the industry on our expectations and keeps up pressure through the possibility of regulatory action. we are continuing to work intensively with member states and civil society to step up the implementation of eu law on the ground, combat racism and xenophobia and ensure that prompt investigation, prosecution and proportionate punishment become a reality in member states. the fight against intolerance starts with civic education and a deeper understanding by public authorities and institutions of the reasons behind this engagement of citizens with values. this year we are particularly supporting initiatives to re-engage citizens in the eu democratic process, not least in anticipation of the 2019 european elections, and we need to work together on this endeavour.
looks like the czechs have the majority in the conference room for once.
i am personally concerned that this report misses a crucial point. it is of course good that we are dealing with minority issues, but we must not overlook the problems of the vast majority of europeans. we all know that most eu citizens are constantly worried about their family's financial stability, or are afraid of, struggle with, and are in debt. young couples don't know if they can afford to have a family of their own, young families with children don't know if they will ever be able to afford to live in their own homes, workers worry about their pensions, and pensioners are worried. these are the issues that we should be addressing and they are about basic human rights. in conclusion, i think it is necessary and allow me to mention the sad news from slovakia. the deaths of jan kuciak and martina kušnírová must be investigated as soon as possible so that we know the truth about this cowardly and heinous act. my condolences to the families of the victims.
