on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, last december for the first time the eu agreed on a list of tax havens. this is to be welcomed, and this is also thanks to the strong pressure from the european parliament for this list. since then, it has taken less than three months to clear almost half of the countries from the list. if such progress continues we will have an empty list by may and therefore all the work done. we will have eliminated all tax havens in the world. well done. i do not know why the member states were so afraid of such a magic list! but seriously, it does not look credible at all. we should be given detailed information about the criteria used to delist those countries and what they have committed to. we have heard that the council is seeking permission to apply transparency. we shall see what and when will be available. tomorrow, the ep will vote on the mandate for a new committee on financial crime, tax avoidance and tax evasion. this committee will carefully assess the methodology, the country-screening process and the impact of the eu list of non-cooperative jurisdictions for tax purposes, as well as the removal of countries from the list and measures adopted towards countries still listed. hopefully, the explanations member states give us will be more detailed and credible then the current ones.
madam president, and of course congratulations to the rapporteur and shadows, indeed the result is not bad at all. i specially welcome paragraph 33 calling for a ban of some systemic plant production products, including neonicotinoids. efsa has published an updated risk assessment today of three neonicotinoids – clothianidin, imidacloprid and thiamethoxam – that are currently subject to restrictions in the eu because they pose a threat to bees. the authority highlights that most studies show that neonicotinoids have a negative impact on bee health, from damaging their orientation capacity to impairing their reproductive ability. given the scientific evidence that clearly proves those substances are harmful to pollinators, i expect the member states to vote for a complete ban of neonicotinoids during the upcoming meeting of the paff committee on 24 march this year.
i would like to talk today about the controversy that may arise in the context of the election of the president of the european commission. the president of the european commission should be the main driver and connector, and this is what president tusk, juncker and czech prime minister babiš have said in recent days. the ep is pushing for the concept of top candidates, who should be those relevant to the nomination. for me, sometimes the ep pushes this issue in an unforgiving way. the european council shall assert its right of nomination, which is also in line with the text of the treaties in force. in my view, the treaties in force should be respected and a sane european council should propose a candidate who, together with the commission, can win the confidence of the ep. the role of the member states should be more crucial. the president of the european commission should be elected by the member states. i think that rather than go through another dispute, it would be better to respect our primary law.
i supported this proposal, although i must say that i too find it problematic to prescribe rules which are intended to have a uniform impact on a very limited market. it is indeed often a burden for small producers, for the local regional economy, for local producers and farmers. on the other hand, the purpose of the scheme is to prevent misleading practices in general, to ensure consumer protection and to prevent unfair competition. these are the values which i measured in my vote and in the end i decided to support this report, and therefore the draft legislation. it is very important to me that the rules on spirit drinks are applied uniformly and that this is accompanied by procedural rules on geographical indications. we have hops in the czech republic, for example, and we want to protect this raw material as well.
it is important to me that there is an exchange of information to prevent tax fraud. tax fraud is often carried out by white-collar criminals, very smart people who exploit every loophole, shift their taxable profits to tax havens, so the exchange of information is very important in this cross-border exchange. it is also important to preserve the transparency issue. however, i would certainly not be in favour of the unification of direct taxes, so as far as this report, this directive, is concerned, i could have supported it, because i support the prevention of tax evasion in different member states, but if the member states were to unify direct taxes, i would certainly be against it. in that sense, i could therefore support this proposal for a directive.
i voted in favour of this motion for a resolution. in my opinion, the rule of law, democracy, is not negotiable. it has clear rules democratic rule of law. i remember very well when we were discussing, many months ago, the situation in poland, in which the polish prime minister, who is no longer in office, was asked whether the polish government would respect the findings of the venice commission of the council of europe on the constitutionality of the proposed changes in the context of the dispute over the constitutional tribunal. the prime minister avoided a direct answer as to whether she would respect the opinion of the expert opinion of another organisation, the council of europe, not the eu. the conviction was absolutely devastating after the venice commission issued it, so i'm convinced that we should have common values, that it's crucial at least in those fundamentals. the rule of law is not a negotiation, and therefore i had no choice but to support this motion for a resolution.
i didn't vote for this report after all. however, i would like to thank my colleague, frank engel, for trying to keep the report on the ground and focusing on the essential issues of combating discrimination, intolerance, racism and violence of all kinds, and of course also putting the emphasis on protecting children online. the problem, however, is that the parts that propose greater support for sexual and reproductive rights are related to fundamental human rights. i also find problematic the proposals of the left that have finally been announced, which call for the general recognition of gay marriage. i have to say that i voted for the deletion of these parts, but we were in the minority and therefore i could not vote for this report in the end because i believe that these matters should be decided by the member states.
i would like to explain why i have tabled this report on the future of european beekeeping. for several reasons, bees and plant pollination in particular, are crucial for sustainable agricultural production. despite the fact that beekeeping products are in high demand in the eu. i believe that we must also focus on those problems which we can influence by appropriate measures at european level. this is mainly to identify cases of counterfeit honey, which cause great damage to european beekeepers and pose serious health risks to consumers. at the same time, the quantity of honey imported into the eu from china and third countries should be eliminated. in this context, i fully support the proposal to abolish the misleading labelling of eu and non-eu honey mixtures and replace it with a clear indication of the specific country of origin.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, my group has tabled a set of questions. unfortunately, we haven’t received sufficient responses to them from the commissioner and i hope that he will still go back to those four questions. you have argued that everything was in compliance with the rules: rules are one thing, the other is the impression. let me add a few more questions to what we originally tabled. i have no special information, everything i have comes from open sources, from the media. is it true that on 19 february, when heads of cabinet met to prepare a college meeting, no mention of the appointment was on the agenda or under any other business, while on 21 february it was there? and that the whole procedure for the appointment of the secretary-general took just nine minutes, where mr selmayr was first appointed deputy secretary-general, then the secretary-general of the european commission resigned, and then mr selmayr was appointed as the secretary-general? my second question: was he the only candidate, or were there more candidates? my third question, again from the media: is it true that the minutes of the 21 february commission meeting were amended to include a discussion about the merits of non-existent multiple candidates? mr commissioner, there is a lot of interest surrounding that appointment and i think that you should do your best to come out with something which is trustworthy, and you should avoid any feeling or any impression that it was a pre-prepared, politically motivated nomination. unfortunately, i don’t think that in this case you did your job perfectly.
ladies and gentlemen, trade agreements should lead to the achievement of the objectives of the european union and, of course, to respect for the values of the european union. the pursuit of economic interests must not come at the cost of abandoning the european values to which the european union subscribes. one of those values is the protection of human rights and the dignity of human life. the sad reality is that women are weaker than men in certain sectors and trade agreements could have a negative impact in these cases. i therefore consider it appropriate that the commission also assess the impact on the status of women in the context of the impact assessment. i welcome the appointment of a gender liaison officer within the directorate-general for trade and would also welcome greater involvement of women in the negotiation processes. i believe it is right that some issues, such as sexual and reproductive health, should remain a matter for member states and i reject any speculation about the impact of the european union's trade policy on women's sexual and reproductive health.
i am committed to preventing and combating violence against women and i know that there are countries that have difficulty protecting the dignity of women's lives, where violence against women is linked to historical and cultural traditions. it is for these countries that i see the istanbul convention as a very important instrument for preventing and combating violence. but i do not agree that this convention should be a stick to those countries that have decided to fight violence in a different way. for example, the czech republic has not yet ratified the istanbul convention, but it has had a law against domestic violence in place for 12 years. it was even initiated by my former party colleague, the deputy speaker of the house of representatives, jan kasal, in collaboration with the white circle of security. our legal system contains the crimes as defined by the istanbul convention. prevention, detection and assistance to victims of violence are all part of the work of governmental and non-governmental organisations. more important than ratifying the convention, in my opinion, is enforcing zero tolerance for violence in practice. we should be concerned with substance, not with the formal fulfilment of the ratification obligation. each eu country has committed itself to the eu charter of fundamental rights, which obliges member states to protect the human dignity and safety of all citizens.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the e-commerce of the 21st century is a beautiful combination of the free movement of goods and the freedom to provide services. this creates enormous opportunities, and it is important that it does so throughout the european union. we wanted to make sure that these opportunities are not just for large companies, large operators and large—scale platforms, but also for small retailers and small craft shops that can then rely on reliable and affordable services. so we have taken an important step in this direction, even though – if it were up to me – we would have gone even further. i am glad that the proposals that i made in terms of the scope – and that is single piece parcel services’ tariffs – has been taken on board, because that will in fact enable us to compare like with like. every single operator, regardless of size, will have to make his or her tariffs public, and they can then be independently reviewed, if need be, by national regulators. if anyone expected that parcels would be cheaper tomorrow, this is bad news, but we have made significant progress towards greater transparency, more competition and more clarity for consumers in the future. we have fallen short – and i am happy to say so – of price regulation, which was also in the air. i would like to thank the rapporteur, my colleagues and the shadow rapporteurs. i think that we should clearly vote ‘yes’ in favour in the coming hours.
mr president, if someone wants to send a parcel to another member state today, he may be unpleasantly surprised, either by the price he pays for the service or by the way his parcel is delivered. unfortunately, information on prices and delivery conditions is often unclear and incomplete. the european parliament's negotiators, led by the socialist rapporteur lucy anderson, made improvements during the trilogue with the council, for example on transparency of tariffs for consumers and businesses, or on the power of regulators to assess price inadequacy. i also consider it a success that the new rules will apply to all the major players in the parcel delivery industry, including online shopping. it is also important to consider the working conditions of the people who work in this sector. price competition cannot be to their detriment.
ladies and gentlemen, the systematic development of an intelligent transport system that enables the safe, smooth, economically balanced and environmentally friendly movement of people and goods is a major challenge for society today. the european union is striving to lead by example in this regard, especially when it comes to meeting international climate commitments and the internal objectives of the european union. i am very pleased that europe's galileo and egnos global satellite navigation systems, which have now reached such a high level of technological capacity, can help. in addition, we must not forget that 92% of road accidents are caused by human error and the use of cits technologies is important for the effective functioning of some driver assistance systems. the european strategy on interoperable intelligent transport systems has enormous potential, but privacy and data protection must be at the forefront and must be treated with the utmost care.
madam president, today marks the 19th anniversary of the eastward expansion of nato. on 12 march 1999, poland, hungary and the czech republic thus joined nato. for the first time in their independent history, these states had real security guarantees and could at the same time contribute to the collective security of a democratic world. but i am concerned to see nato politically weakened on both sides of the atlantic. i mean, in a europe that has had 70 years of peace, not only thanks to the european union, but also thanks to nato. nato is a stabilizer for the balkans, for the mediterranean, including the handling of the migration crisis, but also for relations between turkey and greece, for example, which could otherwise escalate into open conflict. in order to continue to fulfil its role, nato requires all its member states to fulfil their obligations, in particular to spend sufficient resources on defence. the european union and nato must cooperate as much as possible to strengthen security guarantees for europe and the world.
madam president, we have now reached the crucial point in negotiations. we all need to know, and especially businesses, under which terms we will be able to cooperate in the future. what is the best scenario? to keep the uk within the internal market to preserve most of the benefits that we have created with the uk together. the fact that the uk refuses that is a fatal mistake. any other option is simply worse and will build new barriers for eu companies, but also for british companies, and for some businesses, especially smes, this new situation will be very hard to cope with. the worst possible outcome is having absolutely no deal. it would not allow for any transitional period and it would just worsen our mutual relations. it would degrade the eu-uk partnership to a level inferior to what we currently have with turkey, or even with china, and i do not think either side wishes that.
madam president, i apologise for my naivety, but i must say i really expected this to be a political debate. i am sorry, ms panayotova, but with all due respect and sympathy to you, we have received a list of what is to be on the agenda. i would expect at least an attempt from the side of the president of the commission to say: these are the priorities, this is how we analyse the situation, the bottlenecks, this is what the ambition is, this is what the president of the european council and the presidency expects. of course, i know that you cannot go into detail, but not to give us any hint on any of the political issues is, i think, really a failure. no wonder, in fact, that the european council is at a standstill. we are not delivering. we have got issues such as the reform of the eu, greater efficiency, real issues of security, trade, other political sensitivities that we need to address. we are not delivering. this is something that we would like to address concretely in this house with the council. i do apologise, but this system, is in my opinion, unacceptable for the future. guy verhofstadt referred to the russian attack in the uk: i think it is an attack, and i would expect that the council would hint that this is to be on the agenda and will be debated, with concrete measures against the russians and concrete concessions vis-à-vis the citizens, the entrepreneurs, the scientists, etc. this is the kind of issue we want to debate and the way we want to debate it.
madam president, i am pleased to see that economic subjects form a big part of the council meeting agenda. after all, economic performance plays a very important role in the way that people perceive the functionality of the european union. i am especially pleased to see that tax issues are an informal part of the council meeting. i hope that not just digital taxation but also vat reform and ccctb will be discussed and we will get closer to their adoption. i call on the leaders of the member states to proceed and, if necessary, go to enhanced cooperation in the case of ccctb. secondly, i want to mention the energy union. after a very good commission proposal, short—term national interests, as well as the interests of some groups, could now weaken the result and this would be unfortunate. it would be a lost opportunity for europe and for our citizens. last but not least, i also want to mention the issue of trade, but instead of going into details, i will just declare my support for what president juncker said about this subject. i hope that we will achieve our goal, which is to support deferred and open trade globally.
madam president, i am convinced that the challenges facing the european union are indeed many. there are countless reasons, such as brexit, asylum and migration issues, the multiannual financial framework and the role of cohesion policy. the pressure on the budget is enormous and in this spirit the european council must be called upon to be truly united. the point of unanimity, i think, is key. on the other hand, those who call for unanimity must have a frank dialogue. i think it is important to stress this to the heads of government and it is important to the presidency. i would also like to raise the question of cyprus, because i have been to this island recently and i was quite shocked by the way the city is divided, the way the island is divided. it is a bit of a shame for europe in the 21st century that cyprus remains a divided island in this way, and i think that the european presidency and the european council should really focus on this.
ladies and gentlemen, the inclusion of social issues in the european council's deliberations is to be welcomed. social and labour standards are an integral dimension of the single market and therefore need to be discussed in an honest way. to discuss honestly means to discuss with all those who may be affected by the impactful measures. after brexit, 85 percent of the eu's gdp will be made up of eurozone countries. does this mean that it is no longer necessary to know the opinion of other countries and that parallel institutions and summits are being set up to decide for all? is the convening of a summit of the european council for only 19 countries a signal that this will be the way forward? such an approach calls into question the principle of unity that we so often like to talk about and is a step backwards.
mr president, i too supported this draft regulation and i would like to acknowledge the role of the estonian presidency in its implementation. it is really important to me that the barriers which actually prevent the rules of the single internal market from being applied should be removed. so improving the rules for cross-border parcel delivery is really important, and although i am not in favour of using the hardest form of legislation, that is the form of regulation, i think it is quite appropriate here. there is no doubt that this measure, i.e. the regulation, will improve efficiency and transparency in parcel delivery, improve the situation of consumers. i would like to draw attention to the situation of small businesses in particular, which make great use of this service, and to the often unfair practices of delivery companies, which complicate their business. so i very much welcome this amendment, and that is why i supported it.
mr president, if we emphasized the role of the single market in the last report on improving the conditions for crossborder delivery of parcels, then road transport is also crucial for this measure, that is to say for the possibilities of better delivery. it is essential for the functioning of the internal market in general. in order to improve the efficiency and safety of road transport, it appears necessary to increase the safety and qualification requirements of drivers accordingly. unfortunately, the situation on the labour market in many member states of the european union, particularly in the field of road haulage, is very tense, there is a shortage of drivers, and if we undermine the demands we have on drivers in terms of reducing those demands, for example, unequal, unequal conditions across the member states, then i think it will also affect our safety. that is why i welcomed and supported this report, because it introduces appropriate, comparable rules and a level playing field in road safety.
mr president, i have finally given this report my due consideration and i therefore give this explanation of my decision. i'm a big supporter of free trade. i am convinced that the free trade agreements which the european union concludes with third countries, such as south korea, objectively bring great economic benefits. i also see the issue of gender and gender equality and the protection of women as very important. on the other hand, i think this report is a bit of a mix between apples and pears. in short, the report gives me a little bit of a sense of not all of us being in favour of free trade and not all of our colleagues being in favour of these international agreements. that's why i've been holding off, because i think the two issues are too serious and should be dealt with separately and not together like this.
mr president, i supported this report because i think it is right that we should tackle the problems of the poorest regions in the framework of european solidarity. the report covers 47 regions in eight countries and it is very good, and i welcome this in the report, that in addition to financial support and assistance, which is the basis of cohesion policy, the report also deals with other aspects and problems of these poorer regions. there is the question of labour law, there is the question of how far individual governments in the member states should tackle reforms that could lead to greater economic growth in the regions concerned, because any financial support under cohesion policy is welcome and, in my view, right, but it will not solve poverty in certain areas in the long term. without economic growth in these areas, aid will be only aid.

member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the commission takes note with interest and pleasure of the two drafts of the reports under discussion today. i see in both reports pioneering reflections, structures, expectations and conditions of parliament which, if adopted by you, will certainly be incorporated in greater depth in the final work of the european commission towards may this year. i would like to thank the four rapporteurs - mr olbrycht, mrs thomas, mr deprez and mr lewandowski - for their excellent work with their staff and for the good cooperation they have had with my services and with me. we have shown that we are working together across party lines, and that will certainly facilitate the debate on the budgetary framework from may onwards. it is about the future of europe, as mr arthuis always emphasizes, and what can be done to shape the future with budgetary resources, with financial programmes, with the multiannual financial framework. firstly, the own resources, the new own resources - an ancient story, if you read it carefully: they have been unsuccessful since the 1950s. but the national budget, which once accounted for 10% of total revenue, now accounts for 70%. it is therefore time to diversify our income. i am talking about diversification, not automatically increasing. i would like to see a slight reduction in the contribution from national budgets and the addition of new own resources as a further pillar. the commission has already made a number of proposals in its draft report, which are largely in line with the work of the high level group on mario monti. i would point out that this group is not a commission group, not a parliament group, not a mario monti group, it is a group of all three institutions, and there were also three representatives of the council. and unanimous approval of the proposals at the end should mean that there can be no zero-sum game. we shall be discussing our proposals in the coming weeks. but we will certainly define two, three, four proposals on the revenue side, and i think that would make the work for the next financial framework much easier. the subject of discounts was mentioned. we are thinking in unison here, too. a substantial part of the rebates are rebates from the uk rebate to reduce the reduction in uk payments to other net payers. so when the british rebate falls, that part of the rebate automatically falls. we now have further corrective measures and discounts, and i would like to say as a starting point, as our commission proposal, that we will not propose discounts. and let me be frank: i am not ruling out the possibility that, in the course of the negotiations, there may be a need for a few, unbureaucratic, moderate discounts if unanimity is to be achieved in the negotiations. so much for the own resources report. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we've been working together a lot. i have been on your committee on budgets several times, i have been on many groups, i am going to other groups, i have been on special committees. i am not surprised that your report largely coincides with the draft we are now refining and that some of your proposals will be incorporated into our proposal. in our communication of february, we tried to explain to the european council, the council, but also to parliament and all those involved - the committee of the regions, the economic and social committee - what was at stake. it's like a menu. now you have to decide, now the council has to decide what you're ordering. jean-claude juncker, my colleagues and i we are cooks and waiters, we are waiting for your order. if you want a full menu, which includes border protection and social policy, which includes research and erasmus, if you want to double, triple erasmus, expand horizon, we will deliver. and we bill and expect to be paid. it's like a restaurant. now we actually have a problem that we should avoid as much as possible. i would like to avoid a conflict in this house and also in the council and in the public, a conflict which could be cohesion versus migration or agriculture versus defence. the european community is not a mere institution, but a political one. we have traditional programs, but tradition doesn't mean they belong in the house of history. traditional programmes can be sustainable without change. i still think that cohesion policy is viable. why would you do that? look at gdp per capita in the member states and regions. the average gdp per capita in europe is 28 000 euros, in my own country it is 40 000 euros, in luxembourg it is 100 000 euros, in bulgaria it is 6800 euros. this is not what will keep europe together in the long run, this is not what is reasonable in the long run. we must reduce the gap in gdp per head, not by reducing luxembourg to 30,000, but by increasing investment in bulgaria, romania, the new member states and the regions of southern europe, increasing their competitiveness and thus increasing gdp per head. that is what i call a union of solidarity. this is what modern cohesion policy must do. i do not know whether cohesion policy will still be necessary in 2050, but i do know that it will remain necessary over the next decade for bringing less developed regions closer to the average and for increasing the competitiveness of all regions, as well as for social measures and for smart environmental and infrastructure measures. that is why a reduction in cohesion would be completely wrong. the same applies to agricultural policy. in our proposal we will simplify agricultural policy, we will improve greening, we will reconcile the agricultural sector and the environment even more closely, we will give the farmer money so that he can produce good food in sufficient quantity and of high quality, in compliance with our environmental and animal welfare regulations and with the regulations on soil, water and climate protection. that is another reason why the agricultural policy is still viable. now we have very different agricultural structures. we have the small farmer who claims 15 hectares and we have the large company which farms 5,000 hectares. we've got to get this spagat right. that is why the willingness to compromise of all parties involved is part of it. we have two gaps, the brexit gap and the spending gap created by new tasks. our proposal: we will close the brexit gap with 50% redundancies and cuts and 50% additional revenue. but if we are going to tackle new tasks, if we believe, in the interests of our citizens and our member states, that europe can act and finance more efficiently in defence research, border protection, border control, migration and refugee aid, then we need the money of the member states to do this. 80% fresh money and 20% through internal re-allocations is our proposal for this. the second point is the ceiling: you propose 1.3%. i think this is ambitious, but very practical. 1.3 is not beyond good and evil, quite the opposite. the parliament is proposing a very, very well-founded premium. our formula is 1.1x, because we still have some uncertainties. the inclusion of the development fund is an uncertainty, the question of deepening economic and monetary union and what money should be invested in structural support, in the readiness for reform and in the preparation for the introduction of the euro. the second uncertainty is that we are still waiting for some advice from the council, otherwise we will translate our proposal into concrete figures after easter. and then we're going to solve for the x of 1.1x into a concrete number. the commission has already made a number of proposals. she's becoming increasingly empty. at a time when the cap and cohesion accounted for well over 80% of expenditure, the return on funds could still be calculated with relative accuracy. in the next mff, however, the cap and cohesion will fall to about 60-61% of all expenditure. and soon 40 percent will be erasmus, refugee aid, research, where there is no return in that sense. if we invest in education, security, clothing, food, shelter for refugees in turkey and their human dignity, nothing comes back. but the refugees are spared the difficult journey across the mediterranean to us, and the costs are not incurred here, but much more efficiently locally. we should therefore convince the member states and the media that the net contributor approach is long outdated and no longer suits the new tasks and programmes. thank you for your preliminary work. we are happy to hear further oral expectations from you, evaluate them, incorporate them into our proposal and then come back with the commission proposal on 2 may and at the end of may with all the supplementary implementing rules. we are grateful that you have made the mff one of your priorities in parliament for 2018, that you appoint your rapporteurs early, that you have an intensive session schedule until february-march next year. because if we fail to adopt key decisions or the whole package before easter next year, i predict that nothing will happen in 2019 and then 2020 will be decided by the end of the mff. and then 2021 would be another completely lost year for farmers, for researchers, for regions, for mayors, for the economic development of europe. this one should be avoided. then we would have learned from the experience of the past. this is what we did wrong in 2013. and one more point: if our british friends leave us and the 27 member states and the members of the various political groups are able to reach agreement on this contentious issue, they will be sending a very good signal that europe is alive and that it is a europe of good governance.

in writing. the european multiannual financial framework has, almost from the outset, demonstrated several shortcomings. the high level of outstanding commitments already in 2014 is starting to end with a lack of funds for new priorities, which the commission and the council have decided to address in an unsystematic way by ad hoc shifting of envelopes from successful programmes. i welcome the report on the future mff: preparing parliament's position on the mff post-2020, because i believe it is necessary to avoid these mistakes in the future. europeans need a strong union that can respond to the major challenges. a union of sustainable economic growth, quality jobs and economic, social and territorial cohesion. representatives of member states may no longer block eu contributions to measures to combat climate change. these are still well below the 20% level to which the member states themselves have committed themselves. the life programme, which is a highly successful programme but suffers from chronic underfunding compared to other european programmes, needs to be increased. i am very pleased that parliament has managed to establish a strong position. i am also personally very pleased that the european parliament has openly recognised that the financing of the new agricultural policy must go hand in hand with the introduction of practices which are more environmentally friendly and more health-friendly.

mr. chairman, today we're discussing president donald trump's new activity on tariffs on steel and aluminum. i think this decision is an unfortunate, bad decision for international trade. it is provoking absolutely unnecessary emotions towards economic cooperation between the united states of america and the european union. of course, there were immediate proposals for retaliation. every trade war is dangerous and only brings losses to both sides. it is therefore important to seek a diplomatic solution immediately, before a domino effect is triggered. it is clear that the global steel overproduction can only be solved by agreement of all the parties concerned. i believe it is unnecessary to call on the commission's top officials here for urgent trade diplomacy, and i thank the commissioner for the information today. the impact on the steel industry can be dramatic, especially after very strong co2 regulations and coal cuts. the steel industry is already on the verge of collapse, and few people realize the importance of steel in transport and construction. if we fail to resolve this problem of customs protection, i am afraid not only of possible similar actions in other areas but also of serious damage to the european steel industry.
mr prime minister, ladies and gentlemen, we are living through a period of great instability. everything changes, we don't know what's coming in a few years. we don't know what europe will look like, and in this uncertainty, propaganda of all kinds is being spread. on the one hand, there are the fundamentalists of pro-european, euro-friendly politics, who say that the european union must remain forever, that the european union is the only institution in human history that will last for ever and ever. it's irresponsible policy, because the situation is very uncertain and we really can't predict what's going to happen in a few years. similarly, propaganda is being spread by other politicians who say that the european union must be left immediately, preferably tomorrow, whatever the cost, even if it means collapse and disaster. that's also irresponsible politics. both are fundamentalism of the same kind. perhaps there are also people here who are profiting from the fact that we are staying in the european union as long as the subsidies are coming in. all these approaches are wrong, because it is the duty of politicians to assess the current situation correctly and to make decisions accordingly every day. we can't just go on a fundamentalist notion that we know what's right. and it's especially important in times of politics, because there's no one answer that's going to make us reassess the situation. the european commission has indicated that the lisbon treaty is, in principle, ending. it presented a white paper on the future of europe and put forward a number of scenarios for how the project could go forward. it calls for a discussion of these alternatives and it is clear that the probability of keeping the lisbon treaty is very limited. this uncertainty is exacerbated by the uncertainty about russia, the uncertainty about china, the situation in north africa, the middle east, migration, the weakening of nato, which is losing its weight and authority as a result of the policies of the european union, and this threatens us all. the murder of journalists, the murder of former spies, and the call for democracy in this uncertain situation are all contributing to this. the citizens of the european states feel that decisions are being made about us without us, that they have absolutely no influence on what happens in brussels or here. the voices are growing that call for a real alternative, replacing the lisbon treaty with some kind of democratic institution. it is not a question of creating conflict, we all want cooperation between european countries, but based on completely different principles than we see here today. we're really looking for another way. mr. prime minister, we described one such alternative and you got it through your embassy in prague. we would very much like you to read it and we would like to start discussing real alternatives, not just how to keep power in brussels. all the good that the european union does can be done without these institutions. and that's the alternative we should be talking about.
mr president, we have voted here several times on the adjustment of relations between the european union and the united kingdom following its withdrawal from the european union, and i have the feeling that in each of these votes there will be a number of subtle demands in each report, which we all know will be difficult for the other side, the united kingdom, to accept. i wonder why that is. i do not want to be a stickler, but i am beginning to get the impression that the european parliament is using this as part of a negotiating tactic or that it wants to escalate the situation before any possible final vote or that, in the worst-case scenario, it does not even want to reach an agreement. i would very much like to see a degree of mutual sensitivity here and to see that each of these reports is designed and constructed in such a balanced way that such suspicions cannot arise.
mr. chairman, the current negotiations with the uk are based on the idea that the uk will pay for brexit. but if we look at the trade statistics, it turns out that the continent is paying far more for brexit than the uk. our businesses on the continent need a clear and predictable business environment. we urgently need information on how free trade is going to play out. we urgently need assurances that the free trade agreement with the uk will be concluded. the european commission is gambling with our destinies by negotiating and deepening the negative consequences of brexit for all.
mr president, i voted in favour of the european parliament's position on the post-2020 financial framework. it is clear from the amendments we have voted on for a long time that the negotiations on the common position must have been very complex. finally, the proposal also includes a proposal to extend the planning period from seven years to five plus five, i.e. to ten years. that's the part that i personally don't agree with, because i think the seven-year period is optimal, we have good experience with it. on the other hand, i very much appreciate the fact that the european parliament has supported the increase of the erasmus+ programme, which is crucial for young people, has supported the increase of security expenditure and has also supported the ticket for young people aged 18 and over, which is such a pilot programme for young people in the european union. so i appreciate the work of my colleagues on this report, which i was able to support.
mr president, i am aware that we are at a crossroads, that we have many challenges ahead of us. that is why i have supported financial stability, looking to the future, and at the same time i support cohesion policy in the long term, because it is a policy that helps our cohesion and should be the true reflection of european policy as such. it was essential for me that the cohesion policy role as such was preserved in this report, that erasmus was strengthened, which is a very good programme for young people, and that we continue to be able to respond to challenges such as brexit and to new challenges that are reflected in the budget. i consider the report to be balanced overall, and that is why i supported it.
mr president, like my colleagues, i supported the report on own resources reform, also in view of the fact that we have to deal with the consequences of brexit. another reason is a certain lack of transparency in the system of financing the budget. we agree that the revenue side of the budget should be simplified and made more transparent, which is certainly the case. i did not agree, and therefore did not vote, to the introduction of certain proposals for an environmental tax or a corporate tax share. these issues are not sufficiently discussed and need to be considered much more carefully. on the other hand, i accepted the challenge to the european commission to present the relevant vat legislation and a proposal for the reform of the vat-based own resource in the forthcoming legislative package.
mr president, i voted in favour of the european parliament's report on the implementation of the european semester. this report puts more emphasis on employment and social criteria. i consider the annual growth analysis to be a very important tool, providing member states with important feedback on which areas to focus on and which reforms to implement. more flexibility in terms of working time and not abandoning even typical forms of employment are needed if we are to really help to increase employment. these forms also allow employees to coordinate their work duties with others. however, these workers also deserve adequate social protection. therefore, one of the greatest challenges for reforms will always be to strike a balance between flexibility and social protection. however, the most important tool for growth remains education and, of course, in digital technologies, where there is a lack of education among the workforce.
the next item is the topical debate (rule 153a) on international cooperation in the fight against cybercrime – the threat to public services (2018/2627(rsp)).
for the group gue/ngl mr. chairman, terms like hacker and cybernetics are used on a daily basis these days. states have been accusing each other of hacking into various internet servers or interfering in elections. some experts speak of a so-called cyber cold war. the fact that cyberattacks are increasing and will most likely continue to do so is a reality. the question is whether they can be effectively prepared for, or even prevented. the international exchange of experience in this field is absolutely essential. but cybersecurity is a complex issue. the internet is a living organism that will find its way, and i'm afraid it will always be one step ahead of us politicians. the rules need to be set in a balanced way so as not to infringe on citizens' fundamental rights, such as freedom of expression. but we cannot allow the eradication of internet freedom and the deliberate spying on citizens. regulation is not the same as censorship. we have a huge task ahead of us, and the end result must be to increase the security of our citizens.
mr president, digital systems are penetrating every area of life, including public services. we become addicted to them. the border between critical and non-critical infrastructure is becoming increasingly blurred, with fully autonomous systems and 5g networks soon to become a reality. protection against cybercrime is therefore a key challenge and must be based on robust cooperation at european and international level. the member states of the european union have their own professional systems and procedures. but we need to deepen coordination and exchange of information within the european ecosystem. we will discuss the role of enisa, there is also the dimension of cooperation between police and judicial authorities, including europol and eurojust. but you can't create a 'strong europe', cybercrime doesn't respect borders. and that makes international cooperation with our euro-atlantic allies all the more important, but also with countries like israel or south korea.
member of the commission. – mr president, thank you for this debate, which i think has been very timely and has underlined again the importance of these issues. last year we saw major attacks with wannacry and notpetya, but we also saw politically-motivated attacks. i think together, that has raised the awareness of the need to tackle these different cyber threats. i think we are responding. i am very grateful for the support that i hear and indeed the encouragement to keep working on these issues. from the commission side we are committed to develop a cybersecurity agency with real competences and capacities and to develop a certification system that really delivers increased cybersecurity. we will work with the member states to that end. we will work to promote a really effective public—private partnership. we will, as encouraged by you all, work to build the resilience of our infrastructures, our platforms, services and devices, but also to address the behavioural challenge: to build our behavioural and societal resilience too against some of these threats, because, as has been underlined by many of the speakers, in order to benefit to the full from the digital single market and all the social advantages we get from an ever-more interconnected society, we need to deal with the threats and the challenges that seek to undermine it.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, i listened to your debates with great interest, and if we had to summarise the main aspects of the issue, i have to say that on the one hand, cyber security questions are clearly linked to the development of the digital single market, providing opportunities for citizens and european industry to make the most of the digital economy. on the other hand we are facing ever-increasing cyber security challenges, and one of them is the cyberattack wannacry mentioned by many of you. these challenges ahead of us can be faced, and the ambition of making europe the leader in cybersecurity by 2025 can be achieved, through multidimensional cooperation, a comprehensive approach and strengthening the legal framework. in order to increase the preparedness at national and eu level, the key word to overcome those challenges is cooperation and the multidimensional aspects: public-private cooperation, cooperation between member states, economic cross-border and cross-sector collaboration, cooperation between the relevant services of the member states, the eu institutions, the new agencies as well as third countries and organisations. as the presidency, we are determined to advance the legislative work as much as possible. we consider the cyber security act as a priority and hope to reach a general approach before the end of our term so that the trilogues can start under the austrian presidency. the aim is to give a stronger and permanent mandate to the european union agency for network and information security (enisa), the new eu cyber security agency, and to avoid fragmentation of the cybersecurity market of services, goods and processes by establishing a framework for certification schemes at eu level. discussions are still ongoing, notably the task and mandate of the enisa as well as on certification. let me assure you of the council’s firm commitment to continuing the work that i have outlined and our readiness to adopt and effectively implement concrete measures in the field in order to ensure an open, free, safe and secure cyberspace while protecting with strong determination the fundamental rights and freedoms of our citizens.
the debate is closed. written statements (rule 162)
the next item is the debate on the council and commission statements on protection of investigative journalists in europe: the case of slovak journalist ján kuciak and martina kušnírová (2018/2628(rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, today’s debate has been triggered by the appalling assassination of ján kuciak and his partner marina kušnírová. first of all, i would like to offer my sincere condolences to their families, friends and colleagues. the council strongly condemns this crime and expects the perpetrators to be brought to justice. mr kuciak was a journalist who was not afraid to investigate serious crime. he wanted to shed light on what needs to be eradicated in our societies and devoted his life to fighting corruption and the abuse of power. mr kuciak’s courageous behaviour deserves our respect and recognition. the work of journalists like him is a truly vital part of any healthy democracy. media freedom is an indispensable pillar of any democratic society. journalists are among the guardians of properly-functioning democracies. where there is no freedom of speech and people fear for their lives every time they express a critical view, there is no democracy. it is for these very reasons that freedom of speech, media freedom and pluralism are guaranteed under the eu charter of fundamental rights. they are among the core democratic values on which the european union is founded. these freedoms are an obligation on our governments, and all democracies must make it an absolute priority to protect journalists from financial or political pressure. let me be clear: in our union, attacks designed to silence media representatives are completely unacceptable. if they happen, we must act immediately: we must condemn the crime, find and punish the perpetrators and re-establish public trust in the ability of our democratic institutions to function. we are all conscious of the responsibility our governments have to protect journalists and defend media freedom. the slovak law enforcement authorities, together with international experts, are investigating the crime. a delegation from the european parliament was in bratislava last week. i look forward to what they will have to say. the council welcomes the timely initial response of the slovak authorities, including their initiative to provide a detailed briefing during the february session of the general affairs council in brussels, on 27 february. it signalled a clear commitment on the part of the slovak government to ensure a quick investigation. the council stands ready to provide any assistance to the slovak authorities in investigating this appalling crime. we owe this to mr kuciak and to all journalists working to make our societies better.
member of the commission. – mr president, we are all still deeply shocked by the assassination of ján kuciak, a 27-year-old investigative reporter, and his partner martina kušnírová two weeks ago in slovakia. we strongly condemn it and express again our deepest condolences to their families and loved ones. last week president tajani made a strong call for a detailed, in-depth international investigation. all of the political groups requested an independent inquiry commission to investigate what happened. as president juncker said, the killing or intimidation of journalists has no place in europe, no place in any democracy. on behalf of president juncker and all my colleagues in the commission, can i say that we fully agree that the top priority for all of us must be to carry out an independent and thorough investigation of the facts and bring those responsible to justice? we call upon the slovak authorities to do this quickly. we are aware that their investigation relies upon the cooperation of europol and the italian authorities. europol is ready to support the investigations with all the instruments and expertise at its disposal. the eu also provides several additional tools to support police and judicial cooperation between slovakia and italy. we encourage the authorities to make full use of this extra support. this cooperation should be put in place as rapidly as possible in order for justice to be done. in order to find out whether this case involved misuse of eu funds and had a cross-border dimension, we invite the authorities to cooperate fully with the european anti-fraud office (olaf). if misuse of funds were proven this case would illustrate again why we need the european public prosecutor. the murders of ján kuciak and martina kušnírová, as well as that of daphne caruana galizia five months ago, show that investigative journalism remains a dangerous profession everywhere in the world, including here in europe. we can’t accept this. as first vice-president timmermans stated, no democracy can survive without the free press, which is why journalists deserve respect and protection. we have to do our utmost to ensure journalists’ safety and to stop such crimes from happening again in any eu member state. media freedom and pluralism are essential pillars of our modern democracy. they are guarantees of an open and free democratic debate. they are fundamental rights, as enshrined in article 11 of the eu charter of fundamental rights. they’re intrinsically linked to the values which we treasure and which we can never take for granted. journalists have the right to investigate, to ask uncomfortable questions and to report effectively. this is what ján kuciak was doing. that, it appears, is why he was murdered. every day we witness the challenges that political, technological and sociological changes pose to pluralism, to the status of facts and investigative journalism throughout the world. in 2016 the commission dedicated its annual colloquium on fundamental rights precisely to these issues – media pluralism and democracy. several members of this house took part in those discussions actively. they explored the multiple links between a free pluralistic media and democracy and agreed to promote and protect that pluralism and freedom of the media in the eu. the assassinations in slovakia show that we need to continue to be vigilant, including in europe, and do more to protect these fundamental rights on a national as well as an eu level. member states have a duty to make sure that press freedom and journalists are protected on their territory, in law but also in spirit. it is up to national governments, in this sad case slovakia, to make sure that their citizens are protected and to bring those guilty of crimes to justice in accordance with their constitutional legal order and with international human rights obligations. under the treaties, the commission has limited powers concerning media freedom. however, we can and do monitor, encourage and guide. for instance, we are acting to make sure that journalists can do their essential work in safety. journalists are the watchdogs of our democratic societies. with their relentless work and investigations they enable a free and independent media to hold those in power to account. in this context, we are completing our work on an initiative that we announced in our work programme last october to strengthen the protection of whistleblowers. whistleblowers who unveil illegal activities are an important source of information for journalists. protecting them from retaliation is, therefore, a vital part of safeguarding investigative journalism. the best guarantee of media freedom and pluralism is to ensure the independence of media regulators. this is why the commission has proposed strengthening the rules for the independence of national audiovisual regulators as part of the revision of the audiovisual media services directive. this includes requirements of independence that relate also to budgetary and structural provisions. but it is not only about laws. we also monitor risks to media pluralism across europe and map violations of media freedom. we support journalists under threat as well as investigative journalism. we have recently begun funding a scheme dedicated cross-border investigative journalism. many of these initiatives, and i salute this, are independent projects financed by this parliament. one particularly useful instrument is the eu-financed media pluralism monitor, which aims to identify and highlight risks to media pluralism. unfortunately, the results show that no member state is immune from risks when it comes to media pluralism. i can assure you that we will continue to monitor the situation in all member states, including in slovakia. as this case demonstrates, both the eu and its member states need to do more to protect investigative journalism across europe. the murder of this young journalist and his partner is an attack on all that europe stands for, our values, our right to freedom of speech, our right to ask difficult questions, our rights justice and our capacity to hold those in power properly to account. this is what distinguishes our democracies and keeps them healthy and vibrant. mr president, if you’ll permit me, two words on another matter. we live in extraordinary times. various forces seem intent on attacking the rule of law, the very values that lie at the heart of our open societies. we have seen recently the first offensive use of nerve agent on european territory since world war ii, something that the council of europe called a ‘dangerous breach of the rule of law’. i want to join first vice-president timmermans in repeating again, on behalf of the commission, our full solidarity with the people of salisbury, indeed the people of britain, as they tackle this attack.
thank you, commissioner for both issues. on the latter issue parliament has taken a very strong position and i can only reiterate and fully support what you have said.
i am sure that i speak on behalf of the whole house when i say we are looking forward to the report and getting acquainted with the findings.
i must say that it is surely not just me who has the highest respect for the slovaks and the message that they sent out in the streets of many slovak towns and cities.
for the group gue/ngl. mr president, death can never be justified, especially when it comes to young people who were only trying to get the truth. the deaths of jan kuciak and martina kušnírová must be investigated as soon as possible so that we know the truth about this cowardly and heinous act. once again, let me extend my heartfelt condolences to the families of the victims. the case we're discussing here today is absolutely reprehensible, but i would like to ask everyone here not to make slovakia an occupied mafia country. we didn't do it in malta, and we shouldn't do it in slovakia. we all believe in democratic control and the indivisibility of power, so let the slovak authorities act. this is really not the time to be collecting cheap political dots. the events in slovakia should not be judged by the streets, the european union or the european parliament, but by an independent slovak court. in this context, i consider it a complete insolence that our colleague manfred weber is instructing prime minister fica on who to fire from the government. i would like to know if the countries of central europe can also dictate who will be in the german government when it is finally formed. i hope so, because otherwise, there's a double standard. please let's have this debate with respect for slovakia and its democratic and independent institutions. slovakia deserves a helping hand if it asks for it, not condemnation based on speculation. madam president, the murder of jan kuciak and martina kušnírová is a terrible thing and the authorities must find and punish the guilty. media, especially print media, are facing economic challenges today in the context of online news, social networks and other changes in the overall environment. especially time-consuming investigative journalism often lacks resources. the role of traditional journalism is irreplaceable in a democracy, especially given the amount of fake news and misinformation we are targeted with in today's world. the revelation of the panama papers also testifies to its importance. political leaders must respect and, where necessary, defend freedom of expression and create conditions for the free work of the media, not to mention the safety of journalists. daphne caruana galizia was murdered in malta, and now there was murder in slovakia. it is absolutely imperative that we do everything we can to prevent this from happening again.
madam president, the murder of a journalist is the ultimate attack on freedom of speech, an attack on democracy. slovak citizens understood this and took to the streets to demonstrate against the power responsible for the murder of jan kuciak. the spark jumped from the east. the ukrainians on the maidan understood that they were ruled by the same people who ruled under communism, that in their country the most ardent communists became the biggest and richest capitalists, the most powerful oligarchs. the slovaks have understood this. the murder of a journalist was the trigger. tens of thousands of slovaks on the streets are beginning to understand that the famous czechoslovak velvet revolution of 1989 is not over. that it needs to be completed by all the democratic means that it has provided us. it seems maidan has become mobile. hungarians and georgians, who have their oligarchs, have also taken to the streets. czechs took to the streets earlier this year to protest against being ruled by a former kgb agent, a state security agent, a former communist party member, now a wealthy oligarch and the country's prime minister and owner of influential media. ladies and gentlemen, central and eastern europe is being overshadowed by popular resistance. the murdered jan kuciak fell in this battle.
