madam president, i would like to express my sorrow and solidarity both to the families of the victims and, of course, to all slovak citizens. we don't really know who is responsible for this heinous act, and we must insist on a proper investigation, i would stress that. however, the mother of one of the victims, for example, has expressed doubts about the proper investigation. i think it is the task of the slovak authorities to dispel these doubts. i think that in the european union, journalists should not be killed, it's a great shame for our community. i would like to recall the vocabulary of prime minister fico over the past decades. he actually remains, in my opinion, in this dictionary somewhere in the far east, and i think he may have indirectly legitimized the attacks that were on journalists. i think it's important to say that these attacks could legitimize prime minister fico's statements. it is important to me that the investigation be really guaranteed by a european authority, that is, europol. it's our shared responsibility.
madam president, i think we've heard a lot of criticism here and i think we should perhaps take a little lessons and a little optimism from the case of jan kuciak. we should say one thing that still holds true, that one day the truth will triumph over lies and hatred. and if this death reminds us of this message, and if we take away from this that we wake up, that we as politicians stop criticizing journalists for their work and stop being latent when one of our politicians criticizes and threatens journalists, then i think it's something that can serve all the states within the european union. it's not the first death, there was a journalist killed in malta before. we have to fight for freedom of speech, for democracy, for journalists to be able to work, to fight all the time. because those who sleep in democracy will wake up in totalitarianism.
madam president, the murder of ján and martina was understood by the citizens of slovakia as an attack on freedom of speech and they understood it correctly. and if their deaths are to have any meaning, then this murder cannot go uninvestigated. the citizens of the czech republic also understood that they had to defend themselves and the freedom of speech, so today in prague and other places in the czech republic people will demonstrate and express their condolences for the death of the slovak journalist and his girlfriend. it's also because in the czech republic, president miloš zeman is attacking the media and freedom of speech. the czech president also lashed out at the media, including public television, in his recent inaugural address. populists in our country are luring citizens to nationalize public media in exchange for forgiveness of license fees. ladies and gentlemen, it is also up to us, the politicians, the members of the european parliament, to stand up for freedom of speech.
madam president, if there is a strong political will in this house to achieve fairer taxation, it makes sense to deal with both cctb and the consolidation file together. it is high time to deliver. according to a financial times analysis, big multinational companies are paying significantly less tax than before the 2008 financial crisis, with companies’ effective tax rates falling by 9% since then. these proposals are the remaining missing pieces for the eu to fight tax avoidance. they will further strengthen our internal market and improve our tax system, which the taxe and pana committees have proved is necessary to counter aggressive tax planning and tax avoidance. taxation should take place where the value is created. too often this natural principle is not respected. parliament’s position also addresses a specific loophole in our taxation system: the digital dimension. again, we used a very simple principle. digital businesses in the eu without a physical establishment have to be treated in the same way as businesses having one in the eu. it is up to the member states to find an agreement which responds to the need to keep up with rapid globalisation development. i therefore call on the member states to join us in this renewed initiative to create a fairer tax environment for the benefit of fairer competition and, above all, the citizens.
madam president, i am convinced that the taxation of goods and services should indeed remain within the european union. this does not, of course, prevent the adoption of uniform procedural rules. we should therefore look for a strategy to keep the tax within the european union. the tax must be paid where the profit was generated. i think that is the rule that the european commission's proposal also complies with. i'm glad the intelligence people upgraded it. i would, however, like to distinguish this situation from unfair tax competition, that is to say, if the member states are legitimately setting their tax policy and it is not unfair tax competition, then it is indeed within their competence. but at a time when we are witnessing a kind of unfair tax competition between member states, i must admit that i am not entirely happy to see this - and this is very subtle - and it would be good if the european commission were able to respond to this question as well.
in writing. the main idea and the goal we have to achieve is that companies pay taxes where they make profits. i am totally against tax havens and tax fraud. i think we're missing a definition of what a tax haven is. we have to distinguish between tax optimization and fraudulent deception. at the same time, i remain convinced that fiscal policy should remain a full competence of the member states. for example, as the ksčm, we propose two tax rates, a lower one for smes and a higher one for large companies. the proposed directive, however, envisages that within seven years a minimum uniform tax rate will apply to all legal entities across the eu. i strongly support the unanimous decision in the council on these steps.
mr president, i will not repeat what has already been said and i agree with my predecessors. they merely state that spanish olive growers receive only the financial support due to other agricultural producers in the european union and therefore do not receive any special production subsidies. so in my opinion, this is not a violation of any rules, let alone wto rules. commissioner, in my view, the european commission should, in the present situation, first of all provide expert support to spanish olive growers, which has not yet been done here. the investigation by the us authorities undoubtedly represents a significant burden for these producers. second, we should consider challenging the us decision to the wto dispute settlement bodies. it is also appropriate to use the diplomatic service, that is to say to intensify diplomatic efforts to promote and defend the common agricultural policy of the european union, which, i repeat, is in accordance with the wto rules. given the current intransigence of the us side, we should also assess the risk of the extension of the us anti-dumping measures to other european agricultural products, because this risk is real and i think we should be prepared for it. at the same time, we should strongly warn the american side that protectionist policies can create instability and uncertainty on world markets, which will damage world trade and ultimately turn against the united states in particular.
mr. chairman, corporate social responsibility is obviously not a pr charity. corporate social responsibility is defined as the financing of public good projects, corporate, environmental or social policies, and the way in which their activities affect the atmosphere in society. so let's talk about the double standard. working conditions in parent and subsidiary companies, the relationship between productivity and wages, the payment or non-payment of taxes. we are talking about whether companies are not creating first and second class employees and consumers, not only from the point of view of third countries, but also here in the european union. this too can affect people's relationship with the society in which we live. the czech language has a simple recommendation: "stay at home".
mr president, i believe that the issue of corporate social responsibility is one of the most fundamental issues we need to address in the 21st century. i think it's an absolutely undeniable fact that just as every individual and every state, so too do companies and corporations have a huge responsibility for the state of the world. if companies continue to degrade the environment and exploit cheap labor, it's going to be hard to talk about human rights and climate change here. if companies and corporations don't pay their taxes and prefer to hide them in tax havens, then we'll have another and another commission of inquiry here. taxes will be paid by those who can't handle money, and we'll address the lack of funds for pensions. this is not a sustainable state of affairs, this is at the heart of future crises, this shows the injustice of the current system. and we need to act now.
mr. chairman, corporate social responsibility is a growing topic. it emphasises that businesses should voluntarily take account of human rights, environmental protection and other social criteria in their activities. i just want to remind you that this is nothing new. ninety years ago, the firm of tomáš a jan batýov knew that it had to take care of its employees if it was to be sustainable in the long run. they were not interested in their own personal gain. corporate social responsibility should remain largely voluntary. we can't just tell companies to do business in a particular place. but i understand my italian colleagues, i understand the employees who have come here to present themselves, i understand their concerns and their motivations, but i think that in this case it is above all the italian government and the governments of the national states that must be involved with their incentives and conditions for business.
member of the commission. – mr president, in the fast—changing world of work, new digital technology is opening up new challenges but also new opportunities. the skills that people have when they leave school should be a solid basis, but now more than ever before, people need to grow and upskill during their lives and professional careers. working patterns more and more often mean a change of job or profession, working in new ways or taking on new tasks, some of which we cannot predict today. people need to be able to easily show and present their skills, identify their skills gaps and easily find the right learning opportunities to fill them. for over a decade, europe has helped people to showcase their skills and qualifications across europe. many of you might know the europass cv, which has had over 100 million downloads since it was created. it has already been successful, but the time has come – as the two rapporteurs said – to modernise it. in doing so, we want to make the most of the new technologies to ensure that europass continues to support the needs of those looking for upskilling, mobility and career opportunities. i would like to warmly thank the two rapporteurs, mr mann and mr malinov, for your very constructive approach to this during the interinstitutional discussions. the amendments from you strengthen the commission’s proposal, for instance by highlighting the need for europass to be fully accessible to people with disabilities or elderly persons. i want to thank the entire team in parliament and the council for the excellent cooperation, allowing us to reach a timely agreement on this europass decision. we have made progress on the implementation of the new skills agenda for europe. the commission has delivered on all its ten priority actions. our aim, which is well reflected in the text that we have now agreed, is to provide an ambitious, forward—looking framework that offers modern tools for describing skills. the new europass will include the electronic skills portfolio, which will allow users to create, store and share documents and information on their skills and qualifications. it will include a self—assessment tool that will allow users to identify new skills they may need, share the results, add them to their cv, store the results in europass and map their progress over time by comparing with previous self—assessment results. it would also include skills intelligence, which is up—to—date information on trends and demands in different sectors, countries and professions across europe. this information can help individuals in their career management. all these tools to support people’s careers and upskilling choices bring an added value to europeans. the new europass text lays the basis for the next step towards closer cooperation and easier communication of skills and qualifications across the labour market, education and training sectors. let me underline the important role of europass in helping to deliver on the european pillar of social rights. the right to education, training and lifelong learning and the right to active support for employment is enshrined in the pillar. the modernised europass can now play its full part in actively supporting these rights. together, we can deliver on our commitment to making the most of people’s skills. this is good news for individuals, as better matched skills improve their work and life opportunities. however, it is also good news for employers – they can easily find workers with the skills that they need – and it is good news for europe. unlocking people’s talent is the key to competing and succeeding in the global economy. so thank you again for the work you have done.
for the alde group. dear thomas, dear svetoslav, thank you for your cooperation. i have greatly appreciated our positive cooperation from the beginning, from the committee meetings to the conclusion of the trialogue. it's been said that the best way to predict the future is to create it. if we're concerned about the future needs of the labour market, i think it's worth twice as much. europe's prosperity depends on its greatest wealth, and it is not its mineral resources or the latest technology, but it is we europeans who live and work here. in a rapidly changing global world, our skills will be the main driver of our competitiveness. this is why the readiness of graduates matters, because their integration into the labour market will enable them to pursue interesting and qualified jobs and lead better lives. a new version of europass, adapted to new technologies and specific needs, is the answer to how to further support and make people's skills visible. with the new europass, they will be able to present their skills and qualifications more easily and choose from job opportunities across europe. europass is an important tool for promoting labour mobility. travel for work is an important solution to reduce unemployment, especially among young people, which is alarming in some countries and regions. i believe that the potential for labour mobility remains untapped in europe. we should therefore be encouraging young europeans to gain not only study but also work experience abroad. moving beyond the comfort and security of home and facing foreign competition will give them confidence and insight. and that's the kind of europeans we need.

the rapporteur of the committee on industry, research and energy. mr president, madam rapporteur, europe currently produces more than 2.5 billion tonnes of waste a year, and only a third of it is reused or recycled. i think we clearly need new, modernized waste legislation to accelerate the transition to a fully circular economic model. and although the legislative proposals we have here are the result of long negotiations and compromises, i think they will make a significant contribution to a completely fundamental change in waste management. in particular, they set clear targets for reducing waste, preventing it from being generated or strengthening the waste hierarchy. i therefore very much welcome, for example, the increase of the reuse and recycling target to 70% of the total waste volume. or the construction waste measures. of course, in some areas we could be more ambitious than is proposed. for example, i still think that reducing food waste by 50% by 2030 is not enough. or the rate of reducing the amount of waste and litter in the seas could also still be higher. however, i support the compromise and i call on mrs bonafide to support this proposal.

as talented as men. women account for 68% of journalism graduates and 40% of media employees, but the gender pay gap is 17%. women are much less often cited as experts, at around 18%. according to the study, only 20% of films are directed by women. the main problem is the representation of women in media in senior positions (33%). such a disproportion has effects on content, media orientation and organisational culture. the internal culture is often unfavourable to work-life balance. this is a competitive environment characterised by stress and rigid deadlines. so if we want to support and implementation of existing legislation. we are not proposing any new regulations. we call on the member states and the industry to implement positive measures to ensure that women are represented more the question posed by the blue card. mr president, i would like to ask you how you would deal with the migration crisis in this case. what specific solutions do you propose? have you ever been to africa? have you ever been to a debate with african statesmen? did you know that many african countries benefit from having their workers work in europe or other developed countries? what would you suggest as a solution?
mr president, this document, in which parliament refers to all its international commitments and repeatedly stresses the protection of human rights in the context of migration, is certainly needed. but it contains a number of vague phrases: the need for states to unite in the fight against migration, the question of resources and shared responsibility. the document merely states the situation, but offers no solutions. ladies and gentlemen, we know that migration is a global problem, that it is necessary for the eu to take a stand as an international actor and to engage in dialogue with the un. but we need to find a rational solution. we need to talk directly to african countries and tell them how we're going to deal with the migration crisis. and we really need to tell who is a real migrant and who is a refugee. we also have to say to ourselves that we will not be circumventing the rules in the future, that there will not be people walking across the european union and saying which countries they will and will not live in. if someone is at risk, they should accept the first member state's offer and undergo the procedure in that state. and we other states should help this procedure to be done as quickly as possible. only by asking ourselves a clear question can we seek the right answer. and unfortunately, that was not stated in this document.
mr president, i agree that the wave of migration is simply a challenge to which the un and the eu must respond with a coordinated approach. we should take into account the situation of children and women in particular. however, i have serious reservations about the draft un charter and the european parliament resolution. first of all, the un global compact sees migration as a great and positive opportunity. i just don't subscribe to that view, because i see migration to europe in the wave of migration that we're experiencing as a problem. second, resettlement programmes would have to remain voluntary and the eu cannot seek to make them binding. and finally, third, legal refugee routes must not act as a motivating pull factor for mass migration. i believe that the eu's priority should be peace and respect for the human rights of citizens in their countries of origin.

in writing. the syrian conflict is slowly but surely entering a new phase. al-qaeda and other radical elements have been driven from most of the enclaves in the densely populated western part of the state. the islamic state appears to have only controlled some rural areas somewhere in southeastern syria and adjacent iraq, with most of the fighters already moving elsewhere. the united states currently has approximately 2,000 troops in syria, primarily in the northern part of the country controlled by kurdish forces. it is likely that turkey's efforts to weaken kurdish forces will continue in the near future, and at the same time a very likely syrian military advance against idlib can be expected. it is almost certain that a series of heartbreaking images will be released to show the cruelty of the syrian army. we may well face another chemical weapons charge, and we may also expect to be served with a charge from the white helmets in the uk. it is high time for the eu to change its attitude and start giving real help to the war-torn country. the absurd accusations of white helmets, reproduced by secretary johnson and other leading eu politicians, will try to prevent the adoption of appropriate decisions. this is why it is necessary to reconsider the attitude towards the regime of bashar al-assad and adopt an appropriate programme to help rebuild the war-torn country and thus enable refugees to return to their homeland.
madam president, the european union's relationship with russia reminds me of a rabbit staring into the eyes of a cobra. the eu's foreign policy towards russia is cautious and fails to give the right name to relations with russia. this is a dangerous game, madam high representative. russia is waging an information war against us, the european union. its aim is to dismantle the european union. after russia's territorial plundering in ukraine, georgia, moldova, after provocations against the baltics, after the intervention in syria, after the deployment of russian iskander offensive missiles in the kaliningrad region aimed at eu member states, it is time to clearly identify the russian federation as a security risk. we are not afraid of russia, mrs. mogherini. let us fear for russia, let us fear for its weak democracy, let us fear for the russian people and the other peoples of the russian federation.

for the alde group. mr president, i thank the rapporteur and my colleagues on the cont committee for continuing to look at the discharge process in a new way. as rapporteur for the european commission's 2014 discharge main report, i was already working to ensure that discharge was seen as a meaningful part of the life cycle of european citizens' funds. so i'm pleased that the focus on performance and error prevention is becoming a bigger part of the report. on the one hand, there is a need for the control mechanisms to function, i.e. to ensure the efficient, effective and economical use of the union budget. on the other hand, it is desirable to look to the future, to learn from mistakes, to share examples of good practice and to focus in particular on concrete results and projects with high added value that raise the standard of living of eu citizens. the union budget and european policies have a common objective to deliver results that cannot be achieved individually but only as part of a larger whole. each member state is a beneficiary, wherever the european budget is spent.

mr president, i sometimes feel that we in this house, or in the european institutions in general, set ourselves rather unrealistic goals. i would very much like to see us set realistic targets for waste management. the original proposal that was approved here a year ago was not realistic in my view, and this compromise proposal that we have approved today, which i supported, i consider to be much more realistic. i think it's important that we really build a circular economy and not direct our waste streams in one direction, for example, to landfills or incineration. we need to motivate the municipalities, but also the ordinary consumers, the producers to be responsible, and i think that this compromise has perhaps succeeded. i would love to see the plastic problem addressed after china's turn, because that's probably our biggest pain, we should be able to handle plastic at home and not export it.

madam president, the issue of the leak of personal data from facebook has perhaps paradoxically helped people to become aware and to realize that they also have a greater responsibility personally, but also the state and the european union. given the way social networks influence people's thinking, there is a great risk to our democracy and free decision-making. i consider the use of data for disinformation campaigns, especially in russia, but also in other countries, to be a very serious problem, which affects the results of elections and political developments in western countries and in the eu. but this cannot be taken lightly and the eu institutions dealing with this need to be strengthened. for example, stratcom, with its budget of 1.5 million euros and a small team of people, cannot face the disinformation industry that supports russia. so i'm very grateful that president tajani invited mark zuckerberg to the european parliament. he should come out and say what he's going to do to prevent the abuse of facebook user data from happening again. the european union, our citizens have more accounts than the united states, so it's only fitting that he explain here what the next steps will be. it is also right, commissioner, that you are dealing with facebook and leading this company to greater accountability and to take clear action.
madam president, i think there is a high degree of consensus in this house, which is rare, but i think that the issue requires that to be the case. i think that clearly there are systemic issues related to data protection. clearly there are criminal issues which concern facebook ethically, but there is also a question of a business model. but one element, while it has been mentioned, did not dominate today’s debate, and i think it is absolutely crucial, because the very fundamentals of our democracy might have been affected. this is the engagement intervention into elections, most likely throughout eu member states. i would say that this is serious and something that we need to know more about. i want to know more about who the clients were, what the business really entailed and, if we all want to know that, i think that we need to take measures to find out, as sensitive and explosive as it may be.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, the anti-money laundering directive (amld) is an essential part of the eu regulatory framework to combat financial crime and terrorist financing. still, we have clearly seen gaps in some of its current provisions which need to be addressed, as recommended also by the ep pana committee. if properly implemented by the member states, the improvements made to the directive can address these loopholes. i thank the co-rapporteurs and the shadows for their work. it was not easy at all with the council. the national anti-money laundering units’ powers will be strongly reinforced, thus allowing them to better follow the money and detect suspicious transactions. these new rules provide for more transparency, including public access to the information about who really owns the companies involved in a financial transaction. it applies to some extent to trusts as well. i call now on the member states not to delay the implementation of this revised amld which i hope will be adopted by the plenary tomorrow. several member states still haven’t implemented the previous directive, although they were required to do so as from july 2017. the european parliament’s taxe committee will closely monitor the implementation process of these new rules.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, almost three years have almost passed since the dieselgate scandal broke, and a little over two years have passed since the commission proposed a revised type-approval regulation. we finally vote tomorrow on this important file, and we can now say that the eu is reacting effectively to the scandal and delivering strong legislation – legislation that will help restore consumers’ trust and make sure our common rules are fully respected. it was obvious that the eu needed upgraded legislation in this matter so that there are no more loopholes such as have been used in the past by some manufacturers to bypass the eu’s rules. i am convinced that the trilogue negotiations were able to reach this goal, and i hope this house sees it in a similar way. personally, i am glad that this new law will help to fix the system by introducing clear rules on market surveillance, with specific targets for the member states to meet. this set of measures is, in my view, a major benefit of this regulation which should help to solve the situation on eu roads. it also gives new competences to the commission to intervene. both member states and the commission can act not only when type-approving a vehicle, but also when the vehicle is in production or in use. there will be enhanced control over member states’ authorities, both issuing type-approvals and carrying out market surveillance, as well as technical services and other relevant stakeholders. key now is the proper implementation and enforcement of this regulation. i am also glad that a new system of strict penalties has been introduced which could be imposed on manufacturers in case they produce and place on market non-compliant vehicles. in general, i believe that this outcome meets adequately our expectations and provides a robust and reliable framework which fits with our ambitions in the area of protection of the environment and protection of consumers’ rights.
commissioner, the revision of the motor vehicle approval regulation is a natural and inevitable response to the contradiction that has arisen between the rules that have been set, their actual implementation and the supervision that should, throughout the internal market, detect any irregularities in good time. the strict separation of approval and market surveillance authorities, the obligation to share information in case of identified deficiencies or the possibility to check vehicles already placed on the market will contribute to greater transparency of the whole system and will allow for a level playing field for all actors. any disagreement with the legislation should not be ultimately borne by the final consumer. on the contrary, consumers should be entitled to claim compensation if they suffer damage. i therefore also welcome the proposal for a so-called new consumer policy, which is intended to complement existing instruments enabling consumers to assert their rights and to introduce a class action institute.

for the ppe group. madam president, today we are voting on how to better protect victims of crime across the eu, which in practice is particularly the case for victims of domestic violence. so far, this type of protection has been weak. a woman who is subject to domestic violence can apply for a national protection order, which prevents the abusive husband from entering, for example, the apartment they share. the moment a woman leaves her country (for vacation or work) to go to another country, she is already underprotected from the rapist. why would you do that? according to 2010 statistics, eight years ago, some 120 000 women in the eu who had been subject to violence received a national protection order. the vast majority of them are unaware that they can be protected in other member states. to date, only 7 (in words seven) european protection orders have been issued. this is due to the failure of member states to communicate widely to victims the possibility of international protection and the reprehensible lack of information about this instrument. our report calls on the judiciary and the professionals who defend victims to change that. victims of violence must be informed of their rights.
madam president, i would like to thank mrs łybacka and the others who have prepared this report, which can also be an important starting point for the ministerial meeting in paris. the bologna process is one of the few pan-european programmes and we can criticise it for being a bit cumbersome or for some undesirable side effects. it's actually a collaboration of 48 different countries at the intergovernmental level, and it's also important to know that it's being done in an academically independent environment under changing political conditions. and in this situation, of course, the process is slow, but it also depends on how it's done by the independent universities. and i think that's the right thing to do. at eu level, we have erasmus+, which creates a common study area, and we want the bologna process to be carried out within this common european area. so i thank my colleagues and i believe that this is a good basis for a ministerial conference.

madam president, i have supported the directive which proposes measures against the use of financial systems for money laundering or terrorist financing. i believe that this directive does exactly what citizens expect from the union. terrorist financing is closely linked to criminal activity and money laundering. moreover, these phenomena know no borders and, on the contrary, take advantage of the fact that countries do not sufficiently inform each other, do not cooperate or have different rules. it is therefore logical that there is legislation at union level. i consider particularly important those measures which remove the anonymity of ownership and of monetary transactions beyond a certain limit, which must at the same time be sufficiently low. i believe that this directive will help to improve the situation against money laundering and terrorism.

member of the commission. – mr president, thank you very much for this very useful, serious and – rightly – emotional debate about the la manada case. i want to recall once again that we need the laws and they must be properly implemented and applied in the member states. at eu level, we have the victims’ rights directive, which must be properly applied. this directive provides specifically that victims of this kind of criminal offence must be treated well. i can imagine the victim of this horrible case – and many other victims – have to repeat again and again what has happened to them and the torture is prolonged. that’s why in the victims’ rights directive, we want the law enforcement authorities to handle these cases with full sensitivity and full professionalism. we also have the european protection order, which needs to be used better, and you are right that more enlightenment, more information and more training of professionals is needed. we desperately need to have the istanbul convention ratified in all the member states because it specifically addresses cases like that. as i said, we seriously need the laws to be in place, and full and good implementation, including the application and interpretation by the european courts is seriously necessary. i am not here to criticise the decisions of the spanish court or any other courts; mr president, ladies and gentlemen, although things are progressing very slowly, we must really say that children are the most vulnerable group among refugees and we must give them maximum attention. last week i was on a mission in greece, specifically on the island of lesbos, where i saw the situation of unaccompanied children in particular. while the greek government is indeed moving very slowly, i have to commend the work of the people on the ground who are working with great dedication, whether they are domestic greeks or employees of international organizations. i appreciate the work they're doing these days, whether it's in the hot spot of moria, the center of karatepe. i want to personally thank the mayor of lesbos for his extraordinary work. in difficult conditions, they strive to make life in the camps dignified and safe for all, especially women and children. these children, 3,000 of them in greece, are at risk of abuse, trafficking, they have no prospects for life. i therefore wholeheartedly support the maximum age limit for fingerprinting to be lowered so that it can be recorded and protected.
ms morvai, i shall make just one comment, and that is that i do have some experience of the european school and i must say that the pupils studying there are different in nature, of different origins and – you would be surprised – of different skin colour and also refugees. i myself came across that many years ago, and i do not think it has changed.
mr. chairman, i would like to follow up on my opening statement. i am for the protection of life in all its forms. an animal is a living creature and should not be mistreated. that is why i appreciate the fact that the european union has banned animal testing and i am proud that we, as the european union, are in a position to be a world leader in this matter. the opposite is true in china, where the government even requires testing on foreign products that have passed reliable alternative testing. since 2014, we've seen some improvements and limitations in testing, and non-profit organizations and cosmetic companies in the european union have played a significant role in this. they're trying to change china's approach, for example, by supporting chinese labs with training. it is indeed gratifying to see that european companies, conscious of their social responsibility, are helping where eu rules do not reach.
mr president, i would like to support my colleague and indeed all my speakers on this matter, because this is a very serious matter in my view. it's about safety on farms and businesses in general. the accidents that weigh down these operations are extremely dangerous and the operation is really stressful. i once talked to a farmer who told me that he was stressed about his work and the weather, whether it was raining or not raining at all, because there was a threat of drought and a threat of rain. these people are really at the edge of their mental faculties, and i don't even consider the health risks that come with these dangerous operations. i think we can help them. it is not just a question of the european budget, when they did not get very good news today. i think it's about organizing prevention programs first and then using pressure on insurance companies to negotiate fair insurance terms, because often these farmers are the ones who face unfair treatment from insurance companies.
mr president, commissioner, i think that the issue of independent media is indeed crucial to our democracy and the role of independent and impartial media is irreplaceable in a democracy. it's the role of the circulatory system in a healthy functioning body. i would therefore like to support the rapporteur's report. i think it is very good and i would like to add one issue that it does not mention much, and that is the issue of the risk of concentration of power, which is manifested in the aspect of economic power, money power, political power, that is, actually in that power to exercise one's will, and precisely the risk of parallel ownership of the media. unfortunately, in the czech republic and not only in the czech republic, this is a highly topical problem and it seems to me that without a relevant european initiative we will not be able to solve this issue. i do not often call for new european rules, but it is precisely on the issue of media pluralism and freedom that i think a european initiative would be most appropriate.
mr president, dear mrs spinelli, i have read your report carefully and i think it is a good basis for discussion. not to order anything and to open up the issues that need to be opened up today, such as fake news, such as the influence of some foreign powers on democracy and on events in the member states of the european union. but there's one thing that i'm interested in, because i miss it quite a bit. and that's the question of the responsibility of journalists. journalists are responsible for what they write. and i think it's only right that if they ever make a mistake, they should actually try to correct that mistake. and i think that's not emphasized enough in the report. i understand all these cases that are connected and that are directed against journalists. i understand the political pressure on journalists. i think that's very well reflected in the report. but on the other hand, we can also reflect on the experiences of some politicians who have been falsely accused of something, and the journalist has not said it properly.
mr president, in mid-april the united nations general assembly met in new york and the subject of road safety was also discussed. the statistics of the world health organization prove that this is a serious global problem. every year, i stress every year, 1.25 million people die on the world's roads as a result of traffic accidents, and it's estimated that up to 50 million more people are injured, many of them suffering lifelong consequences. the european union is the world leader in road safety, even though our target of a 50% reduction in road deaths by 2020 compared to 2010 is unlikely to be achieved and we are still far from the zero vision. that is why we cannot afford to let up in our efforts to make roads safer, not just in the european union. the european commission will present in a few days' time a new road safety framework for the next decade. we as members of the european parliament will also be making a significant contribution to its fulfilment, and it is therefore necessary to promote cooperation between members not only in the european union but also worldwide.
mr president, i have developed an initiative here, under the co-presidency of the youth group, of young meps, on the issue of paid traineeships, and i think that this is a question that has not been answered for a long time by the leadership of the european parliament or the european institutions. in the european institutions, many trainees are still working for free, doing menial jobs, often making purchases for various officials or meps, and i want to draw attention to this situation. i think a lot of colleagues who laugh, in their offices, maybe it doesn't always work that way, and i think the complaints that come from interns who then describe their internships as being able to learn how to make good coffee or bring the mps lunch during the internship, i think that says it all.
mr president, first of all i would like to express my great pleasure at having had the opportunity to speak in this debate, but for the third time at the end of the whole series, and i would therefore like to ask for some fairness in the distribution of the positions during the 30 minutes. i wanted to talk today about cohesion policy. the draft multiannual financial framework was published today and cohesion policy is not doing very well. that's a statement of fact. but this policy should be the very heart of the european union, because it removes disadvantages, it reduces disparities between regions, and i would like to work for its strengthening, and i would like to work for its strengthening, especially because we are seeing a very dramatic depopulation of rural areas and the inner peripheries within the european union. europe acts like a funnel, sucking people, potential into its center and leaving empty spaces behind. therefore, rural areas should be given priority support under the agricultural and cohesion policies. this is a significant problem, 80% of the european population will be living in the largest european cities within ten years. that's a real risk for our future.
mr president, mr prime minister, article 17 of the lisbon treaty states that the member of the commission must be chosen on the basis of the criterion of europeanism. this means that in the czech republic, for example, 68% of citizens can never have their interests represented in the commission. and they're discriminated against because of their political beliefs. article 22 of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union states that any discrimination on grounds of political opinion is prohibited. you said yourself, prime minister, that you don't want to change the treaties. but i believe that the lisbon treaty, which organises discrimination, must be abolished and we must offer citizens a better alternative to cooperation in europe.
mr. prime minister, i listened to you very carefully. i appreciate your commitment, your enthusiasm and i share your ideas for prosperity, security and values. but let me ask you a few questions. why do you want to open legal routes for migrants? do you think that we can transfer millions of people who live in very difficult conditions instead of bringing peace and prosperity to their home countries? how can you strengthen the competitiveness of european companies if we are making the free market more difficult with the new directive on the posting of workers? in any case, we are following the same line, as you are, and i hope for a secure and prosperous future for our children.
i consider the issue of sustainable transport to be extremely important, indeed important for the development of our towns and cities, and it is a key issue for cohesion policy. yes, there are examples, as my speaker said, where these individual projects have failed, but we have hundreds, thousands of examples where european money has helped european mobility and connected cities and towns. we are talking about the preference of public transport here, and i join those who supported this report for that very reason. it is not just about reducing exhaust emissions and emissions from public transport, but also about meeting the real preferences of public transport, including, for example, the reduction of travel distances, the so-called modal hubs, telematics. and in the czech republic, but also in other states, this is also joined by the problem of suburbanised areas, that is to say those that are in agglomeration rings, and where transport is really very crucial for the local population and for its impact on a healthy lifestyle.
madam president, i would like to explain why i finally abstained in the final vote on this report, which deals with children in the migrant crisis. children are the most vulnerable group and we must give them attention and protection. so in order to protect them, they need to be recorded, registered, and we need their biometrics. unfortunately, the amendments that were adopted during the vote relativize the entire resolution, and i think that's a mistake. it's in the best interest of the children to have their papers, to be protected, to be registered, to know where they are. it's for their benefit. i also have great respect for lord dubs' initiative in the uk to take in 380 refugee children. i think it's a good model for other countries.
madam president, i voted in favour of a resolution proposing a global ban on animal testing of cosmetic products and their ingredients. the european union has been prohibited from doing so for five years. but animal cruelty should not be tolerated anywhere in the world in the 21st century, not just in the european union. and research shows that alternative methods are also more effective. i agree that the european union should use its influence and actively engage in efforts to negotiate an international agreement that would lead to a ban outside the european union. i appreciate the active role of the european union in developing alternative methods and welcome the commission's pledge to enforce the animal testing clauses in trade agreements. i hope that one day he will be remembered in the eu as not only the pioneer of the test ban, but also the driving force behind the global ban.
i am convinced, madam president, that the use of animals for beauty purposes is not in keeping with my convictions, nor with those of many europeans, or, i hope, with those of all europeans in our european area. i am very pleased that the european union is leading the way in this area and is really setting the direction, but on the other hand, the entry of these products, which do not currently comply with our rules, into our european market, i think can be very problematic, to some extent even hypocritical. if we prohibit the use of animals in the european area for cosmetic purposes and then we consume these products produced in neighbouring countries, i think we are hypocritical to some extent. i therefore also call for the removal of these products from our markets and for research into alternative uses for cosmetic purposes without the use of animals.
madam president, world press freedom day is a good opportunity to recall the importance of free media, its role and also to call for support for public media which play a vital role in providing our citizens with objective information. we must fight fake news and the spreading disinformation campaigns. i am therefore pleased that it is also possible to use european funds to support public media and media literacy of citizens of all ages. the document also calls on member states to increase the participation of women in the media in order to increase their participation as experts and in leadership positions. the commission should work with the member states to combat disinformation campaigns and to fight for the independence of the media. daphne galizia, ján kuciak these are the victims that they made in order for the media to play their role and we should remember their names.
madam president, i truly believe that the media's role in democracy is irreplaceable. there are key independent media outlets, and they're facing a whole series of attacks today, and it's fake news that's talked about here all the time. the media becomes part of a hybrid war, an information war. then there's the problem of self-censorship and even that totally unacceptable censorship. the media is under attack, in our immediate vicinity there are dozens, hundreds of journalists in prison, and even in the european area journalists are being murdered. this is truly unacceptable. i'm convinced that the report could have been better, and i endorsed it. on the other hand, it contains some provisions, such as the problematic whistleblower protection in paragraphs 37 and 38. even though today is the day of the press, i end up supporting this report, but i think that the political motives in such a report are not entirely happy.

for the group gue/ngl. mr president, we are looking forward to the debate on the third and final package of mobility measures. i am pleased that the commission is responding to the european parliament's call and is taking care of the safety of our citizens. in 2017 alone, more than 25 000 people died on european roads and 135 000 were seriously injured. the new era offers the possibility of new safety features that should be affordable and save the lives of all europeans. and in that sense, i hope that the commission has prepared its proposals. the commission will also continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and to monitor the implementation of the new rules. however, from the czech republic's point of view, i very much appreciate the initiative to simplify the permitting procedure for trans-european transport network projects. but where the commission tragically fell asleep was in the co2 standards for trucks. the fact that the commission is only now presenting them to us is reprehensible and completely incomprehensible.
mr president, i have only three specific comments to make on this third part of the mobility package. first, meaningful electromobility is conditional on the use of electricity from renewable sources. electric mobility using electricity produced from fossil fuels only solves a local problem, at the cost of large efficiency losses caused by energy conversion and transmission. secondly, if we want to talk about zero emissions, we need to consider the entire production chain, the entire carbon footprint, including manufacturing, not just the operation of the vehicle itself. third, the widespread use of renewable energy is not possible without efficient, technically advanced storage. we need to support european innovators, limit the outflow of new technologies to third countries and develop the entire battery production chain. in this context, i thank vice-president šefčovič for his active approach to this issue and welcome the european commission's initiative, the eu battery alliance. in the european parliament, this is the specific area that the friends of batteries working group that i initiated will be working on, and i very much look forward to working with them.
in writing. i welcome the proposals put forward by the european commission, which is also focusing its initiative on improving road safety in the third part of the mobility package. the proposed measures are important in my view to move us one step closer to the long-term objectives of reducing the number of fatalities and serious injuries in road traffic accidents. the presentation of the strategic road safety action plan, which contains further proposals for action and sets targets for the next decade, will also contribute to this. almost half of the victims are the most vulnerable road users, such as motorcyclists, cyclists or pedestrians. therefore, new legislative measures are needed to improve their protection and to increase the safety of vehicles. this can help to reduce the consequences of road accidents that result in fatalities or serious injuries.

rapporteur: mr president, the report on the interpretation and implementation of the interinstitutional agreement on better law-making follows on from the work of the working group composed of members of the committee on legal affairs and the committee on constitutional affairs, and therefore allow me to begin by thanking all the colleagues who have contributed to the work of this working group. i would also like to thank my co-rapporteur, richard corbett, for his excellent cooperation. the commission has also provided information on the implementation of the agreement on the european economic area (eea) between the european union and the republic of moldova on the participation of the republic of moldova in the union programme 'horizon 2020 - the framework programme for research and innovation (2014-2020) '. in general, the application of the agreement to date is satisfactory and the agreement is, in principle, fulfilling its purpose. i believe that there has been goodwill from all institutions to fulfil the purpose of the agreement from the beginning and i believe that this will continue to be the case. this is an interinstitutional agreement and our evaluation therefore included an effort to preserve and strengthen those elements of cooperation in which the european parliament can contribute its expertise. one such issue is the respect of the correct legal basis. unjustified changes to the legal basis are of course unacceptable to the european parliament. at this interinstitutional level, i would also like to mention the question of the participation of representatives of the european parliament in the meetings of the council's working groups, also as an example of the certain disproportion that still exists in the area of information on the council's proceedings compared with that on the european parliament and its institutions. i believe that in the long run it would be beneficial to eliminate such a disproportion. however, it should be stressed that the report does not deal only with institutional matters. in particular, it assesses how well we have achieved the fundamental purpose of the agreement, which is to improve the adoption of legislation. i would like to mention here a few elements of the legislative process that are mentioned in the report and can illustrate how those principles of better law making can be implemented in practice. we think, for example, that the european commission should clearly state the legal nature of each of its proposals in its work programme and should provide a clear and realistic timetable. this would also help to make the european union more understandable to its citizens. one of the key things is a good impact assessment of the proposed legislation. personally, i have always supported taking the maximum possible account of the impact on smes, which i do not see as an advantage for them over other entities or employees, but as a reality. and the reality is that smes are absolutely vital to the european economy. the big issue is gold plating. i think it is obvious that we must continue to require that, in implementing eu rules, it is possible to distinguish between what is still an eu rule and what is already a national initiative which imposes obligations beyond the standards adopted at eu level. the average citizen has virtually no chance of finding out these days. the european union is a community of law and must therefore ensure that its law is truly modern, effective, clear and understandable.
in writing. i welcome the visible progress we have made in the drafting of eu legislation thanks to this interinstitutional agreement. i particularly welcome provisions that help to increase transparency, such as the establishment of a database for delegated acts. i also welcome the fact that the report draws attention to the so-called gold plating. the fact that a member state transposes eu legislation more widely than necessary into its legal order is not in itself negative. this allows member states, for example, to ensure an even higher standard of protection for citizens. but wider national regulation is not always welcomed by citizens, and the problem is that citizens cannot distinguish between which changes in the new law are brought about by the eu and which by the state. gold plating in such situations fuels euroscepticism and is partly responsible for the rise of populist sentiment in the eu. i therefore consider it extremely important that the commission monitors that member states, when transposing directives or adopting adaptation legislation, do indeed clearly distinguish between provisions which derive from and go beyond union legislation. we must put in place a practice that will enable us to take citizens out of the idea that the eu can do everything.
mr president, i want to draw attention to the situation in syria in my speech. this war is having a significant impact on the political situation in the countries of the european union and citizens should know the truth about this war. bashar assad started the war to suppress the democratic opposition. the assad family and the baath party have ruled syria since 1963, fifty-five years. it was president assad who released the sunni radicals who were imprisoned in 2011, and that allowed the islamic state to emerge. assad is portrayed as a fighter against the islamic state, but in fact he has used the situation to eliminate those who demanded freedom and respect for human rights in syria. seven years after the protests began, the situation is alarming. of the original population of 22 million, six and a half million are refugees abroad and seven million are internally displaced. ladies and gentlemen, bashar assad and putin are the ones responsible for 500,000 dead and millions more injured, and they should be brought to justice.
madam chairman, since i'm the last speaker, i'll allow myself to make a sweet spot after these performances. i would like to start by highlighting your speech and the proposal for a multiannual financial framework, which i think is fairly balanced. there's also a fairly large degree of flexibility, although i personally would imagine a much larger one, to be able to respond flexibly to any problems that might occur during those seven years. the multiannual framework is also quite close to reality, and i think that is also important. the european union should play a greater role in the areas of security, stability, external border protection, digitalisation, but it should also not forget about traditional policies. the last thing i want to say is about control. commissioner, there is a real need to emphasise greater control and greater emphasis from the start of the financial framework on the efficient use of european taxpayers' money.
in writing. i am very concerned about the way the european commission has approached the draft multiannual financial framework 2021-2027 and its communication. i am convinced that the european union, even with its new objectives and priorities, should not abandon the principle of solidarity, which is also enshrined in the founding treaties. i consider it unfortunate that a body which has the welfare of all europeans at heart should propose a plan which turns out to be anything but as positive as it was originally presented. the analysis by the european parliament's research service shows that the reported increases in key programmes are actually lower and the cuts higher. i am disappointed that the european commission has not tried to follow the guidelines adopted by parliament a few months ago, which were not one, but several steps ahead in ambition. as regards the expenditure side, the commission has not been able to present a sufficiently good document. we all know the direction in which the member states will push the amounts when they come to negotiate them. it will certainly not be upwards, and it will be to the detriment of all the students who would like to study abroad, all the mothers who would like to reintegrate into the labour market, all the scientists who are trying in vain to obtain funding for their research, and many others. i welcome the abolition of rebates and the proposal for new budgetary resources.
for the alde group. the revision of the posting of workers directive has opened up a subject that has long been hidden in europe, namely the sensitive issue of wage differentials between member states. the popular slogan 'equal pay for equal work in the same place' that made the directive visible across the states was supposed to bring everything back to normal overnight. does anyone really think that this will magically solve all the problems in the job market? i don't think it was. for over two years, we've been debating whether a truck driver or a construction worker sent abroad will have a salary of this or that. but the problem is not wages. it is about the future of europe and its values. it's a debate about what kind of europe we really want. i ask you now: do we want to preserve the free movement of people, goods and services in europe? or do we want to let europe become a protectionist market where it is no longer possible to move in a 'one-way' direction? the new rules will severely restrict the cross-border provision of services and undermine one of the european union's fundamental values. my position is not that i don't want to give our workers better pay and that i don't support unfair corporate practices. quite the opposite. but try to look at the real situation in the labour market through the eyes of those who are the real job creators. what do they really need? are these other orders that will tie their hands even more? or a simple and effective solution that can be easily applied in practice? we must take account of economic possibilities and consider the impact of our decisions on everyone, including businesses and employers, who are the backbone of the european labour market. the situation in the transport sector in particular is very critical and i am sincerely sorry that some colleagues have very quickly forgotten the promise to set up specific transport rules, which we clearly agreed on in the trilogue. as shadow rapporteur of this report, my aim from the outset has been to strike the necessary balance between preserving the free movement of services and protecting workers' rights. unfortunately, that did not happen. for these reasons, i cannot personally support the directive. voting for the directive, for me, is voting against one of the fundamental values on which the european union is built.
president of the republic of guinea. your excellency, mr president of the european parliament, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, it is a great pleasure for me to speak to you today. i would like to express my sincere gratitude for the honour you have bestowed on me by receiving me on an official visit to your prestigious institution, where i first came to receive the freedom passport. over the years and with the european union's investments, your assembly has established itself as a legislative body capable of responding effectively to the legitimate concerns of european citizens and of contributing to the search for solutions to the challenges facing the world. in this context, i would like to express our gratitude for your continued support for the efforts of developing countries, in particular in strengthening the rule of law, good governance, and in the fight against irregular migration and climate change. your excellency, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, as you know, since i became the supreme judge of my country in december 2010, guinea has strengthened its democratic process, in particular through the establishment of the royal institutions of the national assembly, the constitutional court, the court of auditors, the high council of the judiciary, the economic and social council and the high authority of communication. these institutions, now fully operational, operate independently and in strict compliance with democratic principles and values. we have also established a constant political dialogue, involving all political parties, civil society, the administration and development partners. this climate of dialogue has contributed greatly to the calming of the socio-political environment and to the building of peace and stability. all this contributes to the preservation of the many democratic gains made by guinea in recent years. we also have a permanent relationship with the european union, in accordance with article 8 of the cotonou agreement, to which my country is a party. the second session of the consultation framework held in conakry in march 2017 addressed issues related to the preservation of democracy, the fight against corruption and impunity, migration and the official development assistance that the european union provides to our country. we welcome this framework for exchanges between our country and the european union, which promotes mutual understanding in our political, economic and social cooperation. it facilitates the definition of common priorities and principles and strengthens the links between the different areas of cooperation enshrined in the cotonou agreement. excellencies, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, during my term as head of the african union in 2017, our continental organisation has been working on addressing challenges ranging from issues of peace and security on the continent, to institutional reform and funding of the organisation, with a view to strengthening our credibility and dignity in dealing with international problems. the reform underway should ultimately enable us to make our organisation more efficient and to promote and consolidate our financial autonomy, whether for the functioning of the institution or for the financing of projects and programmes included in the ten-year plan for the implementation of agenda 2063. i remain convinced that africa is stronger when it speaks with one voice. i have therefore appointed champion heads of state to be the spokesmen of our union on all specific issues of continental concern. in addition, african integration, which is at the heart of our union's agenda 2063, has gained irreversible momentum with the launch of the single air transport market in africa and, more recently, the launch of the agreement creating the african continental free trade area. in the long term, we want to build a stable, peaceful and secure africa, made up of states with inclusive, efficient and competitive economies in the global market and whose growth benefits all citizens. we want to make africa an emerging continent, with a single market with efficient structures while avoiding competition between its states. to do this, we need to invest substantially in infrastructure, particularly in the transport, telecommunications and energy sectors, in order to accelerate the transformation of our economies. that is why we launched the african renewable energy initiative at cop21 in 2015, with the aim of providing our continent with 300 gigawatts of energy generation capacity by 2030 and significantly increasing the use of renewable energy. i would like to thank the european union for its continued valuable support in making this initiative a reality, which i have the honour of coordinating on behalf of africa and of chairing the board of directors. the european union is a strategic partner for africa, not only because of its geographical proximity to our continent but also because of the quality of its relations of cooperation with africa in the security, economic and humanitarian fields. the close cooperation relations between the african union and the european union deserve to be highlighted. the third african union-european union summit, held in abidjan in november 2017, under my co-chairmanship, on the theme 'investing in youth for accelerated inclusive growth and sustainable development', reflected the willingness of the leaders of our continents to move further towards harmonising relations in favour of youth. the summit not only provided an opportunity to review our partnership in depth, but also to set out our common priorities in the four strategic areas of peace and security, cooperation in the field of governance, mobility and migration, and economic prospects for african and european youth. our cooperation with europe on migration and mobility will be strengthened with a focus on tackling the root causes of irregular migration, namely development issues and human trafficking. we welcome the adoption at the valletta summit in november 2015 of the emergency trust fund of an initial us$1.8 trillion to support the efforts of our states to stabilise our youth on the african continent. we particularly welcome the launch of the marshall plan by mrs merkel at our last meeting in berlin and are confident that this marshall plan will promote win-win cooperation. of course, we have said that for there to be a true marshall plan, the european union must devote a share of its gdp comparable to that which the united states once devoted to europe. the new eu external investment plan, set up and adopted by the european parliament in 2017 and with €44 billion of available resources by 2020, will therefore support our efforts to achieve sustainable development and create new opportunities for young people. in the same spirit, i welcome the contribution of the european union to the financing of the integra programme in guinea, amounting to eur 65 million, through the resources of the trust fund for the fight against the root causes of irregular migration. this programme will mr president, i voted against this trialogue agreement and i think it is not a good agreement because instead of improving the movement of services in the internal market we are building new barriers, new administration and more bureaucracy. this is not good news for employers, who find it difficult to provide services in the internal market, but i am convinced that it is not good news for employees either. we want, of course, to increase the convergence of wages, salaries between east and west, to reduce the differences in social standards between east and west. but i think that this should be done without resorting to protectionism and bureaucracy, that is, we should have improved the enforceability of existing rules and helped businesses to be able to compete and increase their competitiveness, instead of administrative barriers and bureaucracy.
