mr president, at the last plenary session we adopted a resolution on the situation in gaza, and although this was before the recent unrest, i believe that we can rely on this resolution. the european parliament stresses israel's right to defend itself against attacks and calls on it not to use disproportionate means to this end. unfortunately, we consider the recent incident and the recent israeli response to be inadequate. of course, we feel sorry for the 60 gazans who died. we feel sorry for these victims, but we also see this as a consequence of hamas' provocation. so whoever provoked it is responsible. and it should be said that hamas is a terrorist organization. we can't blame israel for whoever provoked this attack. the 1947 resolution provides for a certain status for jerusalem. we, of course, recognize the right of every country to determine which city will be its capital. for us christians, jerusalem is a holy city, and we know it's equally important to judaism and islam. we would like this city to be a city of peace and spiritual contemplation, not conflict and war.
on behalf of the alde group. madam president, commissioner, a country which silences freedom of expression is not a democracy. security forces that hurt their citizens are not the legitimate protectors of a nation. a president who violates the human rights of his people is not their representative. any country that murders unarmed protesters is a dictatorship. while many outside are surprised by the sudden turn of events in the brutal repression seen in the news, let's be clear: nicaragua has not been a democracy for a long time. the crackdown in the last month is the culmination of years of authoritarianism, repression and fragmentation of democratic processes and institutions. the authorities must immediately cease any violence against protesters and release all arbitrarily detained prisoners. negotiations must produce real change; a change that all nicaraguans yearn for; a change that will only be achieved through free and fair elections. what nicaragua needs is an end to the current authoritarian regime and national reconciliation. the first step is to call for new free elections and establish a balanced truth commission with independent members. the european union must be prepared to help in any way possible.
mr president, we should hear from the president of the united states of america at the beginning of june whether we can expect an unconditional exemption from the steel and aluminium tariffs or not. the introduction of duties alone should not have a significant impact on the european economy when compared to imports of these commodities from other parts of the world the surplus is huge. a much bigger problem could be the retaliation of the european union against the united states. the eu's top officials should therefore try to calm the situation and not take immediate dramatic measures. the aim should be to reach a friendly, mutually reasonable solution promoting transatlantic cooperation and setting new trade parameters. rather than retaliating, europe should focus on securing the european market in case of a diversion of steel from third countries originally destined for the us market, i.e. those states that did not eventually reach an agreement with the us. the excess steel production in china alone is more than twice the total annual production of the european union.
mr president, this is of course a very unpleasant situation, which we have not caused, but we have now the opportunity as the european union to show that we are indeed a positive global force and that protectionism must not and will not be our tool. we will have to respond to the us measures if there is no agreement, but i would strongly urge that these measures be moderate, that they be proportionate, that they be limited in time and, of course, that they be in line with the rules of the world trade organisation (wto). we must work with other partners, such as japan, which has already shown its willingness to oppose the justifications used by the us administration, particularly in the area of national security. but if we do go to arbitration or appeal to the world trade organization, we have to be prepared for a very long process. but we have the opportunity to take a positive view of this.
for the ppe group. madam president, libya is a challenge. it is a test for maximum coordination of the various instruments of european policy. this includes development, security, migration policy, humanitarian aid and the fight against terrorism. the european union is the largest donor in the region. for example, it contributes financially to all projects carried out by unhcr in the territory. but the real impact and awareness of our efforts is small, both among ordinary libyans and our citizens. this situation needs to change. one step that would help this is the report's recommendation of moving the european union delegation towards libya itself. the european union should continue to support the efforts of un special representative ghassan salamé in his efforts to stabilise the country and the process of national reconciliation. however, much more needs to be done to take account of the real dynamics in the country. continued support for common foreign and security policy missions and operations is essential for the stability of libya. they're not just helping to fight smuggling, human trafficking, monitoring the un arms embargo. the missions shall cooperate with the libyan side in developing a comprehensive strategy for the management of the maritime and southern borders. the european union must allocate sufficient resources, including the next multiannual financial framework, to implement the policies listed.
madam president, i would first of all like to thank mr panzer, our colleague, for his report, which is really comprehensive and very well describes the current situation and developments in libya. madam high representative, i also want to thank you for the update on the paris talks. of course, we need libya to be a stable partner and have a democratic government. libya is key to the detention of migrants. it is important that refugees in this country are treated humanely and that new refugees do not disappear, that human traffickers are fought here. in the context of the uncertain situation, it is therefore important to support the 2017 united nations plan of action for libya. as you yourself said, libya is a rich country, and that's why we need to engage young people in the reconstruction of this society, to prevent them from being manipulated into being involved in crime and terrorism. it is in everyone's interest to support the rebuilding of this country and democratic elections.
the question posed by the blue card. mr president, you mentioned here that the member states wanted to take responsibility for border control, you mentioned hungary, you mentioned italy. so i'd like to ask you why italy has not taken on this responsibility?
mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for this report. i think the document is built on a very good foundation. i think that in addition to describing the reality of the situation, he's also suggesting some ideas for improving the system. it's been said many times here, we're not happy with the way it works. every time we go to france, there's a rather embarrassing passport control here in strasbourg. i think anyone who walks through it has to tap their head, because that's not the way to prevent an attack or increase the safety of citizens. i think that no one thinks that this check will prevent any terrorist from getting into strasbourg. i think that within the schengen area we lack the enforcement of the law, and that is what is described in the report. it is a matter of all the member states really respecting what they have and not really circumventing it, whether in the form of the golden visas mentioned or the various passports they give to third country nationals. this is what leads to scepticism about the functioning of the schengen area. the schengen area itself is a great idea, and believe me, as a student at the pontifical university, i've experienced what it means to be out of schengen, and i've experienced those border controls, and i would never want those controls to come back to europe.
mr president, the schengen area is one of the european union's greatest achievements. it is an essential part of the european integration project. the schengen area has come under pressure in recent years and its basic rules and conventions are no longer functioning. i am referring to the reintroduction of internal border controls within the european union or the construction of border fences within the european union. for the first time, article 29 of the schengen code has been activated, new border controls are emerging. it's very dangerous. if the schengen area disappears, the european union as we know it will disappear. the preservation of the schengen area remains a key instrument for the expansion of economic and social benefits, the free movement of persons, goods and capital. and not just conservation. let us work, ladies and gentlemen, to extend the schengen area. romania and bulgaria, for example, are professionally seeking to join the schengen area. they're trying to protect their borders to justify their entry into space. i recently visited the bulgarian-turkish land border, where the bulgarians have built a fence about 200 miles long. they have thus demonstrated in practice that they are able to protect the external borders of the european union. they have contributed to the effort to avoid internal borders in the european union. thank you for that, bulgaria!
mr. chairman, thank you for your understanding and for the opportunity to speak. i do not agree with the criticism of the temporary reintroduction of internal border controls by some member states. that criticism comes up pretty often around here. at the time of the refugee crisis, it was clear that the european union was not yet able to secure its external borders, and so such a measure was of course legitimate and, i think, appropriate. conditions have changed and the european union has established a european coast and border guard and taken a number of other measures to secure the external border. it is therefore time to adopt a version of the schengen borders code that sets more objective conditions for the re-establishment of internal borders and prevents the excessive use of this measure. at the same time, we must not slacken our efforts to protect our external borders even more.
mr president, i am convinced that timely and clear information and sensitive treatment are essential to better protect victims. the fact that several member states have still not transposed the directive makes it difficult to evaluate, but the report nevertheless highlights the main obstacles to its implementation. i am pleased to say that the czech republic is now among the countries that have fully transposed the directive, since the adoption of the amendment that entered into force in 2017. but there is still work to be done, of course, and above all to improve the individual approach to victims. i can identify with some of the recommendations in the report. i therefore believe that in order to make the protection of victims as effective as possible and to ensure as much consistency as possible within the european union, member states should coordinate the definition of the concept of 'victim' and other key terms. i support the need for helplines, but i think it's more effective to have one universal helpline where experts can connect. it's better than setting up new specialized lines that will fragment information. so i appreciate some of the new proposals regarding the victims of terrorism, like setting up a special website. but i don't think we need to adopt a new specific directive. this amendment can be ensured by adding provisions to the existing directive.
mr president, i think it is necessary to say at the outset that the european union has also had a role to play in the dramatic change, improvement and strengthening of the rights of victims of crime. the aim of this report is the implementation of secondary legislation and as we know, the state of this legislation in the european union is not ideal. victims are still seen as mere evidence and treated as such. few people take into account that the victim of crime has often been through a very traumatic situation, and our legal systems do not always have a procedure for dealing with such sensitive individuals. ladies and gentlemen, it's very important to know that there are a number of victims of crime who do not have the capacity to be questioned in a normal way. it is therefore necessary that we address these people and improve their access to what my preamble mentioned, so that they have their rights adequately explained and know how to exercise them. the most problematic, in my view, is the very concept of sacrifice. i think we have a lot to catch up on in the european union.

the next item is the report by tokia saïfi, on behalf of the committee on international trade, on the annual report on the implementation of the common commercial policy (2017/2070(ini)) (a8-0166/2018).
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i cannot help but imagine how different this report would have been if we were discussing it three years ago. just think of how much has changed in global trade since the trade for all strategy was published. i am happy that, despite setbacks to global trade today, the eu is moving forward and not letting itself be tempted by protectionist tendencies in the world. but we still need to deliver: we need to speed up the processes of negotiating quality agreements and make sure they work in practice. we need to make sure that european businesses – in particular our smes and consumers – can take full advantage of the benefits from these agreements. we need an eu that takes delight in cementing a free and rules-based system on a global level. effective implementation and monitoring of trade agreements is key to achieving all of this: it is key to making sure that all european actors have the information and assistance they need to thrive via our trade relations, and it is key to identifying areas where we have potential, and we should focus on these areas. as i mentioned at the beginning, a lot has changed in three years. now is the perfect time to reflect on these changes, and not just how we should adapt to them – but how we can lead and drive these changes.
member of the commission. – mr president, the world is changing rapidly and so is our trade policy. we have in recent years focused very much on trying to bring trade policy into line with these changes age by making it much more efficient, making sure that it responds to the demands of our consumers, of our companies, and making sure that it includes digital trade services, that it is efficient and that it is implemented. we have increased transparency considerably; you can now find information on all on—going and concluded trade negotiations and detailed – sometimes regional – examples of how it works in practice. there really is a lot of information on our website. it has become even more value—based, emphasising the values of the european union when it comes to labour rights, the environment and human rights, but also to the value of open rule—based trade. we are doing this through our trade agreements, which are mutually beneficial, sustainable and fully in line with multilateral global rules. it is true that they sometimes take time – and there are some examples that have been absolutely too lengthy – but we are trying to do our best. the mexican agreement was concluded in two years. i think that is quite an achievement. but also, all the time, we have to take substance into account vis—à—vis speed, and you and many other stakeholders are putting increasing demands on what should be in a modern trade agreement and that of course also makes it take a lot of time, as we have listened carefully to these as well. implementation is key. we need to do much more there. we have focused on working with member states, on working with business associations, on working with civil society, on working with chambers of commerce and what have you, and on trying to reach out and to explain the benefits of a trade agreement, but it is simply not good enough yet, and we need to do much more to reach out to smes. lots has been done, but we can do more here as well. the same goes for evaluation and for enforcement, especially on the sustainable development chapters where we have this 15—point action programme where we will focus on much stronger enforcement and cooperation with the different stakeholders. we will be discussing with you this year’s edition of the annual fta implementation report. we will try to cover important developments, both when it comes to hard data and qualitative assessment, and also give analysis of our major ftas, our deep and comprehensive trade agreements and our economic partnership agreements, and take a closer look at some of the areas and sectors you are raising. free trade agreements cannot solve all the problems of the world, that is important to underline, but they are a very important tool, not only to contribute to growth, jobs and investment, but also as a way to promote our values and to connect with our partners – on a personal level as well – building bridges and not walls. thank you very much, mrs saïfi, for your report.

mr president, mr prime minister, you have mentioned a number of things which you consider to be central to the european union. i have repeatedly pointed out that for decades - 60 to 70 years - the european union has been facing a democratic deficit. and that is the main problem of the european union. that's why people don't go to the polls, why they can't identify with the european union. it is high time that we start thinking about an alternative, about a different arrangement for the european continent, where there is no democratic deficit, an arrangement that citizens can identify with, and it is another alternative to the five options that the european commission has presented that we could discuss, but that all continue into the future with a democratic deficit. we need to start a debate about a real alternative. i sent out a text a few months ago describing a variant of what europe might look like in an alternative configuration. i wanted to thank you because your foreign ministry responded to my initiative and at least indicated that in fact in luxembourg there is the greatest willingness to discuss an alternative arrangement of the european continent, where there would be no democratic deficit, where democracy, the rights of citizens, their democratic right to vote, would not be threatened by institutions such as the european union, which prevents them from having a democratic influence on what happens in the european union.
mr president, the purpose of the three oral amendments which i am going to present now is to keep the report up to date and to reflect what has been negotiated this morning in the area of cooperation and information sharing as regards the negotiation and conclusion of international agreements. i am doing so on behalf of the two co—rapporteurs, myself and mr corbett. the first concerns paragraph 71, where the word ‘two’ is to be replaced by the word ‘three’. at the end of paragraph 71, once again, the following sentence should be added: ‘notes the progress made so far and strongly insists that these negotiations should be concluded still under the bulgarian presidency’. the third is to delete the whole of paragraph 74.
mr president, i did not vote for this motion and i have two comments on it. one is about the resources. let's not mess with our own resources. the european union is a union of nation states and therefore it should be accompanied by contributions, and its budget should be accompanied by contributions, which come primarily from the nation states. if we create more and more resources of our own, we will cut the european union off from its basic building blocks and build it up somewhere in the air, and most importantly, we will have to invent some new pan-european taxes and fees. i don't see the logic in that. the second point concerns the change in the structure of the budget. i understand that cohesion policy should be limited, that for example the countries of central and eastern europe should receive a little less, while the border countries of the european union should receive a little more to combat illegal migration. okay, but in that case, we in central and eastern europe have to be compensated somehow. so please stop bothering us with refugee quotas and then maybe we'll agree to it.
mr president, let me start by saying that i am a proud czech as well as a proud european. europe for me means history, values and principles. they can help us to build a better future together, so we should not lose them, we should keep them. the selling of passports or residency for thousands of euros is surely not consistent with these values at all. i firmly believe that such a policy undermines the fairness of our society, creates a lot of economic problems, and downgrades, relativises or seriously undermines the achievements of our europe. sometimes it is associated with financial wrongdoings, criminal risk or at least an increase in real estate prices for residents. if you put into your computer search engine a question about the best passports for sale, you can be almost sure that among the top ten countries, you will find two, three or four eu member states, not excluding our presidency. some passports and residencies in the eu are cheap and some of them are expensive but very beneficial. as has been said, this is not a national matter – because whoever gets them has access to the schengen area. there are very strong reasons to reconsider this practice, so i believe that if the council acts in a sincere way, we will move forward and the situation where we are at the top of the list of passport sales will be in the past.
mr president, i am of course personally not opposed to solidarity between european countries when one of them is hit by devastating fires. however, i am afraid that the european commission's proposal has been a very quick one, as it also lacks an impact assessment, which i do not consider to be standard and reasonable. the european commission's proposal also recognises, for example, that it is crucial to focus on disaster prevention. however, only 10% of the total increased amount will be spent on prevention measures between 2018 and 2020, and i simply find that ridiculous. the european commission's proposal is full of unanswered questions. it increases the administrative burden on member states, appears to be costly and puts too much power in the hands of the european commission. the intentions may be good, but significant design flaws can result in loss of life for the experts sent in, and we simply must prevent that.
the question posed by the blue card. madam president, i have a question for you: why do you think the european commission, which is so vehemently opposed to various consumer issues, is so lax in this respect in the case of cars and in the case of the scumbags who turn the speedometer? why do you think the european commission, after four years of or perhaps even longer criticism, is not taking action and doing something about this phenomenon that is destroying the european used car market?
for the group gue/ngl. mr president, it has been a great honour to work on a document entitled 'manipulation of the odometer of motor vehicles: a review of the legal framework of the european union'. i am glad that many of my amendments have been accepted and that a solution has been found. the manipulation of speedometers in motor vehicles simply has to stop, because it is aimed primarily at consumers - that is, at all of us. at this point, up to 50% of cars sold across borders have a revved-up tachometer. this issue will be of increasing concern to the countries of central and eastern europe, as this is where diesel cars from western european countries, which are not supported by governments, will be heading. the manipulation of speedometers will not be reduced, and it is therefore necessary to draw inspiration as soon as possible from systems that have been in operation for a long time, such as the car-pass in belgium or the netherlands, which has been mentioned several times today. it is not to be expected that so many citizens fall victim to fraud that can easily be prevented. we must be able to stand up for them and end this shameful practice.
mr president, i would first like to thank mr ertug for his report, which not only describes the serious problem in the european union's cotton market, but also deals in detail with the solution. i fully support his proposals. the best practices already in place in some member states have been known to the european commission for some time, so i do not understand what is expected. i worked as one of the reporters on the vehicle technical capability package last term. we already called for the mileage to be recorded during technical checks and the european commission explored the possibility of exchanging this data between member states. four more years have passed since then, and the tachometers are happily rolling. it's not just the consumers that are suffering tremendous damage, i don't have to repeat that. this is unacceptable. the european commission must indeed come up with a concrete proposal for a solution as soon as possible. the european parliament has its own vision and i am sure it will support you, commissioner.
in writing. the manipulation of odometer status records is probably as old as the odometers themselves. an uninformed person might think that this is not a major problem. it's the state of the art that counts. as we go through the discussion, we all start to realize that tires, seals, and even plastics, they all age. that's true, but when you look at used car prices, you find that there are two almost equal factors that determine the price of a car, which is its age and its mileage. given current estimates of the number of vehicles traded domestically or across borders, so-called mileage congestion is a major problem. if up to 12% of vehicles on the domestic market and as many as 50% of imported vehicles have been subject to odometer tampering, then this is a major problem that deserves an eu-wide solution. i therefore support the proposals to create a linked database of all vehicles in operation within the eu. and then there is the introduction of an automatic transmission system for their mileage, which i consider impressive at an estimated cost of eur 1 per car. the commission has also launched a new initiative to combat fraud in the european union.
madam president, this debate is being triggered by the case of embraco, a company which manufactures components for appliances, as has already been mentioned, whose management decided to move production gradually to slovakia. production in italy had simply not been profitable for this company for some time, despite, i want to stress, financial support from the regional and national governments. therefore, the company decided to close its factory in italy and move it to slovakia. first of all, we should remember that this is a manifestation of the functioning of the internal market of the european union, that is, the freedom of establishment, which is a fundamental pillar of european integration. it is natural that if a firm decides to operate where it has better conditions and the level of labour costs is one of those legitimate aspects , it will move its production within the internal market of the european union to another country. it's her free choice. the decision to move production to another member state is a strategic and managerial decision of the company concerned, in which the state, and even more so the european institutions, should not intervene in any way. states can certainly offer incentives to companies to discourage their decision, but the final decision must be made by the companies themselves. these incentives must of course be in line with the competition policy of the european union, and i would like to stress this, and the commission has the prerogative to monitor this policy. if the decision of a company to relocate production to another member state of the european union has a negative impact on employment in the region or on the development of the region as such, it is up to the government or, where appropriate, the regional authorities to implement this aid within the framework of employment policy.
madam president, turkey is our strategic partner, and that is why we are concerned about the government's policy and the development of a situation where thousands of people, journalists, academics are still in prison. turkey is the largest recipient of our financial aid. the court's auditors note insufficient progress in areas of support such as the independence of the judiciary, the fight against organised crime or freedom of the press. the main reason is the lack of political will on the part of turkey. the commission must send a clear signal to president erdogan that the european union will not support his dictatorship. we must make our assistance strictly conditional on respect for human rights and the rule of law. we need to channel it into supporting ngos and media freedom. the european union must give turkey clear terms of our partnership. commissioner, you have allies in the european parliament. we have finally resumed negotiations with the turkish parliamentarians after two years and we expect that after the june elections there could be a much more intensive cooperation.
the question posed by the blue card. i would just like to ask: you spoke about the european people's party group not confusing the general political debate, probably in reference to the speech of mrs šojdová. but i think she actually talked about how she definitely doesn't support these activities of turkey, but she also talked about the situation in turkey in general. i would like to ask you if you agree that the situation in turkey is extremely worrying, as mrs sjödröm has just said.
madam president, i want to express my concern for the future of turkey and join my colleagues in this serious suspicion of an unforgivable, illegal use of european funds. i think this matter needs to be thoroughly investigated. of course, every state has the right to protect its borders, but it must do so in a way that is compatible with the protection of human rights. i would also like to say that i wish the agreements with turkey to continue, but the other side must also be willing to implement them under the terms agreed. i have very serious concerns about the future of turkey and whether we are not really seeing the birth of a new authoritarian regime or even a dictatorship, but are we also helping to do so in some way. i would very much like to see history ask what the european union was doing at this crucial moment for turkey, so that we do not have to be ashamed.
the question posed by the blue card. madam speaker, you are the next in line to call for women to be able to have abortions as a solution to rape. do you really believe that abortion is the best solution to prevent further rape? is there no other way to prevent women from being raped? wouldn't that be a better solution?

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur and the shadows for the excellent work they put into preparing the committee report. your vote tomorrow concerns the first of the two proposals we put forward on the european market infrastructure regulation, or emir, in 2017. this proposal is about simplifying emir, reducing costs and burdens where possible without calling into question the core objectives of emir: financial stability, risk mitigation and transparency. the proposal is part of the commission’s regulatory fitness and performance programme. it emphasises the need for cost reduction and a simplification. the need to revise some of emir’s requirements became evident from the responses we got to the commission’s general call for evidence on financial services legislation in 2016. our proposal is technical but important. its savings potential is big. the estimated cost reductions are up to eur 6.9 billion in fixed or one-off costs, and up to eur 2.6 billion in annual operational costs. our proposal includes a more professional clearing obligation for non-financial firms: it exempts the smallest financial counterparties from the clearing obligation altogether; it introduces a new mechanism to suspend clearing in exceptional circumstances; it incentivises clearing and improves access to it; and it simplifies reporting requirements, in particular for businesses without compromising the quality of data. companies, market participants, regulators and supervisors all agree on the need for this proposal and largely support it. i am very pleased that the debate and the vote takes place now as the adoption of the proposal is urgent. as mr langen outlined, pension funds currently benefit from an exemption from central clearing which expires very soon. without legislative change this exemption cannot continue, but we still need this exemption for a limited time. while the market has progressed, it has not yet adopted viable solutions for pension funds to centrally clear their derivatives. in recent months the commission has brought together all stakeholders to discuss progress towards a clearing solution for pension funds, and last week i hosted a high-level meeting with those stakeholders and i was happy that some of you joined us for this. i was glad to see that industry stakeholders all committed to design and implement a solution. we need a bit of time to put this commitment into practice, but let me be clear, we are not giving a carte blanche. progress during this extended exemption period must be monitored, and the commission will set up a monitoring group for this purpose. finally, let me highlight again that the purpose of this proposal is to introduce some targeted amendments to simplify and to make emir requirements more proportionate. but these amendments need to uphold the fundamental objectives of emir. for example, when it comes to reducing requirements to report or exchange bilateral margins for derivatives: if we go too far, supervisors will no longer be able to supervise and monitor over-the-counter derivatives and mitigate systemic risks. we must keep in mind that this piece of legislation is one of the cornerstones of the new system we put in place after the crisis in agreement with the g20. it’s our collective responsibility to ensure that it remains effective.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the original emir entered into force in 2012 and it is rightly considered the gold standard worldwide, but since the entry into force of emir it has been possible to identify areas where further attention is needed, or indeed where there is a risk of duplicative requirements which can add costs for firms dealing in derivatives. in particular, it is right that we are streamlining the reporting requirements by moving toward single-sided reporting. it is also positive that there will be exemptions for intra-group transactions and the removal of the backloading obligation. furthermore, it is made clear in the ep report that data quality is more important than quantity. we must also must bear in mind the practical aspects of implementation of emir. through the emir refit, we have been able to identify areas which can be further streamlined in the regulation. for example, an exemption for certain small financial counterparties from the clearing obligation, and it will now be possible for the commission to suspend the clearing obligation in cases of instability in the markets. finally, i am glad that the rapporteur has included a proposal to bridge the limbo period for pension funds. we must ensure that the pension funds are able to continue to make use of the exemption in the period between august of this year and the entry into force of the emir refit regulation. this will be one of the key priorities for my group as we move towards our negotiations with the council. my thanks to the rapporteur and to the shadows.
mr president, civil aviation in the european union and the united states currently accounts for more than 60% of world civil aviation. easa has been performing the tasks for which it was set up with relative reliability. it is logical that new phenomena have emerged in the aviation industry over the years and that, at a time when air transport is developing dynamically, it is necessary that the agency's new tasks respond to the situation. this amendment includes, for example, a new chapter on aviation safety management. the development of light aircraft, i.e. ultralights and lsas, is a positive momentum for the compromise reached in their parameters. but i believe that sooner or later we're going to have to come up with different definitions of payload. for many years, there has been a heated debate on the definition of the activities of aircraft modellers on the one hand and on the other hand the regulation of unmanned aircraft, so-called drones. i consider the current form of the regulation to be a good compromise. as with any compromise, of course, i see room for improvement. however, i will vote to accept it as it stands without any substantial changes.

mr president, it has become a habit, i must say a bad habit in this parliament, to speak constantly about matters which are not in its competence or in the competence of the eu at all, and that is precisely the case with this report on the modernisation of teaching. i can of course agree with many of the points in this report, that it emphasises digitalisation, the so-called knowledge society or the role of science and technology subjects in education, but that is not the point. the point is that teaching or education and training policy is not a matter for the eu, but for the member states. these determine the structure of education and the requirements that are placed on education. the eu can of course help by organising and co-financing various exchange programmes such as erasmus, but it does not organise teaching and we should therefore not go beyond these limits, although i understand that some colleagues are improving their parliamentary statistics with these reports. i therefore did not vote for this report and abstained.

mr. chairman, we are commemorating 10 years since the russian invasion of georgia, abkhazia and south ossetia. this occupation continues. unfortunately, at the time, we didn't register any major rejection and strong reaction from the west. maybe that's why russia was encouraged to expand further, because then we had the occupation of crimea and also parts of ukraine. so it is not worthwhile to stand by and watch. i am therefore pleased with the comments made by mrs mogherini, that the eu is supporting georgia and also the occupied territories, where it is developing certain programmes to further integrate these territories into georgia and democratic europe. we are also commemorating the 50th anniversary of the occupation of czechoslovakia, and i would like to remind mr scholz that this was also called fraternal aid at the time, but it was an occupation and in georgia it is an occupation.
madam chairman, the world around us is changing. the union must cooperate much more closely with nato to ensure the security of our citizens. moreover, the eu must increase its own responsibility for its security and defence. permanent pesco defence cooperation should be only a first step. nato and the eu currently have over 40 actions in which they develop cooperation. we need to learn together, for example, how to counter hybrid threats or cooperate in cyber defence. another area of cooperation i see is in the diversification of the arms industry. put simply, the eu makes weapons, nato uses weapons when it needs to defend itself. the state actor that is currently the biggest threat to international peaceful coexistence is the russian federation. i therefore welcome the strengthening of nato's forward presence on the eastern border of the alliance and the deployment of four multinational battle groups in estonia, latvia, lithuania and poland. i am pleased that the report says that the joint strategic approach of the two organisations towards russia needs to be strengthened.
madam president, i would like to begin by saying that ukraine is a bit like our black conscience. let us recall the budapest memorandum of 1994, which guaranteed the territorial sovereignty and independence of ukraine under the auspices of the united kingdom, the united states and others. since we could not help ukraine militarily, we must at least help with financial instruments and other aid for ukraine. the independent anti-corruption court that was set up a few days ago is proof that ukraine is indeed making progress in reform, even though the progress should undoubtedly be more drastic. we must do everything, not just reforms, of course, but also questions about the people who are doing the work. i see the progress that is being made in ukraine, although i would welcome a more drastic approach. i think ukraine needs our help and i'm happy to support this resolution.
madam president, the warsaw declaration of the nato summit held in 2016 recognised cyberspace as an operational arena in which nato must defend itself as effectively as it does in the air, on land and at sea. cyber space knows no borders, and hostile attacks can be political, security or economic. these include attacks on vital infrastructure, cyber espionage, mass surveillance of eu citizens, disinformation campaigns, the spread of malware, restrictions on internet access and the functioning of the it system. attacks are being carried out by a variety of states - russia, china and north korea - as well as non-state actors. the european cybersecurity agency in its 2017 report says that there is unwavering evidence of attacks on democracy and cyberwarfare. these activities threaten our security, public order, democracy and our strategic autonomy. the response to major incidents should always be a joint eu response, including restrictive measures.
madam president, i am very pleased that the eu is working to strengthen its cybersecurity rules to address the growing threats. 86% of europeans believe that the risk of cybercrime is increasing and that there is therefore a need to address and address this issue. we need an actionable response plan to large-scale cyber attacks, to protect european societies and above all our citizens. in this context, we must not forget the education of the population. reducing risk through adequate threat awareness is a prevention method that is worthwhile for all member states. only an educated population will also better understand the challenges associated with cyber threats. because again, if we don't address the problem in a comprehensive way, it can have a negative impact on our security.
draftsman of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. madam president, i would like to express myself in today's speech as a concrete, consistent defender of cohesion policy and to congratulate the rapporteur on a very good report. cohesion policy is important not only for the regions because it removes disadvantages, but also because it helps in concrete ways and also because it comes from the bottom up. we don't have to pretend that cohesion policy is some kind of cinderella, but rather that it can take advantage of the latest scientific methods. the way in which cohesion policy really helps is by focusing on new technologies and innovation. there are many good projects in the area of cohesion policy, waste prevention, recycling, recycling, efficiency improvement, reduction of consumption, for example in energy use. the role of municipalities and cities in the use of new technologies, biological waste in agriculture is also important. we need not pretend that cohesion policy is a static phenomenon, on the contrary it is very much alive, and there are a number of good proposals, concrete measures, in this report. i therefore fully support it.
madam president, cohesion policy is the key to making the circular economy a reality, even in countries that have a slippery waste infrastructure. if we are to achieve the ban on municipal waste disposal, as we have recently agreed here, we need to give poorer countries the means to do so. in countries like the czech republic, slovakia and poland, we are long behind in building infrastructure for sorting and recycling, and energy recovery is still in its infancy. although things are changing here, too. we know that ending landfilling and meeting the goal of increasing recycling will not be easy. i am therefore very pleased that the european commission has decided to support the circular economy financially not only in this programming period, which is now ending, but also in the new programming period, thus enabling them to make a smoother transition to a low-carbon and circular economy. this support thus exemplifies the very essence of cohesion policy, which is based on the requirement to reduce the disparity between the levels of development of the various member states.
mr president, the commissioner's statement, and the statements of many of you, show that if we do not adopt the convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence, the istanbul convention, then we have no instrument, no legal basis to combat violence against women. but i want to vigorously protest against this injustice. because we have here the european convention on human rights, which clearly fights against torture, inhuman treatment, protects the dignity of human life and respect for family and private life. citizens ask me: "what will happen if we do not accept the istanbul convention?" and i say to them: "in the czech republic, the istanbul convention does not bring anything new". the czech republic has had a law against domestic violence since 2006. ladies and gentlemen, i understand that in some countries this may be important, and i respect your opinion, but please respect the subsidiarity and decision-making of other member states.
mr president, i would like to thank the prime minister for his realistic speech. prime minister, you spoke about the rule of law and said that this should apply to every state. would you also agree that international law is part of the rule of law principle? if so, there are grave breaches of international law taking place, like the ones in crimea and georgia. if a member state proposes to leave such grave breaches of international law unpunished and, for instance, to abolish sanctions, would you agree that such a state is working against the rule of law and thereby against the values of the european union?
mr. president, mr. prime minister, the eu is not working. the world of european institutions and rhetoric that paints everything pink is not the world that people live in. it's a world of endlessly changing regulations, increasingly stringent and brutally ruthless and yet unnecessary. it's a world of prohibitions, orders, duties and fines. a world where established traditions and nouns don't apply. the butter is just butter, the light bulb can't get hot, and the vacuum cleaners don't suck. instead of democracy we have a democratic deficit, instead of free trade we have a single market. free movement of capital is a wonderful thing, but the vast majority of europeans have no capital. the ruthlessness of the united kingdom, which has chosen to exercise its legitimate right, is evident at every turn. and migration has shown that the eu is not managing its own existence and wants to address its failures with solidarity among its member states. it's a world where citizens have absolutely no say in what happens, and that's what we have to change. we must create an alternative, a viable path that will fix the european continent, a blueprint for establishing cooperation on better foundations.
mr president, i supported this draft report because i believe it is very important that cohesion policy also responds to the current challenges of the time. this is a problem of the circular economy and the recycling of raw materials, among others. this report has also met my expectations because it deals with specific projects that are important for waste prevention, recycling, energy efficiency, reducing consumption, and actually putting raw materials back into circulation so that they are not used just once. i am very pleased that a specific report is responding to these issues, calling on cities, municipalities and farmers to cooperate in the case of bio-waste. i think this is very important, and it is also important to me that it shows that cohesion policy is not some kind of cinderella, but is the dominant eu policy and has a bearing on the other innovations and technologies that we are coming up with.

member of the commission. – mr president, first of all, i would like to thank you for this very lively debate on the migratory situation in the mediterranean. i take from this debate that we all share finally – or almost all of us – the same objectives, and that is very important for us. there cannot be one and only one answer. there is no single solution and we all know that. no member state can do this alone. it is impossible. the only way is to adopt all the missing elements and to implement a comprehensive migration policy. this policy starts with cooperation with third countries. it continues with the protection of our common european borders – our external borders. it is in line with a future-proof european asylum system based on solidarity and responsibility. it is very much relying on effective return of people that have no right to stay in europe. it is completed by the creation of legal pathways to come to europe and by effective integration policies for those legally living among us. we need to continue our common work and our joint efforts in all aspects in parallel. as regards some of the points you raised – mr corrao and many of his colleagues here referred to the support given to italy – we have worked continuously and relentlessly over the past three years to ensure that much greater solidarity is shown to all member states facing disproportionate migration pressures due to their geographical situation. i want to commend the herculean efforts made by italy over these past years. the commission – and you all know that it has stood and continues to stand by italy politically, operationally and financially – has granted more than eur 653 million to italy under the eu migration and home affairs funds to date. on top of that, we have offered eur 193 million in emergency assistance. the european borders and the coastguard agency deployment is currently deploying 260 experts for joint operation themis. eighty asylum experts from member states and interim staff have been deployed to support the operational activities. europol guest officers are present in several hotspots and 12 963 persons in need of international protection have been relocated. we are also working closely with italy to prevent departures from northern africa. we have invested eur 200 million in libya to assist migrants, and eur 46 million for border management. we will also grant tunisia eur 25 million for a border management project. as i said, this approach has brought results. arrivals in italy have decreased significantly. i look forward to working with the new italian government to ensure that arrivals decrease even further. as i said during my introductory remarks, i am not here to play any blame games, and anyway it doesn’t help. the aquarius incident shows that we need structural solutions and that solidarity cannot be an ad hoc or one-off thing. this is the essence of our discussions on dublin, on stronger external borders, on cooperation with third countries and on our proposals today for more migration and borders funding. these are european challenges which require genuine european solutions. this is not the responsibility of italy alone or of malta alone or of spain alone. this is the responsibility of the european union and its member states as a whole. under these very pressing circumstances, and given the experience and the lessons we have learned over the last few years, we should reiterate our determination to safeguard the principles and unity of europe in a spirit of responsibility. we should stand as one, guaranteeing a future-proof, balanced and fair european migration policy. all of us here have a great responsibility to show leadership and to ensure the unity and cohesion of our societies, avoiding divisive rhetoric. migration has become a challenge for our unity. it is up to us to turn this into an opportunity, into a strong bond among eu institutions, among member states and among our citizens.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, commissioner, i have listened carefully to your views on the humanitarian situation in the mediterranean sea. the topic is of crucial importance, and it is my hope that we will be able to find a common solution quickly. the migrants certainly deserve that but so do our constituencies. we shouldn’t forget that most of the refugees and asylum seekers, particularly children and women, were affected by war and terror in their home countries. migration is one of the major humanitarian challenges that the european union is currently still facing and, as we discussed together yesterday and today, we need a holistic approach to migration management. the european union response to this challenge has two main dimensions: strengthening the instruments for limiting irregular migration and addressing the root causes of migration. many of you referred today to the reform of the dublin regulation system and – sharing the common understanding that the european union needs a properly functioning common asylum system – the council has not stopped working to enhance the security of our citizens, to strengthen border control and to manage the migratory process more efficiently. the council has listened to you carefully and we welcome parliament’s input in this debate. the bulgarian presidency fully shares your concerns and the sense of urgency around the need for asylum system reform which is both effective and protective. so we are doing our best to advance. we have proposed a qualitatively new approach to the dublin regulation. we are working not just on general principles but on the legal text of the regulation, article by article, and on concrete solutions. so we have built into the dublin regulation a new crisis mechanism for managing the migration and asylum systems in the union when under pressure. the presidency proposal provides clear criteria defining levels of pressure, and lays down measures aimed at establishing a proportionate response in each phase, allowing for timely mitigation, based on common action, of the challenges faced in addressing the root causes of the pressure at the very beginning, thus preventing escalation of the situation. the presidency is trying to strike a balance between responsibility and solidarity. as i have already mentioned in relation to the preparation for the european council meeting yesterday, the process of seeking consensus is now being led by the president of the european council. it is up to him and the leaders to look for compromises and propose a way ahead. any solution that will be found should take account of the interests of all member states – the frontline member states and also the member states that have been destination countries for asylum seekers. any decision will need to provide sustainable solutions that will help the union to cope with situations similar to the one we faced a couple days ago.

the next item is the report by andreas schwab, on behalf of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, on a proportionality test before adoption of new regulation of professions (com(2016)0822 – c8—0012/2017 – 2016/0404(cod))(a8—0395/2017).
madam president, i thank you for this motion. i regard it as a step in the right direction for an open internal market. the opposite is what we have seen recently on the ep's floor. instead of removing administrative barriers, new ones are being created, for example, in the adoption of the directive on the posting of workers. i personally believe that europe needs to be protected above all from protectionist measures by the member states and from social dumping by third countries. the imco committee recently buried the e-card proposals. it could at least make a small contribution to a more functioning market by reducing administrative barriers. however, it has been shown that even the removal of these barriers can be seen by some countries as an attack on their labour market. i am therefore pleased that we are at least giving this proposal from the services package a chance, and i believe that a common framework for the implementation of the proportionality test for all professions, including healthcare, will help to remove obstacles to greater mobility of persons, but at the same time will also contribute to strengthening the certainty and quality of the professions practised in the internal market. thank you, and i hope this motion will pass.
mr president, i think it is right that the eu monitors the situation of ethnic minorities and, if there are problems, demands that they be remedied and, of course, also supports the member states in addressing their situation. let me mention specifically the situation in the czech republic. the czech republic has its own roma integration strategy until 2020. it aims to reverse negative trends in roma development in education, employment, housing and social spheres. there are 245,000 roma in the czech republic, half of whom are integrated and half of whom still live in ghettos. let me say here that if we want to help and prevent ghettos, exclusion, poverty, we must help the roma, but the roma themselves must be willing and active. the czech government, and especially the schools themselves, are striving to integrate them into education, because this is the basic condition for their future successful life. and here too, the eu is helping, because eu funds have been used to prepare a number of programmes to integrate roma children and improve their education. i am proud that the czech republic has finally managed to correct the injustice and to properly repair the land on the site of the former roma concentration camp in letech u písku. it was the minister of culture of my political party who ended this activity, and this is a condition for roma to feel safe, to actively participate in improving their lives. we're gonna help them with that.
mr president, i also thank the commissioner for his report and the attention he has paid to the issue of language education. i think it's in the eu's interest to have people talking to each other. to do this, they undoubtedly need a lingua franca, which is english today, and if they are gifted, they can certainly master another foreign language. the eu must also promote education in the mother tongue. of course, it is in the interest of the member states to be active in promoting the language literacy of their citizens, but given the mobility it is also up to the eu to help them coordinate language education, and that is why we propose a mechanism for regular transparent assessment of language competences across the member states. the eu should support educational institutions that provide instruction in the official eu languages for citizens living outside their own member state. last but not least, we should also help to make university education accessible through an online system for the cross-border recognition and validation of language certificates for the purpose of school leaving certificates. we should also use the competence, the capacity, that the oecd has. i would like to thank my colleague mr malinov for his initiative and the commissioner for his cooperation.
mr president, despite the international ban on ivory exports, illegal ivory hunting and trade has reached record levels. it is estimated that between 20,000 and 30,000 african elephants are killed by poachers each year. one of the main reasons for this increase in illegal trade is the growing demand for ivory products in asia. but we must also acknowledge that demand for ivory in the eu is growing. in my work on the palm oil report, i have seen firsthand how the eu too often ignores how we contribute to the destruction of nature and biodiversity. but it doesn't pay to look the other way when it could be the cause of extinction. the eu has its obligations and must live up to them. there is no other way if we value natural resources, there is no other way if we value ourselves.
in writing. i am strongly in favour of closing the eu ivory market. the us, china, hong kong and the uk have already adopted measures to close their domestic markets that are much stricter than those currently in force in the eu. both the african elephant loxodonta africana and the indian elephant elephas maximus are listed in the annexes to the convention on international trade in endangered species of wild fauna and flora (cites). under the current cites regime, international trade in ivory is prohibited, but domestic trade in ivory is not regulated by cites. therefore, the european parliament reiterates its call for the closure of the eu market for eu ivory. it should be noted that the illegal trade in ivory has more than doubled since 2007. illegal fishing has reached high levels in recent years. it is reported that poachers kill 55 elephants a day. since 2011, between 20,000 and 30,000 african elephants have been killed each year. although trade in ivory in the eu is strictly regulated by the eu regulation on trade in wildlife and wild fauna and the importation of ivory into the eu and its trade within the eu and its export from the eu are generally not allowed for commercial purposes, the eu has a major role to play. if the eu ivory market is not closed, we cannot expect any improvement.
mr president, allow me to begin by thanking my colleague mr zdrojewski for his report. it's so great that it was very difficult to prepare an amendment to it. culture, it's the monuments, it's the theater, it's the movies, but it's also how we treat each other, what kind of environment we create around us. cultural policy is often underestimated, undervalued, even financially, while culture is europe's greatest asset compared to the world. europe's creative industries and cultural heritage are the largest in the world and have enormous economic potential, contributing to european cohesion and unity in our diversity. at the same time, culture is also going through digitization. we're talking about copyright, and that includes the availability of copyrighted works on the internet. not just about availability, but about quality, and i want to emphasize that. mr zdrojewski's report responds to digitalisation in great detail. it emphasises the need for accessibility for disadvantaged groups. culture cannot be exclusive, which is why i also welcome the pilot project for 18-year-olds, discover europe, where young people can discover european culture for free. in short, we need to find effective solutions, to support culture from both public and private sources as an investment, and this starts with children in need of quality education and training for the future.
mr president, i believe that structural and financial barriers to access to culture are a major problem. yes, perhaps not the greatest, but certainly significant. access to culture is a fundamental right of all citizens and deserves our attention. the famous czech actor jan werich once said: "i think that civilization is not good if it is without culture. to be happy and content, a person needs not only physical comfort but also mental comfort. culture and civilization must go hand in hand. i agree with that quote completely. in the post-crisis era, we should make sure that culture is available to everyone, not just the elite. culture is not a matter for the elite, but for all without distinction. culture is such a human thing that if we don't take care of it, we risk a lot more than we think.

madam president, we have become accustomed to freedom as well as to the fact that we no longer know the feeling of hunger. after seven decades of peace, we have become accustomed to the comfort of not the author. mr president, allow me to express my own views on the resolution concerning nabil rajab. the epp/ecr resolution is a resolution that really reflects this case and other cases where people are imprisoned in bahrain for their political opinions and for their activities. in this sense, the eu, through this resolution, expresses its views and concerns on this case, which has a far wider scope. since 2011, more than 13 activists have been arrested in bahrain, with all cases being relatively unclear. unfortunately, there are restrictions on human rights and freedoms, which are contrary to our european values. the right to a fair trial, the right of access to a court of law, the right of defence and other rights must be respected at all times. activists and human rights defenders are an important component of any democracy and it is essential that they have a place in democracy. this resolution does not end it. we want to have an open dialogue with bahrain and we hope that we will succeed.

mr president, we want to support the bahraini human rights defender nabil rajab today, first and foremost because we believe that freedom of expression is a fundamental human right and that its exercise contributes to the good functioning of society. nabil rajab is facing a total of seven years in prison today for expressing his views on twitter, including on saudi arabia's bombing of yemen, and thereby committing the crime of insulting a neighboring state under bahraini law. mr rajab's trial has been conducted in a manner that violates bahraini law on several occasions. but there is a chance that mr. rajab will be pardoned. we now call on bahrain to ensure that mr rajab is not subjected to any humiliating treatment, torture and that his trial meets international fair trial standards.
mr president, the rohingya are being persecuted and are suffering a severe humanitarian crisis, and children are the most vulnerable. unicef states that 720 000 rohingya children need assistance and protection now. that is 720 000 children being denied a chance for a future: being subject to rape, violence and trauma, being orphaned, being trafficked, being hungry, being denied an education, being forced to give birth to their rapists’ children, and without proper healthcare. these are children who have been suffering far too long and enduring beyond what anyone should have to endure in a lifetime. no child deserves this. it is the duty of the myanmar government and that of bangladesh to do everything they can to safeguard the rights of all children affected by the crisis and to guarantee their safety. the eu must continue working to stop the crisis and it must look into other measures if myanmar refuses to uphold the rights of the rohingya.
madam president, i have supported the cult report on my own initiative and i thank my colleague mr zdrojewski for the excellent way in which he has presented it. the starting point is the universal declaration of human rights, which contains the right to access culture, and i consider this an important value. the report rightly addresses the obstacles to greater access to culture, especially for disadvantaged groups. it also seeks solutions for culture in an age of increasing digitisation, for accessibility and quality. a special area is devoted to the financing of culture, which is primarily the responsibility of the member states. but the eu can help significantly with its funds and programmes. we will soon be discussing the creative europe programme for the next financial period until 2027. the commission proposes to increase the budget for this programme to eur 1.85 billion over seven years. i think we need to support this raise. the creative industries in europe are very strong and represent a great deal of employment and growth for europe.
madam president, i voted in favour of the agreement reached at the trilogue on the draft directive on the adequacy test before the adoption of new legislation on professions. a common framework for assessing proportionality will increase the transparency and predictability of national regulation. at the same time, the quality and safety standard for the performance of specific professions must remain guaranteed. more transparent rules will then facilitate eu citizens' ability to practise a regulated profession in another member state. the framework for the implementation of the proportionality test will thus be clearly defined, while avoiding excessive procedural complexity and unnecessary administrative burden. i therefore consider it appropriate that member states should have a certain margin of discretion in assessing whether it is appropriate to consult stakeholders before introducing new rules.
madam president, this year marks the 10th anniversary of russia's invasion of georgia and its ongoing occupation of the georgian regions of abkhazia and south ossetia. we've had a very detailed discussion about this. i have supported and signed this resolution and i call for support for georgia and its sovereignty. i also call on the politicians of the eu countries to support the boycott of the world cup in russia, which starts today, by politicians, it is very symbolic. of course, neither the athletes nor the fans can blame the organization for the bad decisions. let's remember that it was less than two weeks after the sochi olympics ended that russia invaded crimea. we can ask ourselves what will follow after the championship is over.

the next item is the report by francisco assis, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on violation of the rights of indigenous peoples in the world, including land grabbing (2017/2206(ini)) (a8—0194/2018).
madam president, to date, more than 750 million women have been married before the age of 18, and 250 million of them were married before the age of 15. these are truly frightening numbers that demonstrate serious human rights violations. the problem is that a legal ban on child, early, and forced marriage would not in itself guarantee an end to such marriages. the widest possible international cooperation is needed to see an end to this shameful practice. the eu should now push for the establishment of a clear legal age of 18 as a uniform minimum age for marriage in both the eu member states and third countries. i believe it is the duty of the european union to condemn these marriages in the strongest possible terms and to act with utmost resilience in this regard, both within the eu and internationally. we in the european union have enough instruments to influence third countries, so let us do the same in this matter.
madam chair, i think we all see forced marriage as a violation of human rights and human dignity. we are hearing about the rise of forced marriages, especially in countries where there is now war and humanitarian crisis. for example, we know that in syria before the war, there were about 13 percent of these marriages, and recently, that percentage has risen to 40 percent. what's the reason? forced marriages are caused by both a long tradition, partly religious, and by living conditions. parents see the marriage of their children, especially daughters, as a solution to difficult social conditions or a way to protect them from even worse forms of violence. for many girls, this means dropping out of school, domestic violence, mental problems, and other complications, such as childbirth. however, customs and circumstances can be stronger than the law, and even where such marriages were forbidden by law, their number has decreased only minimally. that means more education and better living conditions are the answer.
the european union has always been a major player in the development of cities, and the european union has always been a major player in the development of cities. our democracy was born in the greek polis. even now, cities are the main hubs of population growth. we know that 70% of the population of the european union lives there today, and that trend is growing. i'm more of a proponent of not urbanizing so fast and not depopulating the countryside. but the reality is just relentless. the growth of cities and their agglomeration places new demands on them, on their infrastructure, on their spatial planning, and it is therefore good that we are also looking at the role of cities in the institutional framework at european union level. i support greater involvement of city representatives in consultation on measures and legislation, both through the committee of the regions and through informal opportunities.
madam chair, 3d printing has great potential to achieve a whole range of socially beneficial goals. in particular, it allows the concentration of production, thereby shortening the production process and reducing transport and storage costs. all of this is, of course, ecological. for decades, this method was reserved for certain sectors of industry. however, in recent years it has become increasingly accessible to other sectors and consumers. for example, in the event of damage to a product, it allows the consumer to make a replacement part instead of buying a new product. this is of course useful in the context of environmental protection, but it has its risks, and the rapporteur has mentioned some of them. as a member of the committee on culture and education, i consider the risk of infringement of the rights of authors to be a problem. as market developments and demand suggest that 3d printing will become even more accessible and the risk of misuse will therefore continue to increase, i welcome the suggestion that the commission consider the possibility of adopting specific regulation of some aspects of this technology.
mr. chairman, i would like to express my concern about a new form of organized crime that threatens our security and complicates our handling of the migration crisis. it's a trade in stolen identity papers that helps illegal migrants get into the european union. these people, having acquired real passports, can very easily evade border controls, immigration regulations and visa requirements. according to europol, more than 200,000 people crossed european borders in this way last year. security experts warn that these practices are the cause of uncontrolled infiltration of criminals and terrorists into the european union. smugglers of migrants are getting more and more organized. they create sophisticated solutions and professional networks and also operate transnationally, making the security threat to the european union ever stronger. i therefore call on the european commission to address this issue and to come up with effective solutions.
mr president, i would like to focus on cohesion policy for a moment. unfortunately, it is to be weakened considerably in the coming period, by at least 10% of its resources. cohesion policy is indeed crucial for the future of europe, it removes disadvantages, builds on cohesion, helps the development of weaker regions. without cohesion policy, many regions would be fundamentally weakened. the scissors between them would have developed much more than they do now. cohesion policy is for me an expression of europeanism and solidarity. i consider the weakening of the impact and character of cohesion policy to be extremely dangerous for our future. i would like to discuss the type of cohesion policy we want, the type of cohesion policy our citizens want, but i do not think it is possible to weaken cohesion policy without also affecting our future.

mr. chairman, i also supported this material. i think the role of cities in our future is extremely important. up to 80% of europe's population will be living in europe's largest cities within a few years. and this is a truly staggering challenge for the quality of life, the accessibility of infrastructure, the availability of adequate public services in these cities, the mastery of spatial planning and the overall sustainable development of cities and towns. this is indeed a very significant challenge for our future period. i would also like to mention the problem of the suburbanized areas, i.e. those areas which are adjacent to the largest cities in the form of agglomeration rings. and in these rings, there are really very fundamental themes for further development. i am glad that the report addresses these issues and i am glad that it has not avoided the very delicate issue of representation in the committee of the regions, where i firmly believe that there should be a change in representation in this consultative body in the coming period.
mr president, i also voted in favour of this report by mrs bergeron, who draws attention to the problem of 3d printing and calls on the commission to consider specific legislation on certain aspects. i think we all recognize that 3d printing is a great opportunity, it drives innovation, it makes manufacturing more efficient, so it's good for business and good for consumers, and it's also very good for the environment. but it does come with risks, especially in terms of security, intellectual property protection and legal liability. i therefore believe that it is necessary to consider whether it is possible to find common rules at european level in order to prevent the easy and impunity of counterfeiting of works of art and other works.
mr president, i will continue where my colleague, ms šojdová, left off. indeed, intellectual property protection is a certain notoriety that we have in our civilized circle. this means that not only do rights holders have their rights, which should be really unquestionable, but users also have not only rights, but of course, obligations. the right to access those works, to the simple settlement of those rights, the ease of access, the clarity of that editing. the new technologies that are ahead of us are really a huge challenge. it is an opportunity to innovate, to find far more efficient solutions in manufacturing or in the provision of services. 3d printing has been proven to be many times more environmentally friendly, which are all aspects that we really have to fight against, so to speak, but we have to respect what's at the top. and that is to protect the rights of those who have them, that is to say the rights holders.
