mr president, i think it is very important that we address the situation of climate change and migration, and that is why i think this discussion made sense. i supported the part of the resolution that calls for action at the un level to provide a response to the migration of people who are expecting, who are leaving areas that are severely affected by climate change, and there is a need to respond in the places, in the regions where these changes are occurring, or to help and to address the situation there. i cannot agree with the challenge to change and redefine the term climate refugee in order to provide an international legal framework and protection. i don't think it's the solution to give the same international protection to refugees who leave climate change-affected areas as those who flee war and persecution.

rapporteur of the committee on employment and social affairs. mr president, i have observed with great regret for some time now that the debate on the mobility package has turned into a pure ideological battle, and unfortunately in the empl committee even more so than elsewhere. but few realize that there's more to the game than just drivers and haulers. populist solutions threaten the eu's founding principles, our common market and our common future. we will not prevent fraud in the international road transport market by applying the same rules that have been tailored for the construction industry. not only is this unfeasible in practice, but it will also cause enormous damage to the internal market and endanger transport services. the biggest problems in transportation, which relate to cabotage, nomadic drivers, letter-box companies and enforcement of rules, have been captured by the transportation committee. unlike the committee on employment and social affairs, the commission has managed to find a solution that ensures a sufficient balance. i therefore call on all to refrain from supporting politicization and non-compliance with pre-negotiated agreements. will you support the tran committee's qualified balanced proposal or will you support the building of more barriers? it's up to you.

mr. chairman, i very much appreciate the tran committee's compromise proposal and i urge you to support it. i consider it to be very balanced, both in terms of social protection for drivers and in terms of preserving the free movement of services. this compromise improves the social protection of drivers in many ways, for example, after a week's work drivers are entitled to rest outside the cabin and will only be able to spend it exceptionally in the cabin if the parking area is adequately equipped. it's not just about comfort, it's also about road safety. the transparency and enforceability of those new rules should be ensured by accelerating the mandatory use of smart tachographs from 2022. ladies and gentlemen, the exemption of the transport sector from the posting of workers directive is a fundamental change to prevent the creation of new administrative barriers. and if we are really concerned about building the free movement of services and workers, then we should support this compromise.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, let me thank you for the very good material and the very good document on which it is possible to have a fairly good debate. i think that the issue of the establishment and implementation of the budget of the european union is one of the most important issues that we have to solve in the structural reform of the european union. but there are still some budgetary problems. we are running surpluses, and the areas financed by the european union are changing, and we have to respond to this when we draw up the budget. i welcome the simplification and flexibility of the budget. i think that is the right way to go, but it is also very important that european funds are used effectively. the current set of rules, both on the drawdown of funding and on the ex post controls, needs to be revised. as a member of the budget control committee, i could tell you about it. in the long term, the under-utilisation of european funds is also a huge problem. the eu's helping hands are thus underused by the entities. this is due to the current complex and sometimes lengthy process of raising funds and administrative burdens. i therefore welcome the simplification of the rules for beneficiaries of eu funds. but simplified management must not lead to inefficiency. the rules must then be implemented responsibly and monitored and controlled to prevent circumvention. and if that happens, there must be severe sanctions. then we have to focus on the outcomes, whether there is really an improvement. my only concern is that simplification will lead to abuse.
in writing. it is strange, but the proposal deals with the problems of supplementing the relevant committees and commissions on the us side in a significant part of the text. the fact that the us side has somewhat underestimated the eu-us cooperation on privacy protection is clear from this. in practice, our partners across the grand lagoon have always taken unilateral action first and only then bilateral negotiations. the classic case is data on passengers in air transport between the eu and the us. another interesting fact is the lax approach of congress, which, among other things, rejected the federal commission's proposal on the protection of consumers of broadband and other telecommunications services. in the wake of the facebook data-sharing scandal involving up to 60 different companies, similar actions are being taken in the eu with little understanding. incidentally, facebook was found to have provided the personal data of more than 2 million eu citizens for commercial use. a number of servers used for, inter alia, broadband and other telecommunications services are located in the usa. the commission has also concluded that the us privacy shield is not a violation of the eu privacy shield. the conclusion is clear from the above, and i fully support the motion for a resolution.
madam president, commissioner, we often complain about the low turnout and the lack of interest in the elections. there are citizens who would love to come to the polls to express their opinion, but for various reasons they cannot. they're people with disabilities. there are 80 million disabled citizens in the european union, yet they are hardly represented in public life and are not given much thought in the programmes of political parties. however, it is not enough to ensure that polling stations are accessible to enable them to participate fully in the elections. having sufficient information is also essential for making a responsible choice. however, unfortunately, political party materials and programmes are often not available in formats that can be read by people with disabilities, which limits not only their right to vote but also their ability to participate actively in political life. the right to vote is as important for people with disabilities as for everyone else, and we should therefore work to ensure that they are not second-class citizens in the next european parliament elections, or any other.
madam president, commissioner, i am sure we can all agree that the right to vote and stand as a candidate for persons with disabilities is guaranteed by many international conventions, and article 29 of the un convention has been cited. we also no doubt agree, as the debate has shown, that the member states should first of all take measures to make it easier for all disabled people to exercise their political rights at a level that is, let us say, adequate to guarantee the accessibility of that right. this is mainly about the adjustment of the electoral procedures, it is about the availability of the voting room. and of course, the availability of materials related to the exercise of this right to vote in relation to national elections, but also to the european parliament elections. the key word is barrier-free. so we've already talked about voting booths that should be accessible to all people with disabilities, of course. the electoral procedures, including remote voting, should also be accessible and fully understandable to persons with disabilities. then there are measures concerning member states and political parties. the availability of pre-election materials in sign language, in those other tools that simply make it easier for these people to access these materials, is, i think, very important and has been mentioned here as well. election debates should also be interpreted in this way for people with disabilities. there was already talk of equal quotas, and i would like to say that too. i am not in favour of quotas, but it is clear that member states should encourage political parties to nominate candidates in their territory to include women candidates in their list of candidates.

for the group gue/ngl mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i consider the work on the draft copyright directive to be one of the most difficult and demanding of my entire career in the european parliament. there are too many conflicting interests and positions of different groups to find an acceptable compromise. i think we can all agree that copyright needs to be modernized to fit the current digital age. but what form it takes is another matter. the controversy of the subject is also evidenced by the fact that 166 amendments were submitted, not to mention the hundreds of e-mails we receive every day. but unlike the reporter, i don't think this is just a stinging campaign. we want good legislation, but we will not back down. i would like to take this opportunity to thank all the colleagues who have contributed to this difficult task. i think we've done a good job in some areas of the juri committee. however, our group still has a number of provisions in the draft directive which we cannot support. i hope you will support our amendments to article 4, which we want to ensure that even today educational institutions will be able to use digital materials, sample songs, to teach without having to sign a contract. i personally find the current wording of article 13 totally unacceptable. i know all the political factions are divided on this point, but i believe we are capable of reaching a compromise.
mr president, article 11 will cause the services we have been used to with search engines such as google and some others to be restricted. the citizens of the european union will be harmed. article 13 will cause our files, what our kids create, and what we create and put on the internet, to be censored by robots. robots will decide our freedoms, our property, things that normally only a court can decide. and it shows that the european union is not about people at all, it's not about what the citizens of the european states need. the european union wants to concentrate more and more power in brussels, and that's why we need to replace the european union with some alternative.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the european commission, a functioning copyright system is a prerequisite for ensuring the competitiveness of european creators on a global scale and for the distinctive character of european culture. but only copyright that reflects a properly defined public interest. and that public interest cannot be reduced to the interest of one particular group of future users of those rules. the public interest must be the result of a careful consideration of the requirements of all those who attach legitimate expectations to the existence of copyright, both authors and consumers. it is a primary consideration for my decision-making that the resulting text actually reflects the public interest and cannot be challenged for incompatibility with the charter of fundamental rights and freedoms, where intellectual property is listed as part of protected property. and i would also like to see the word censorship not used in this discussion to ensure the copyright purity of the use of copyrighted works. for those of us who have lived for decades in regimes of actual censorship, where content was excluded for its content and not because of the technical imperfections of existing instruments, it's a hard to swallow bite.
for the ppe group. mr. president, ms. mogherini, ladies and gentlemen, we are all following with great concern the escalating situation in libya. libyans deserve to live in peace and stability. this vision is slipping away from them, and we in the eu must make the most of all the tools available to help change this. while i have great respect for the work of the united nations in stabilizing the country, i personally believe that at this time, right now, there can be no insistence on a december election date. the political, institutional and organisational infrastructure is still lacking, but in particular the security conditions. i fear that elections under these circumstances would only lead to further escalation of violence. at the same time, libya needs a new impetus to resolve the crisis with stability and political transformation, a realistic plan. the libyans need our continued support, both political and humanitarian. and i would also like to thank you very much, mrs mogherini, for opening the eu's representation in tripoli itself, not in the distant tunisia.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, cash remains a predominant source of financing, including for terrorists and other criminal organisations in europe. the current rules on cash control need to be updated to address the new challenges. recent events have shown that terrorists have managed to find ways to circumvent the current rules on cash control. in particular, public authorities have noted that criminals have resorted to sending or receiving cash via post, or in a parcel, in order to escape the obligation to make a declaration under the current rules. moreover, french customs authorities found non-declared cash and gold worth eur 9.2 million in postal parcels and freight packages during an investigation at charles de gaulle airport in 2015. national authorities have also seen that certain precious high-value commodities, such as gold, are now being used to escape the obligation to declare, since gold is not considered as cash under existing rules. i believe this new regulation will target these loopholes by imposing a simple rule. if you plan to enter or leave the eu with eur 10 000 or more in cash, you must declare it to customs. as for cash sent by freight or post, the disclosure will have to be made. i was among those who believe that no differentiation should be made between accompanied and non-accompanied cash, and i was thus calling for a mandatory declaration for cash sent by freight or post. unfortunately, the commission was not convinced by this approach, nor were the other political groups. however, we all agreed to introduce this possibility in a review clause, and i hope the commission will conduct these assessments thoroughly and update us on the outcome. overall, i am satisfied with the outcome of the negotiations with the council. i encourage members to support this text and member states to implement these new rules quickly.
it is a pity that we cannot issue green cards in this house, otherwise i would have immediately joined my colleague in explaining that he will be able to continue to transfer money to the uk, but he will have to report it, fill in a simple form which may solve the problem that at present is that money is being transferred across borders in cash without any controls and is used to finance various activities linked to terrorism or illegal activity. yes, mr president, perhaps you should sit down with the victims of terrorist acts and explain to them how the terrorists got their hands on weapons or how they got their hands on explosives and how they paid for them. we are talking about the single market here, we are talking about the need to really fight against the different forms. it's not just money, it's been mentioned here, gold, but we can talk about other ways of financing today. this directive must not be stopped. i thank the reporters for their excellent work, but we also need to start looking at the issue of cryptocurrencies and the way in which the activities of various terrorist groups are financed.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, it was perhaps a rare situation when the commissioners played a power play, to use an ice hockey expression. but, more importantly, i believe that the text we agreed will complement another important directive we are working on, the fifth anti—money laundering directive, by introducing a strong and efficient criminal law instrument to tackle money laundering. the new rules, combined with other legislative instruments, will contribute to a cutting of the financial sources of criminals. the amount of money generated from criminal activity in the main illicit markets in the eu has been estimated at eur 110 billion per year, corresponding to 1% of eu gdp. for criminals to benefit fully from the proceeds of their crimes, these criminal proceeds need to be laundered and the number of money laundering cases in the eu has been growing, according to europol. in my capacity as chair of the special committee on financial crimes, tax evasion and tax avoidance, i can only recall here how important it is to agree on common minimal rules on the way member states criminalise money laundering. the investigation we conducted in parliament’s previous committee of inquiry on money laundering, tax avoidance and tax evasion clearly shows that differences in national legislation on money laundering are a major obstacle to cross-border cooperation and exchange of information. by harmonising the definition of what constitutes a criminal offence, we facilitate the possibility for financial intelligence units to exchange information regarding criminal investigation of money laundering or terrorist financing. i believe we did it right and i am convinced that the trilogue outcome will gain overwhelming support in the house.

madam president, i would like to turn our attention to the second part of the joint debate, namely the question of how to deal with the serious problems at the frontier of legislation on chemicals, products and waste. in january this year, the commission published a communication addressing the possibilities of addressing the issues at the very edge of the legislation on these chemicals. the communication aims to promote a broad debate within the union and to find ways of addressing the main problems identified. we're dealing with four critical issues. insufficient information on substances of concern present in products and waste. secondly, the presence of substances of concern in recycled materials. third, uncertainty about how safely to handle these materials and whether they can be disposed of. fourth, the difficulties in applying the methodology. the commission's conclusions are therefore four problems. we should address these problems so that we don't have barriers in the circular economy. the ambition must be to achieve full compliance with the legislation. so i have the following two questions for the council. what is the council's position on these issues and on the possible measures proposed by the commission to address the four most important problems? more specifically, how does the council intend to contribute to further harmonising the interpretation and application of the provisions on waste by the member states?
in writing. it has become a common practice for key documents on recycling and material flows to stress the need to move from a linear to a circular economy. given that the basic materials of different plastic products are different hydrocarbons, it is not easy, especially for retail packaging materials, to ensure the chemical composition classification which is an important precondition for recycling. logically, the first priority is to prevent plastic waste, the second priority is to reuse, and the third is to recycle. this naturally requires the use of appropriate collection and sorting technology. today, only 30% of plastic waste is collected for recycling in the eu. 31% of plastic is landfilled and 39% is incinerated. since eu-wide rules against landfilling of plastics will be in place by 2030, there is a need to increase the possibilities for re-use and recyclability of packaging. the commission is invited to introduce a ban on microplastics in a range of products by 2020, while setting requirements to ensure that they are released from a wide range of materials. the horizon 2020 programme is to allocate eur 100 million for research into sorting, recycling and the use of plastic resources. i therefore strongly support the above activities mentioned in the report, as well as the inclusion of the 'ocean free of plastics' programme.
mr president, i am glad that we are also dealing with this problem, because it is estimated that passenger transport will increase by about 40% over the next 40 years and passenger transport by 60%. high number of fatalities on european roads: for example, 25 000 people killed per year. it's important to talk about emissions that are the result of transportation, air pollution in cities, which is the source of more than 25 percent of greenhouse gas emissions. i'm not going to go into the other effects, but i'd like to mention the positives. transport accounts for 4% of gdp and more than 5% of total employment in the eu. we should follow the principle that whoever is the user and polluter pays. we should promote sustainable urban and rural mobility plans. we should encourage the introduction of a multimodal passenger transport system, improve mobility and the quality of services for citizens. it is also important to raise citizens' awareness of the environmental impacts of different modes of transport. the development of low-carbon transport services should be our priority. we should also provide citizens with sufficient information to find an alternative to road transport, especially autonomous transport. another measure is also bicycle sharing in towns and villages.
the rapporteur. - mr president, we have on the table the report on the implementation of the plant protection products regulation. i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, the european commission, and in particular the european parliament's research service. it was a brilliant job, we've been working on this thing for over six months. we used to say that the european pesticide approval system was the most rigorous in the world. yes, that's right, the system does. but when we first look at how it's been put into practice, we find that unfortunately, this system is marred by disrespect, by circumvention, by abuse, by a lack of transparency, both at our level, at the eu level, and at the member state level. unfortunately, pesticide regulations are not fully implemented and the precautionary principle is often ignored, and the current situation could easily endanger the health of europeans as well as species diversity, ecosystems or soil. just a few facts that this research has shown us. there are a number of products on the union market that have not been reviewed for more than 15 years. the european commission is taking some very substantial steps in this direction and i am confident that its work will be successful. emergency authorisations, which are strictly to be used only in cases where plant production is threatened, are regularly abused by member states to circumvent the traditional authorisation process with potentially far-reaching health consequences. the case of neonicotinoids, which we have temporarily banned at eu level for the health of bees, and the ban in many countries has not been enforced at all, because that is how they were authorised, speaks for all of us. at our european level, the confirmatory procedure is sometimes abused, and unfortunately we have products on the market for which research is not yet completed, for which we do not know exactly what the consequences are, for which there is not enough data. among other things, this has resulted in the availability of many herbicides today that contain the same active ingredient as the infamous agent orange. for many pesticides, we do not know the long-term impacts on biodiversity, and we have a complete lack of assessment of the impact on soil, soil macro-organisms and soil micro-organisms. we can't deal with the cocktail effect, we can't deal with the long-term exposure of organisms to these substances and these consequences. but even if everything goes perfectly, at the end of the process, we have a standing committee on plants, animals, food and feed, which is more or less anonymous and doesn't have to be guided by anything, in principle, or by science. this report is an implementation report, nothing more, nothing less. we tried to stick to the conclusions of the research study, and i'm a little sorry for some of the somewhat emotional and unfortunately false amendments. an example of all this is the amendment of the european people's party, which has been unmistakably supported by other right-wing groups, which says that the number of pesticides available on the european market is decreasing. that's not true, the european commission is doing a really good job of that, and according to the european commission's public database, there are twice as many drugs approved in europe today as there were in 2009. this report is very timely, and its conclusions, research results and proposals can be taken up by the pest special committee, which is to propose improvements to our european pesticide authorisation system. i also used its results to work on the draft regulation on the transparency and sustainability of risk assessment in the food chain. unfortunately, this parliament does not have a legislative initiative of its own and i hope that the european commission will help us to propose the necessary changes to the legislation. some of the shortcomings identified are already being addressed by the european commission's proposals, others are yet to be addressed. in any event, this report will help in any further steps planned to improve this legislation.
rapporteur. – mr president, i would like to thank colleagues for a very important discussion. apparently a majority of this house are aware that there are problems in the eu system for pesticide approval and the implementation of this system, either at eu or member state level. i am grateful to the commissioner for saying that this means something to the commission. i look forward to the next time that we cooperate on this issue. but there is one last thing that i would say to ms mcguinness. i am sure that you adore science and you adore facts, so you simply cannot say that the number of available active substances is getting lower. i have a table of commission statistics here. it says, very simply, that, in 2009, 224 active substances were approved, and in 2018, 490 active substances were approved at european level. so the number of available active substances on the european market is not going down. that is simply not true. i would like you to give us the possibility to vote in a split vote against this fake fact.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it has been seven long years since the first interpellation by myself and several other colleagues, in response to the first signals of double standards. seven years since the european commission responded that different countries have different taste and price preferences, that the food is fine, so what? ladies and gentlemen, this is nothing less than the very essence of the functioning of the internal market. the previous parliament in 2013 already called on the commission to address the issue, to carry out a europe-wide survey and to assess the effectiveness of existing legislation. but it is only after five more years of persuasion and proof, of various surveys, that we have a report of double quality and a clear position of the european parliament. i would like to thank all the colleagues who have worked over the years to ensure that this highly political and symbolic theme, which speaks of the equality, fairness and equity of the european internal market, is taken seriously in the european union. i thank all those who have helped to explain here, in the commission and in the council, the essence of the matter: that it is really not normal for a product which appears to the consumer to be the same at first sight to have a different composition without the consumer also being aware of it at first sight. it's not a battle between lavender and purple, but a battle between less meat or meat substitutes, a battle between raw materials of lower quality, different quality and ratio, more additives and so on. what we want is for consumers to be free from deception and to have the right to products of the same quality. what's weird about the requirement: mark the same for the same and the different for the different. in short, not to be a consumer, forgive the expression, but a fool. many people in our countries have personal experience of dual quality, not only of food but also of non-food products. that it's a matter of taste? even with baby food for infants? but go. nor is the direct price-quality ratio applicable here. i am glad that the european commission has also been shaken up by juncker's state of the union speech last year. we have interpretative recommendations on the table, a common testing methodology, money to collect further evidence. more tests in the member states are planned. but be careful. we already have hard data, the results of tests in the laboratories of renowned universities, as in the case of the czech study in which i myself participated. it is certainly good to collect data at union level. but that's not enough after all these years of consumer frustration. especially when some countries where the double standard has been revealed refuse to participate in a pan-european survey. ladies and gentlemen, many of you will agree with me that it is not the job of politicians to tell manufacturers what to produce and how to produce it. the aim is not to unify products, to invent some kind of uniform taste for the euro. however, consumer preference must no longer be used as a pretext for lowering quality. most consumers are unaware that products of the same brand can be adapted to different markets. if a manufacturer wants to offer a regional adaptation to a customer, what's to stop them from proudly endorsing it on the packaging? i want accurate and at first glance easy to understand information about the composition of the product. that's the basics. member states must also introduce a regulation on the cooperation of national consumer protection supervisory authorities. they must share data, point out to each other non-compliant products and possible consumer misrepresentation. the role of consumer organisations is also important. i have great respect for their daily, ant-like work in monitoring the market and educating consumers. but we can't expect miracles from them. the most important thing from my point of view: the lack of legislation at eu level. a provision which clearly defines what constitutes dual quality without any possibility of different interpretation. that the sale on the internal market of products which can easily be perceived by the consumer as being identical with a deliberately different composition is misleading to the consumer. the regulation will also provide legal certainty to the supervisory authorities, who will not be afraid of costly legal battles with multinational companies. not so that your favorite drink tastes the same everywhere, but so that dishonest people feel the real possibility of punishment if they do not act fairly. ladies and gentlemen, i believe in the honesty of most manufacturers. yet, where they are lacking, there are lawmakers to stop their citizens, no matter where they live in europe. today's vote is a chance to show that we stand for a fair european union, where there are no products and no second-class citizens.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'double quality' in the definition of the term 'double quality'. but it is also an important issue in terms of how our internal market works. there can be no second-class consumers in the eu internal market. consumers who are destined for lower quality products. indeed, all consumers without distinction expect to buy identical goods in the union under the same brand or packaging. and they are rightly surprised to find that it is not. we need joint and decisive action at european level, and that is why i am glad that the european commission has already presented a legislative proposal. but we should also take advantage of the possibilities of existing regulations today and put pressure on manufacturers and traders, as well as on the supervisory authorities. they need to be provided with the right conditions for their work and cross-border cooperation between them needs to be significantly intensified. i also consider it essential to strengthen the role of consumer organisations. these can help people when they have doubts about the quality of a product. but the key for me is action. time is running out, and companies should show good will and start taking concrete action. we've been talking about this for a long time, and i just hope we can move from words to action.
for the group gue/ngl mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that the dual quality of food is a particular concern for consumers in the new eu member states. it is demonstrated that you will get products on the market with the same brand and the same packaging as those sold in, for example, germany, but their quality, composition and the recipe used are completely different. so the consumer is essentially being cheated. to use the excuse that czech consumers have different tastes or price preferences than german consumers is ridiculous. and we're not just talking about food here. unfortunately, the same is true of cleaning or hygiene products. i'm glad because, thanks to the tireless efforts of my colleague, sehnall, there's finally something that can be done about this problem. he's been pointing out the dual quality of the products since 2011, as far as i know. i welcome the commission's legislative initiative in this respect, which has perhaps finally understood that we are facing a real problem, not an excuse. it is certainly right to revise the unfair practices directive, but i am in favour of putting discriminatory practices against consumers as an additional item on the so-called black list of prohibited practices. i have signed the amendment tabled by mrs sehnall in this spirit and i appeal to all colleagues who want to do something for consumers in the eu to support this proposal. i hope very much that the promises will not remain on paper, because we do not want to have a double standard of consumer quality in europe.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we all understand that the same packaging must contain the same product of the same quality, and it is surprising that it has taken so many years to persuade the commission to help enforce this rule, this justice. i am pleased that it is on the floor of the european parliament that a number of initiatives have been launched and that the commission has heeded the call for clearer rules. we are proud that the czech republic has played an important role in this process. i would also like to thank my colleague, mrs olz sehnall, who has been working very systematically on this subject for many years. it was also the ministry of agriculture of the czech republic that pushed this solution at the level of the council of ministers. the methodology developed by the european commission can help member states assess whether a product is in breach of the rules, and we should know the first results as soon as possible. i am of course also expecting a positive outcome in the directive we are discussing. thank you again to my colleagues for this report.

madam president, yesterday was september 30th, and we in the czech republic commemorated 80 years since the young czechoslovak republic experienced great shock, humiliation and betrayal by its allies. it was decided in munich whether we were going to be here or not. chamberlain, daladier and mussolini believed hitler that a concession would prevent war. and he didn't. today we can commemorate this sad date to know that peace and freedom were bought with millions of sacrifices. we must remind the younger generation of this date so that they understand why we are so opposed to the european union as a community where decisions are not made for us and without us. it's important not to forget.
madam president, i would like to comment on a subject which is of particular importance to me, namely the weakening of cohesion policy, particularly in the context of the preparation of the new multiannual financial framework. this is a sensitive issue indeed, and apart from the fact that cohesion policy in general is weakened by cuts in the budgetary share, new conditions are also to be introduced to limit or condition the use of this money, which is to be used to eliminate and balance disparities between regions. so there are going to be conditions that are really going to be politically sensitive, i have to mention the reception of refugees. i would like to say that i agree with some of the conditions, but for some, such as the one already mentioned, i consider this view to be a road to hell indeed. if we want to preserve the role of cohesion policy as the most dominant instrument of european policies, it is impossible to accept conditions that make the drawing of these funds a highly political issue, which i think does not really belong in the new framework, and i would very much like to see us change this together.
madam chairman, commissioner, i would like to draw your attention to the case of an ordinary man. it's the case of george sagan, who was recently convicted in france for allegedly smuggling 15 iraqis. a man who's been driving a truck for 15 years, who's never had a problem with the police in his life. he is a law-abiding citizen of the czech republic living in spain with his wife, who is seriously ill. the french authorities gave him 18 months hard labor for alleged human trafficking. these people confirmed that they got into his truck without him knowing. this is the rule of law? this is the commissioner, the european union, that i am supposed to fight for and explain to people? do you really think 18 months for a man who did nothing is fair and justifiable? i ask you to address this matter within the commission and help emmanuel macron understand what law and justice are.
madam president, i also share the view of many colleagues that it is high time to unblock the council on migration. leadership of the presidency here is needed, and many of the members of the european council are probably happy to have migration still on the agenda because it is a way to bash the eu all the time, and in fact the czech prime minister says there is no other issue vis-à-vis the eu than migration. but i am afraid migration is not the only issue, though it looks like that’s the only problem. if we look at what our public says, 70 to 80% feel that the eu should have a higher role in trade policy. 70 to 80% want a european defence policy. roughly the same number says that there are challenges in the economic area where europe has a role to play. my question is, when are we going to close the gap on a number of those issues? when are we going to be delivering on those issues? so migration, yes by all means, and let’s finally settle it. however, we finally need to address these issues as well. one sentence on brexit: i’m all in favour of unity, and it’s great that we have it, but in terms of, for example, aviation safety, if easa does not start technical talks with the caa, we will be in a mess from next year on. that is my message to the commission.
madam minister, mr vice-president timmermans, the european council has a key responsibility to come up with solutions to the current problems, and those are still, without a doubt, migration and security. colleagues, you are also calling for solutions to other important issues, such as social and environmental issues, these are important issues, but without restoring citizens' confidence in this crucial issue of security and migration, we will not move forward. in salzburg, the council of ministers made progress. strengthening the european border and coast guard is undoubtedly an important step, in particular in terms of increasing human and material capacities. it is also important that we make progress on the issue of establishing hotspots outside europe. ineffective efforts to combat illegal migration and the influx of economic migrants make it politically impossible to help those who really need it. people who are vulnerable or, for example, children who are victims of war. i speak from experience, because for several weeks now i have been trying to persuade the czech government to accept voluntarily, i emphasize voluntarily, 50 unaccompanied children from greek camps who are in dire need of protection, care and education. even this symbolic proposal is met with distrust by populist forces that exploit the fear of illegal immigration that we have unfortunately experienced in recent years. i want to assure you that the czech republic is solidary, contributing financially to the aid fund and to specific humanitarian projects. people in the czech republic want to help those who really need help.

the debate is closed. i have a short announcement. when i chair the plenary, at least, i will not be giving space during the catch—the—eye procedure to anyone who has not been present for the debate. i think that colleagues know who i am talking about. i will make an exception today, but for me a debate is a debate and that means one has to be present. catch—the—eye procedure
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank honourable members for this very fruitful debate and for their contribution in this really challenging field. i would like to reply and make some remarks on some of the observations. first of all, let me underline what i said in my opening remarks. the european union is not opposed, as a matter of principle, to discussing a potential legally—binding instrument, and this is why we have engaged in the process led by ecuador. the second point, also on your observations. we nonetheless insist that the scope of the discussions should include all companies, not only transnational or multinational, as many human rights violations are committed by domestic companies. some of you raised this issue. my third point is about the european union’s position on this specific legally—binding instrument. the european union is a staunch supporter of the united nations in a rules-based global order and we expect all eu member states to comply with these obligations and implement them strictly. full implementation of these existing human rights obligations would help to respond to the numerous cases documented by civil society organisations and human rights defenders. we believe that any possible further steps regarding the international legal framework for business and human rights at un level must be inclusive, firmly rooted in the un guiding principles, and address all types of companies whether international or domestic, as i have already said. in the world of today, it is only within a multilateral framework that we can ensure that all the victims of human rights abuses resulting from companies’ actions receive the same level of protection. the european union has a strong track record in agreeing with and implementing the un guiding principles. we need to acknowledge progress also by a number of european companies to implement these principles, and expect others to follow. however, we should continue to explore options for filling the gaps in their implementation, for increasing respect of human rights by private operators and ensuring access to remedies both multilaterally and bilaterally. we should also see how we can best promote a global level playing field in line with international standards in this area. let me add that in the current challenging global context multilateral cooperation is more important than ever and it is essential that the european union and the un speak with one voice when it comes to upholding democratic values and the universality of human rights. this is the challenge.
commissioner, thank you very much also for responding to concrete elements that have been raised by colleagues.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, as already mentioned by the commissioner, the european union is not opposed to discussing a potentially legally binding international instrument on business and human rights. i think i am also repeating what you have already said when i say that any possible further steps regarding the international legal framework for business and human rights at un level must be inclusive, firmly rooted in the un guiding principles, and address – as you have already mentioned – all types of companies, transnational as well as domestic. let me also reassure you that the eu will continue to cooperate with all stakeholders globally on initiatives to ensure implementation of the un guiding principles on business and human rights, including through national action plans, and to promote accountability for human rights violations and the need for an effective remedy for all victims of human rights violations and abuses. with regard to your question on the formal mandate for negotiations, the council will take a formal decision on the opening of negotiations if, and when, it receives a relevant recommendation in accordance with article 218. to sum up, thank you for your fruitful statements. this has been a very useful debate, which demonstrates the high degree of convergence between the views of parliament and of the council on the importance of ensuring an effective level of protection for victims of human rights abuses resulting from companies’ actions, and of improving their access to effective remedies. this much—needed progress must, in the first instance, be achieved through clear commitments from all states to implement a duty to protect human rights and to abide by their existing international obligations. thank you again for your fruitful statements and for your attention.

the next item is the debate on the statement by the vice—president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy on eu support to unrwa, following the us withdrawal of financial support to unrwa (2018/2852(rsp)).
mr. chairman, i consider the israeli-palestinian conflict to be one of the most difficult conflicts in modern history. here in the european parliament a few years ago, the israeli president said that european reconciliation is an inspiration for israelis and palestinians. i believe that there will be peace there, and that there will be reconciliation. to do this, they need the help of other actors, including unrwa, which has an indispensable role to play in the resolution of this conflict, particularly in terms of humanitarian aid. the united states has decided to stop funding because of inefficiency and i ask the european commission to be very rigorous, to strictly control what it is used for, if it increases the contribution. i agree with my colleague, mrs škripka, that it is unacceptable that these funds should be used for unilateral propaganda.
mr president, commissioner, as has already been said, the ruthless civil war in yemen is now in its third year, and it is really a conflict between saudi arabia, supported by the west, and iran on the other side. it is the bloodiest conflict in the world today, next to syria, with tragic humanitarian consequences. it's kind of a side of the world's mainstream, maybe because the world's great powers are playing their games here, including the u.s., the u.k., and france. unfortunately, we really don't see any really effective political activity here from the un or the european union. the only thing we can and must do at this moment is to save lives, and i thank the commissioner for bringing this up. we put out fires that others start. the eu is actually becoming the largest humanitarian organization. of course, it's good that we're helping, but there's a lack of prevention and rescuing is not enough.
in writing. yemen was created in 1990, after years of bloody conflict, by the merger of traditional mountain resorts around the capital sanaa in the north and the people's democratic republic of yemen, the former british colony of aden. not only two different parts of the country, but also different interpretations of the muslim religion, sunni and shiite, have long divided this country. political instability culminated several months ago with the capture of the capital by the militant shiite organization al-houthi and the flight of the president to aden. sunni forces have received massive military support from the saudi arabian military, and the saudi military is slowly but surely advancing in its heavy bombardment of a number of rebel-held areas. the world public is watching with concern the heavy fighting in the port of al-hudaydah, where humanitarian aid has been pouring into the northern part of the country. of course, there's a wider context to this whole thing. the shiites have the backing of iran, the sunnis of saudi arabia, backed by a broader coalition of arab countries. somewhat unnoticed by the world public, which has focused on the lack of food and medical care, the main reason for the rivalry over yemen for years has been the bab-al-mandab strait, a strait that connects the red sea to the indian ocean. whatever the outcome of the conflict, millions of people in this country will once again be poorer and more economically dependent on foreign aid. what yemen needs now is immediate help to stop the fighting.
mr president, the automotive sector is undoubtedly the driving force behind european industry. that is why i am very concerned about the objectives proposed, which are very aggressive in their ambitions to enter the car market. they leave the customer's decision aside and dictate to the manufacturers how much they have to sell under sanctions and what the product should look like. they set beautiful goals, but they don't think about how to make sure that there's enough electricity to meet the needs of these cars. at the same time, there is no major technological change in the storage of sufficient electricity, including really fast charging. the solution is not the violent political gestures that we have had in our negative historical experience. it would be more sensible to give a huge boost to research and development in this area. the only thing that can bring about a fundamental change are new technologies and innovations in transport. all the revolutionary technological inventions we use today have been gradually promoted on the free market. they became successful in the marketplace when not only the customers but also the whole society was ready for them. if the european union wants to skip all this, it means that we are going the wrong way in a politically driven market, against free competition in an open competitive environment.
mr president, commissioner, it is clear that the european union has a vital role to play in reducing overall emissions and combating climate change. but we can't be reckless in this fight, throwing a baby out with a bathtub. our objectives for the automotive industry, which employs 13 million europeans, must be realistic and achievable. we must not allow this traditional sector to lose its competitiveness in global markets. let us encourage innovation, let us green-light all new technologies that use non-fossil fuels, but let us not be overly demanding and let us not concentrate our efforts in one direction only. a direction that could make the car manufacturers scapegoats, albeit for the sake of certain noble goals.
colleagues, i need to ask you all to respect your time slots. we are running behind and will have to catch up, and we also have the catch—the—eye procedure, so please respect the time that has been allocated to you.
member of the commission. – mr president, i have listened carefully to the debate today and i have heard a broad range of positions, in particular on the level of ambition, and that is also the situation in the debates that have taken place in the council. the transition to zero-emission mobility needs to happen during the next decades. however, this transition should happen at the right pace to allow sufficient time for the re-skilling of workers in the supply chain. and a smooth transition is particularly important for the many small and medium-sized enterprises operating in this sector, which are the backbone of the economy in many regions. some members addressed the issue of the introduction of a malus in the zero and low—emission vehicles incentive mechanism, in particular mr liese and ms ayuso. the commission proposal contains a new mechanism incentivising manufacturers to put more zero and low—emission vehicles on the european union market. it leaves manufacturers the choice as to how they compose their fleet but provides this benefit for those choosing to go for zero and low—emission vehicles. adding a malus would mean that manufacturers meeting their co2 targets could actually be penalised if they do not meet the benchmark, and analyses also show that the economic impacts depend on the combination of the target level and a possible bonus/malus benchmark for zero-emission or low-emission vehicles. with the increased penetration of zero and low—emission vehicles, driven by the high bonus/malus benchmark level, less effort will be needed in improving the efficiency of conventional vehicles to meet the fleet-wide co2 targets. and this results in a projected shift towards larger segments for conventional vehicles, leading to an increase in the net economic costs for consumers. some members commented on the non-paper on assessment of higher ambition levels, produced by the commission, and my friend bas eickhout actually said it was a political document. well, i would point out that the commission services have not manipulated any calculations and are not misleading the co—legislator: the non—paper clearly and objectively presents the results of recent analytical work and it aims to inform the ongoing decision-making process on the impact of options not considered in the impact assessments. the non—paper’s analysis is therefore only factual. it is fully transparent and based on the same methodological approach: as in the impact assessments, we provided all the necessary underlying background. in particular, the non—paper confirms that the only impact on jobs up to 2030 is positive for more ambitious scenarios. however, it also points to the fact that the transition towards zero-emission mobility will lead to differences between sectors. while overall employment increases with the level of ambition, existing jobs in the automotive sector related to the combustion engine risk being lost if the transition is too fast. the non-paper also confirms the key role of plug-in hybrid electric vehicles in this respect, allowing a transition to zero-emission vehicles while limiting automotive job impacts. in conclusion, i want to thank all of you for the very interesting debate tonight and i am looking forward to tomorrow’s vote. i am also confident that the council and the leadership of the austrian presidency will be able to reach an agreement on a general approach at the environment council next week and that a final positive political agreement among the co—legislators can be found before the end of the year.
thank you commissioner. we are all looking forward to the vote tomorrow. madam rapporteur, ms dalli, your closing remarks.
