the next item is the joint debate on:– the report by jeppe kofod, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, on harmonising and simplifying certain rules in the vat system (com(2017)0569 – c8-0363/2018 – 2017/0251(cns)) (a8-0280/2018); and- the report by tibor szanyi, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, on rates of value added tax (com)(2018)0020 – c8-0023/2018 – 2018/0005 (cns)) (a8-0279/2018).
mr langen, it is my understanding that ecofin today adopted a decision on the reverse charge system. i would have to double check, but i understand there has been a development in that respect.
colleague, i apologise. seriously, i do understand, but we all have hugely important points to make, but we would still be sitting here at midnight if we were to address them all. my apologies. i am sure this is relevant but i had to take the floor away from you.
mr president, the reason for the rise of neo-fascist violence in europe is the success of far-right parties in elections and, of course, the various forms of neo-nazi and neo-fascist propaganda. an example is the surge of commemorative events in croatia celebrating the ustasha as heroes, which a recent council of europe report has pointed out and also warned against. the far-right violence in chemnitz, germany, i think we all remember enough. despite these incidents, the strength of far-right parties is not, in my view, sufficient at the moment to undermine the democratic foundations on which the european union is based. but unless democratic parties find a way to effectively combat them in political competition, this situation can easily actually turn for the worse. i would also like to point out in this connection that the danger to european democracy is not only from the far right but also from the far left. i consider left-wing extremism to be no less destructive than right-wing extremism. and in europe there are still a number of communist parties that are orthodox adherents of marxism-leninism, an ideology that has a comparable number of human lives on its hands. every democracy is inherently fragile and vulnerable, and we must take care of our european democracy so that it does not slip through our fingers. in practice, this means choosing the right combination of preventive and repressive measures against all forms of extremism: both right-wing and left-wing. if you've read this correctly, i'm also for retaliatory measures. the attack on european democracy can come from both sides.
mr president, electoral law is fundamentally linked to the rule of law and the exercise of political rights, as well as to the free movement of citizens of the european union. it is astonishing to me that in the 21st century some member states deny the right to vote in national elections to citizens living abroad. after all, the right to vote is one of the basic rights of citizenship, and citizens of the same state should be equal in their rights. when the state unjustifiably denies citizens the right to vote in certain situations, it effectively creates second-class citizenship. was the citizen guilty of having worked, studied or resided in another country? that does not mean that he is indifferent to the fate of his country and that he should not have a say in its affairs. the commission should therefore take greater account of the 2014 commission recommendation and allow at least those citizens living abroad who request to participate in national elections. but in the institutions of the european union, we should be concerned primarily with the european elections. i consider it unfortunate that some member states - and the czech republic is one of them - do not allow their citizens living abroad to vote for their national candidates in european elections. i very much support the current bill that has been introduced in the czech republic to introduce postal voting. in the european elections, the law would allow czech citizens to vote for czech candidates from abroad for the first time. i consider it fair in terms of equal treatment of citizens living abroad. and i believe that would increase the turnout. this new possibility will of course also create the need to avoid double voting. but i believe that good cooperation and exchange of experience with other member states, as well as the recently approved amendment to the electoral code, will help us in this.
the situation in romania is alarming. the government's policy of tolerating corruption of officials is unacceptable. the government should listen to its citizens. if he's not serious about a 100,000 demonstration, then this debate in the ep is necessary. we are here to represent the voice of romanian citizens, who are not being listened to by the government. i fully support the commission's efforts to monitor the situation in romania through the cooperation and verification mechanism. but i am not calling for financial sanctions against romania in the form of a stop to payments from eu funds, because that would be the most harmful to the citizens.
mr president, mr prime minister, we are talking about the future of the european union, and you know very well that the european union is called in russian "европейский союз". that sounds very much like the soviet union, and i'd like to limit myself to one legal analogy. you know very well that all the communist states had in their constitutions a clause that spoke of the leading role of the communist party, according to which there could be no legal opposition, according to which there could be no fair competition between political parties. and we have a similar provision in the lisbon treaty. article 17 (3) says that the members of the commission are chosen on the basis of the requirements of europeanism, that is, there can never be a fair competition between political parties in the european union. this means that the opposition, which thinks only a little differently from the mainstream, will always be discriminated against in the european union. and i would like to ask you: this example that i have just cited is one of the dozens of examples of the democratic deficit in the european union. a terrible democratic deficit. i want to ask you to make estonia do our part to eliminate the democratic deficit from the european continent immediately, consistently and definitively.
mr. chairman, i supported this motion, which was approved. i am in favour of all harmonization and simplification procedures in the field of indirect taxation in general, and i would like to emphasize that. it is important to make the harmonization measures so that they do not fall on european companies, that they do not weaken their competitiveness, and i think that this proposal has succeeded in doing so. i am distinguishing this proposal from direct taxes, where i believe there is again considerable interference by the member states. i do not support excessive activism in direct taxes by the european parliament or the institutions in general. but i would like to point out that vat will change, it seems, several more times until 2022. yesterday, another amendment to the vat amendment was approved, following a czech proposal, and it is called reverse charge. the czech prime minister calls this change a bombshell. on the contrary, i think that the administrative burden on taxpayers will be very unpleasant and i would like to invite the member states to really think about it, because constant changes in the field of indirect taxation are certainly not indicative of good practice in the field of harmonisation.
mr. chairman, i would like to explain why i did not vote in the final vote to change the emission standards for new passenger cars. reducing co2 emissions is important in the fight against climate change and the european union is a leader in this. the commission has proposed to amend the emission standard to reduce co2 emissions by 30% by 2030. i think it was a wise decision. i was willing to vote for a 35% compromise, and that's already a very ambitious plan. however, despite the fact that many of us in the epp voted against the amendment, parliament finally approved the 40% emission reduction commitment, and that is already an unacceptable ambition for me, counterproductive, because the european car industry will be less competitive, we will clear the space for chinese competition. i hope that this decision will be corrected in the negotiations with the member states.
mr president, i could not support this proposal, i considered the original proposal from the european commission to reduce emissions from new cars and vans by 30% by 2030 compared to 2021 to be realistic, and i considered the more restrictive proposal of 35% to be reasonable and acceptable. however, i would no longer set myself ambitious goals that i would back down from anyway, and this is i think one of those goals that was approved. 40% reduction is one of those targets that is very ambitious, but not realistic. the setting of these limits must, in my view, reflect both the need for the changes that the climate is causing and that are set out in the paris convention, and the possibilities of the automotive sector, which provides thousands of jobs not only in the czech republic but also throughout the eu. we need to encourage car manufacturers to invest in the development of low-emission and electric cars, while avoiding measures that would have a dramatic impact on the price of the car and thus on the end consumer.
mr president, it is very good that the european parliament has started to address new phenomena in the digital space, whether it is blockchains or technologies for sharing, replication and synchronisation of data. these are all new phenomena, they are phenomena that are truly global in nature, and therefore belong in some way to the agenda of at least the european parliament, and they are also phenomena that will certainly affect the digital space in the future. they will affect the flow of information, and therefore the economy. i have supported this report, but i would like to make the case that in any consideration of control or legislation in the case of these new phenomena we should be cautious and not fall on thin ice. these are sensitive issues, and as we've seen in a few cases in the past, when we've dealt with the internet, with attempts to regulate all sorts of things related to it, it hasn't always been handled well.
mr president, i would like to explain why i supported the report on the european union's agenda for rural, mountain and remote areas. this report looks at the territory that is the majority in the european union, 70 to 80%, but it has a much smaller population in this comparison, about 20%. so even though it is such a large area, it is a smaller population. but it's a very important area in terms of food security, in terms of its agricultural production, in terms of environmental quality, in terms of oxygen production, in terms of water retention. these are all very important functions that these areas have and deserve our attention, our protection. the people who live in these areas also need services of the same quality. i therefore consider it important that the european union does not weaken the policies that are specifically concerned with ensuring infrastructure and the quality of services in these areas, whether the common agricultural policy or other specific programmes.
mr president, i supported this motion for a resolution, and unlike my colleague and rapporteur, mr sośnierz, i believe that it is very important to show our helping hand to the people who live in these areas. i certainly do not want 80% of europe's population to live in the biggest agglomerations within 10 years. it's a big risk for our future. if, in fact, the population is moving only to agglomerations and their rings. we should also invest in these mountain areas precisely because this land deserves it, the agricultural production that takes place there deserves it, and we must invest in infrastructure, that is, in the transport services of this territory, but also in services. there must be health services, school services, social services available. i think that the area that mrs šojdrová was talking about is quite extensive and to abandon it as my colleague mr sośnierz has suggested is, i think, really disrespectful to the people who live there.
mr president, i supported this report, i think it is partly a concern and i stress that the european union is also partly concerned about the care of remote, mountainous and agricultural areas. on the other hand, we know that the european budget is limited, that it will certainly not increase in the future, and therefore all these issues must also be linked to the revision and reform of the common agricultural policy, which has so far been based on support for production. and we have to transform it to support the comprehensive development of these areas, that is, to develop infrastructure, networks, to increase the quality of life, to protect nature, to protect water management. all of this is interrelated and must therefore go hand in hand with budgetary restructuring and reform of the common agricultural policy. i hope that this report is taking us in that direction.
madam president, i didn't join mr. sulík in applauding because i think he didn't understand what the debate was supposed to be about. if you read the title of today's item, it's about minimum wage. the minimum income as presented here by mrs agea is to be introduced in italy as a form of social support. a lot of people have said that. i'm not going to talk about the guaranteed minimum income, the blanket benefit, which some of you may have talked about. the european union is based on the concept of a social market economy. this concept counts social support as an expression of solidarity for those who cannot work or have lost their jobs. for those who are sick or caring for a loved one. social support must be derived from the cost of living and must be conditional on activity, because the goal is a dignified life and that counts with work. that is why social policy must be supportive, but also motivating, of course, and i think the commissioner has spoken very strongly about this. the decisive factor is, of course, the action of the member states, which decide on the level of solidarity, the level of social support, the level of the minimum wage, which should not have been discussed here today. it's really about social support. the european union has only a complementary role, which it fulfils in the form of the european social fund. it helps where unemployment is high, because only by solving unemployment can a truly dignified life be achieved for all.
madam president, commissioner, first of all, allow me to smile a little at the speeches by my british colleagues who are calling for the sovereign right of the member states to decide on criminal matters, and then on the other hand to see the united kingdom, which is the one that uses eurojust the most and calls for cooperation the most, because of course it is the one that needs to coordinate the fight against crime or, in the case of terrorism, the most. but sometimes it is. it is simply that someone has to go through brexit in such a way that they realise where and in what family they have been, and i personally see eurojust's role primarily at the coordination level and that is where i think it needs to be strengthened. i think you were right in your opening remarks to say that it is important to divide the competences between the public prosecutor and eurojust in such a way that they do not overlap, that they are efficient and that they can work together better. i think that is the point of this report and the point of our action, which we have been trying to promote here for four years or more. i think that eurojust is of great importance in the field of cooperation and, of course, unlike my colleagues, i know that it will not interfere in any way with the national sovereignty of the individual member states. i think that in addition to what you have said here, it is also important to say that eurojust has proved itself and that this agency, which is one of the european union's showcases, needs to be constantly praised and constantly shown the results of its excellent work.
the rapporteur of the committee on legal affairs. madam president, commissioner, the commission's proposal on the mutual recognition of freezing orders is a typical example of european legislation that brings undeniable added value to all citizens of the european union. only with transnational instruments can we effectively combat transnational crime. it should be borne in mind that the current situation and the lack of sufficient harmonisation facilitate the financing of criminal activities. it is our responsibility as eu legislators not to make life easier for crime and to adopt procedural rules that allow for the confiscation of the proceeds of crime of a cross-border nature. the committee on legal affairs has drawn up an opinion on this proposal, in which it proposes, in particular, that certain wording be clarified so that the resulting text is indeed sufficiently precise.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, commissioner, this is a regulation that i believe the whole parliament should support. it is a regulation that i personally fought for, sending a letter with my colleagues to the president of the commission asking the commission to act and make a proposal. i am happy today to see the result that we can confirm the new fifth freedom in the european union: the free movement of data. the free flow of data is vital to the digital economy and the single market as a whole. the free flow of data is what makes cloud computing services work. everything from email to accountancy software needs the free flow of data to work. everything from traffic to weather information needs the free flow of data to work. it was clear that at the european level, we needed to prevent actions which would have put our digital future at risk, including ill—advised national measures restricting this free flow. this regulation is an example of what the european union is all about. it is about removing barriers and preventing new ones from arising. it is about making the single market work for everyone – businesses and citizens alike. it is about agreeing common european rules and then treating every member state equally – rules that allow data to be stored anywhere in europe without discrimination. it is about adopting simple rules and simple legislation that solve real life problems so consumers and taxpayers can get the best value for their money while facing less red tape and pointless barriers in their daily lives. this is why i was proud to be part of the team and i want to say thank the rapporteur, maria corazza bildt, for her work on this file.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, the issue of money laundering in the eu banking sector is extremely topical at the present time. the presidency and the council as a whole fully share the sense of urgency and the commitment of parliament and the commission to addressing this issue. our three institutions have worked together closely in recent years in a common endeavour to establish an effective regime to counter money laundering and the financing of terrorism. the amendment adopted earlier this year to the anti-money laundering directive is just the latest example of our good cooperation. turning to the issue at hand, the recent cases involving money laundering in some eu banks have shown that, unfortunately, anti-money laundering rules are not always supervised and enforced effectively across the european union. this is a great cause of concern, and i can therefore only support the commissionʼs push towards ensuring that anti-money laundering rules are effectively supervised and that authorities cooperate closely with each other. ministers at yesterday’s meeting of the ecofin council heard a presentation by the commission on its proposal of 12 september, an important contribution that the council is ready to consider very thoughtfully. beyond that proposal, we also need to consider a longer term solution and improvements to the current system. with this in mind, the council will be considering setting out comprehensive and long—term measures in this area by december. this is something that the council’s financial services committee is discussing today. the presidency is, of course, fully committed to maintaining and promoting the existing cooperation between the institutions. i am therefore naturally very interested in parliament’s views on the content of this latest proposal, as well as in the legislative approach that parliament will take. i look forward to hearing your views.
member of the commission. – mr president, like you, the commission has been shocked by the recent money laundering scandals in european banks and also by the failings that have come to light regarding the quality of national supervision and information sharing. these scandals endanger the reputation and integrity of europe’s financial system. while europe has the strongest anti-money laundering rules in the world, they need to be better enforced. the commission firmly believes that we need to enhance supervision in the eu. we are using existing powers and have asked the european banking authority to immediately investigate whether other supervisors in the member states concerned have breached eu law. some investigations have already been concluded, while others are still ongoing. at the same time, we cannot wait for further scandals to emerge. we must ensure the integrity of the eu’s financial system and we need to do so urgently. that is why, on 12 september, the commission tabled a legislative proposal to strengthen the powers of the eu supervisory authorities in anti—money laundering issues. as president juncker made clear in his state of the union address on the same day, we need to strengthen the enforcement of our anti—money laundering rules. our legislative proposal is part of a broader strategy to strengthen the eu’s framework for prudential and anti-money laundering supervision for financial institutions. through our amendments to our european supervisory authorities review proposal, which parliament is currently considering, we are proposing to concentrate tasks and resources within the european banking authority for anti-money laundering purposes, reinforce the tools for carrying out anti—money laundering tasks and strengthen the coordinating role of the european banking authority in international anti—money laundering issues. our accompanying policy communication also sets out improvements in the way that prudential supervisors reflect anti—money laundering considerations in their work and includes recommendations to the european central bank. we are also advocating further enhancement of the prudential framework for banks by improving information exchange and reinforcing the duty of cooperation between prudential and anti-money laundering authorities and bodies. the capital requirements directive is currently under negotiation between the council and parliament, and parliament has tabled useful amendments to that end. finally, the communication touches on longer term considerations as to whether a greater degree of harmonisation of anti—money laundering rules will be needed and whether we need a centralised anti—money laundering authority at eu level. alternatives could also be considered in order to ensure seamless information exchange and optimal cooperation between all relevant authorities in the eu, including those in the field of taxation. but, for now, i would again like to stress the need to move rapidly and decisively. in particular we need to quickly agree on the legislative proposal so that it can be adopted before the end of this term and the european banking authority (eba) can quickly assume its new tasks, which means that we have no time to lose. finally, i would like to take this opportunity to commend parliament for its persistent efforts, especially the work of the pana and taxe committees, to highlight the critical importance of the fight against financial crime, including tax evasion and money laundering, and to call to account all actors from both the private and the public sector. it is clear that collectively more can – and must – be done to address these failings and repair the damage that has been done to the eu’s international reputation.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, we have now less than one hour to discuss a very serious problem – the problem worth billions of dirty money. so let me try to illustrate, in the following 100 seconds, how serious the issue is, starting this some names: ablv latvia, versobank estonia, pilatus bank malta, danske bank branch estonia, and ing bank. these are just a few names of banks that showed serious weaknesses in the area of money laundering, and some of them collapsed and some of them suffered significant losses and have had to pay significant fines. but that is not enough: we all remember the ‘azerbaijani laundromat’ and the problems related to the use of crypto-assets for the financing of corruption or terrorism. money laundering is a very serious issue that is closely related to the crime, corruption, tax evasion and other acts that are seriously damaging our society – and we have to act. so far, it doesn’t seem that we are doing so sufficiently. take the example of insufficient regulation of crypto-asset exchanges, or the fact that the us authorities have to help us to find certain weaknesses in our financial sector. the protection of our financial sector against money laundering is clearly only as strong as the weakest point, and the weakest point seems to be very weak. in terms of improvement, i guess the focus should not be on approving new legislation, as it often has been. also, i am not sure that we should establish a powerful new institution. we should focus instead on enforcement and on strengthening the weakest spots in our system. the european supervisory authority (esa) review provides a good opportunity to find the right balance between the responsibility of supervisors and that of other branches of governments, including the police. we should not miss this opportunity, as clocks are ticking and the problem is very serious.

mr president, madam commissioner, despite the strengthened legislative framework adopted in recent years, we have recently witnessed a very serious problem with money laundering and therefore there is a legitimate concern whether our legal framework is really adequate and whether there are no gaps or deficiencies. the case of the collapse of the latvian bank, the freezing of the assets of the maltese bank due to suspicious transactions by its iranian owner, show that today's european anti-money laundering legal framework really lacks the necessary resilience to failures by the owners or managers of banks, or even national supervisors. the alarmingly low level of cross-border cooperation between national supervisors is also demonstrated by the revelation in the panama papers case that 21 billion euros of illicit capital flowed from russia to various banks in the eu. i therefore believe that the situation requires a decisive response from the commission, and i thank you, commissioner, for taking the lead in this initiative. effective anti-money laundering should be our priority. objections we may have to the current state of the rules: insufficient or delayed action by supervisors, shortcomings in the cooperation and information sharing of supervisors at national level or even between authorities in different member states or with authorities of third countries in the fight against money laundering. i therefore welcome the commission's action and its proposal for the relevant legislative amendment, in particular the one on banking union. the european authority needs to be given a stronger mandate in the fight against money laundering today.

the author. madam president, madam commissioner, industrial broiler farming is the dominant model for supplying the european market with a large quantity of relatively cheap meat. the so-called professional farms, with over 5 000 broilers, now cover 90% of the total broiler population in europe. the worldwide expansion of intensive poultry farming is not just a question of animal welfare, it is a question of human health. broilers are typically raised in a single space by the tens of thousands, with no access to daylight, no enrichment of the environment, no possibility of meeting the normal needs of the animals. mobility problems, dermatitis, deformity, pain, stress, inability to exhibit natural behaviour, these are just examples of the problems these animals face during their lifetime from transport to slaughter. it is truly shocking what we, as citizens of the developed countries of the 21st century, tolerate as normal economic practice. if the public knew the plight of these animals, i believe that most people would not be willing to eat the meat at all. over the last 80 years, we've reduced the life span of broilers to an average of 49 days, and at the same time, we've increased their carcass weight by 100 percent. can you imagine that? from hatching to 9 pounds in less than two months? but these experiments with nature are already getting out of hand for manufacturers, and that should be of interest to those who don't care about animal welfare. the poor health of broilers in factory farms requires the preventive use of antibiotics to prevent the emergence of epidemics and the spread of disease. statistics show that in the eu, around 70% of all antibiotics are used in livestock production, mainly in large farms, and most of this is used for the mass medication of feed or drinking water. this use of antibiotics then leads to the emergence of resistant and multi-resistant strains of pathogens. some strains of campylobacter, salmonella and escherichia now show resistance, and more are on the rise. if we don't start addressing this global problem in time, we risk having problems with our own health care. because we have no effective substitute for conventional antibiotics, and losing this tool would literally set our health care back a hundred years. the fact that factory chickens are a major source of ammonia pollution, among other things, seems like a piece of cake when compared with the problem of antibiotic resistance. i believe that we should carefully assess the impact of large-scale production and promote more sustainable farming practices and in this context we would like the commission to answer the following questions: will the commission take action to improve the enforcement of the broiler directive, in particular with regard to the systematic and consistent use of animal-oriented indicators? what action does the commission intend to take to promote alternative systems for chicken production such as those that favour more suitable breeds, lower rearing intensity, enriched environments, access to daylight, etc. given that such systems can help reduce ammonia emissions and reduce the need for uncritical use of antimicrobials and improve animal welfare? when will the commission publish a report on the socioeconomic impact of mandatory labelling methods?
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. – madam president, let me share with you just three points from the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ) to the budget. first of all, as we know, member states are not very keen to have a large budget. this is the reality and, as a consequence, we must aim even more to use money in a highly efficient way in areas where it is required by eu citizens or where it creates high value-added. second, isas are very important for the good functioning and stability of the financial sector in europe. cooperation with national regulators is absolutely essential. that is why we believe that they should be provided with adequate financing that respects their current challenges, including relocation or assignation of the new tasks. at the same time, we must push for increased efficiency of isas. last but not least, econ is also calling for adequate resources, for example for the coordination and surveillance of the macroeconomic policies of the member states’ fight against tax fraud and tax evasion and other areas.

the budget of the european union is an instrument for implementing european policies. they must address the priorities of european citizens and ensure that the objectives we set ourselves are achieved. i welcome the fact that the rapporteur did not play the council and commission, whose proposals were of an insufficient standard, and proposed to increase the budget. thanks to my group, the european parliament will negotiate with the council a plan that includes increased funding for the erasmus+ programmes, the youth employment instrument and the european child poverty guarantee. i also welcome the increase in climate change funding. the european union has been underperforming in this field for many years, despite repeated criticisms. if we tolerate failure to meet our own commitments, we will undermine our image in the eyes of the public and waste the time we have left to take the necessary adaptation and mitigation measures. on the contrary, i regret that my colleagues on the committee on budgets did not listen to my requests and increase the resources of efsa. the agency will objectively need a higher budget, inter alia, in connection with its work on pesticides. the failure to secure the budget is in this case tantamount to a threat to nature, but also to the health of all of us.
mr president, i would just like to draw attention to some important concepts associated with this report, this legislation, in my one minute. firstly, it is the availability of water if it is actually available to all citizens and to avoid speculation on the availability of this water. secondly, it's the quality, not just the clean water, but of course the water that is free of, for example, endocrine disruptors. or the issue of microplastics. the other is sustainability, the ability to deliver this accessible water to the citizens, but sustainability of the system as well. this means that the system must be able to respond to new technologies, while ensuring that the water available does not dramatically increase household costs. the second is transparency, i.e. the predictability of the system of supply of this water and the predictability of the quality of the water itself. the last one for me is a partnership project, i.e. partnership with municipalities and cities. i would also like to point out that in the czech republic, for example, we have many citizens who unfortunately still do not have access to water. they make up 11% of the european population.
mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, the right to water and sanitation was recognized as a human right eight years ago on the un's soil. in this context, it should be borne in mind that member states are obliged to put in place action plans aimed at ensuring universal and equitable access to safe and affordable drinking water for all by 2030. but how do we do that when we're seeing a lack of investment in maintaining and restoring water infrastructure? how does the state intend to ensure that access to clean drinking water is not restricted by the lack of affordability, when water management is constantly threatened with privatization and is already being privatized in a number of states? how do we ensure safe water for citizens if the commission does not present an updated eu strategy on endocrine disruptors? if we want to prove to the citizens that their areas of interest are our priority, we have to start from the bottom. the foundation for me is water, so we have to start with the right to drinking water.
mr. chairman, plastic can be a great invention, but it's changing over time into our nightmare. the environmental impact of plastic products is, in my view, still not sufficiently addressed. we are now literally flooded with tons of plastic that ends up in our seas and oceans, which is contrary to our efforts to preserve the oceans as such. europe produces 150,000 tonnes of plastic waste every year, which ends up in the sea. we must take drastic action against plastic products, or it will soon be too late to solve the problem. if we really want to prevent negative environmental impacts, we need to set a stricter legislative framework. i fully support this policy being focused on single-use plastics. uniformity also excludes sustainability. we have to deal with plastic bags, straws, plastic utensils, etc. on the other hand, we must come up with practical measures to replace these funds at the same time. only environmentally friendly products will allow us to avoid the current plastic waste situation from worsening.
plastics are responsible for 85% of the waste in the oceans. every year, 150,000 tonnes of plastic from europe ends up in the ocean. but the statistics about the impact of a single waste unit are also frightening. for example, a single cigarette filter can contaminate between 500 and 1,000 liters of water. i support the banning of products for which we already have more environmentally friendly alternatives. for the other products, investment in the development of alternatives will be needed before prohibiting them. at the same time, i believe that the amount of plastic contained in a product must be taken into account when implementing the measures of the directive. it is unfortunate that the draft directive and the report of the committee do not take account of the differences in the amount of plastic contained. while we may want producers to invest in alternatives and share the costs of waste collection, these obligations must be proportionate. i therefore welcome the relaxation of the liability regime and the addition of the commission's obligation to draw up guidelines to help member states apply the producer responsibility regime and (hopefully) prevent excesses.
mr president, i would like to thank my colleague, mr hetman, for a very good draft report. it is encouraging that the eu economy is currently growing and we are getting back to pre-crisis levels. moreover, i would remind you that at the beginning of 2018, the lowest unemployment rate in the euro area since 2008 was even measured, namely 8,6%. but i would like to point out that we should not focus solely on the positive indicators, which perhaps a little bit distorted, prove that we have in many ways restarted our economic performance. these indicators do not, in my view, sufficiently reflect all the circumstances. it is true that unemployment not only varies from one country to another, but is also different, especially when comparing the regions of the member states. moreover, the eu still has plenty of room for improvement in the area of youth, women's and successful labour market integration, for example of immigrants. the response to globalisation, digitalisation is still insufficient and many workers are indeed vulnerable. so i would be very careful in those areas. closely linked to this is the funding of cohesion policy, which also helps the least developed regions to grow.
mr. chairman, i'd like to follow up on my esteemed colleague mr. shkripka. i also wanted to commemorate the 100th anniversary of the founding of czechoslovakia. i would like to point out that we will be commemorating this important anniversary with the french president emmanuel macron and german chancellor angela merkel at the end of this week. this is a special moment for our country, as we remember the role of the first president of free and democratic czechoslovakia, tomáš garrigue masaryk, men of october 28th. their glorious vision of a free state, the goal they set for themselves with a well-thought-out plan, is truly a demonstration of their statehood. their work, which, despite the overwhelming difficulties, has survived all the disasters of the 20th century, provides our generation with the precious values of freedom, democracy, in which even the individual is important and has his place and irreplaceable value. their vision has collapsed, but cooperation continues to build the eu. i must also remind you that on october 28th, the men deliberately aligned themselves with the western democracies. unfortunately, subsequent historical developments after 1948 tore czechoslovakia... mr president, the facebook scandal has been a warning for all of us about what can happen when data is misused. now, however, we have to focus on the lessons learned. first, we need to be extremely cautious in protecting our data online. internet literacy and staying safe on the internet must become a standard part of our education systems. second, platforms need to better self—regulate. the days of community codes are over and now we need strict guidelines and rules. more importantly, platforms should act. lastly, fake news is a direct threat to our democracy with a potentially significant impact on the election results. important decisions, such as whether to stay part of the eu or not, should not be decided based on targeted fake news. we should bear this in mind in the run—up to the forthcoming european elections. we want to keep the internet an open and free forum of discussion, but we need to know how to spot the fake.

first vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i have literally come running from the college meeting that has just ended to present to you the work programme for 2019 that we have just decided. i think we are all aware that the next six months will be crucial for our union. for europeans going to the polls next may it is not enough that the commission has made proposals to make their lives better, they want to see results on the ground, concrete decisions. together we have a very short window of time in which to find agreement on the many proposals that are on the table and economic growth benefits all, and creating opportunity in the transition to the digital and low carbon economy. challenges such as demographics and migration, climate change and the competition for resources, which require a shared push towards sustainability. challenges to our values and democratic societies from terrorists, cyber-attackers and disinformation campaigns. in an increasingly volatile world, we have to show that europe is able to deliver solutions to these biggest of questions. today’s work programme reflects fully the ambition of jean-claude juncker’s state of the union address last month and the accompanying letter of intent. it represents the commission’s commitment to work intensively with you, and the council, to deliver what we promised under the 10 priorities on the basis of which this house approved the commission’s mandate, as well as, of course, to prepare the union for the future. our work programme has been enriched by the fruitful exchanges we have had with parliament, through the structured dialogue with the conference of committee chairs and with the conference of presidents as well. we have also had a good exchange with the council in line with the interinstitutional agreement on better law-making. so the first priority for our work in the coming year is all about delivery. with the initiatives presented alongside the state of the union address, the commission has tabled all of the legislative proposals needed to deliver on our common agenda for jobs, growth, fairness and democratic change. together with this house, and the council, we have found agreement on nearly half of these and a further 20% are well advanced in the legislative process. the priority must now be to turn the remaining proposals into legislation and then to implement it effectively. the joint declaration on the eu’s legislative priorities for 2018 and 2019, signed by the presidents of the three institutions, has proved valuable in helping us to find shared priorities, and especially to stick to them. i know from our dialogue that this parliament shares the commitment to maintain and further step up the momentum. the commission will be a constructive and energetic partner to you, as well as to the council, in helping find the agreements needed to deliver what we promised on migration, on the reform of our common asylum system, on the digital single market, on a fairer and deeper economic monetary union, on the european pillar of social rights and on internal security, to name but a few. to keep strong focus on delivery on these shared priorities, we are only proposing a very limited number of new initiatives in our work programme. some of these are policy initiatives which are needed to complete the 10 priorities in areas such as further boosting investment, addressing the challenges of artificial intelligence or tackling disinformation campaigns which threaten our democracy. whilst continuing the significant efforts to ensure an agreement with the united kingdom, we will bring forward the necessary measures to adapt the acquis ahead of brexit. we will also continue our better regulation work. to consolidate this as part of our collective dna, we will present the results of our ongoing stocktaking this spring. today we are announcing evaluation work and fitness checks to ensure our existing laws are fit for purpose and up to date, including in areas flagged in the opinions of the refit platform. we will also pay particular attention to ensuring the laws we already have are properly implemented and applied. in parallel with the work programme, we are also presenting a communication which sets out how we propose to strengthen the role of subsidiarity and proportionality principles in our policymaking. finally, our work programme takes forward the roadmap to the sibiu summit on europe day next year. this will be a crucial moment for the eu of 27 to shape our collective future. the commission will help prepare this discussion with contributions on delivering a sustainable european future to implement the sustainable development goals and surpass our paris commitments. we will advocate moving to more efficient decision-making in energy and climate, tax and social policies. we will also look at how to strengthen the international role of the euro and, as we will discuss further in the debate later this evening, at strengthening the rule of law framework. to build a strong foundation for our european future it is clearly essential that we find agreement before the sibiu summit on a future mff – multiannual financial framework – which will enable us to deal effectively with the challenges ahead. honourable members, this is the time to deliver; let’s get to work.

the next item is the topical debate (rule 153a) on evaluation of the lisbon treaty (2018/2888(rsp)). i should like to inform members that for this debate there is no catch-the-eye procedure and there will be no blue cards, so no blue cards will be accepted.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, thank you for inviting the presidency to your monthly topical debate. the council has not yet discussed the evaluation of the lisbon treaty as such. the council implements the treaty with a view to achieving results. we believe our focus should be on delivering results for our citizens, in particular, right until the end of the present term. be assured that the austrian presidency will be focused on this. on this i would simply note that the ordinary legislative procedure, which is now generalised after lisbon, works well – and i would like to thank the european parliament for the good cooperation – and that the relations between the council and parliament on the whole have indeed improved. beyond this, there is certainly a debate about the evaluation of the treaty and, more generally, about the future of the union. we appreciate that parliament has been working on several reports on certain aspects of the treaty. i am sure that in today’s debate a variety of views will be expressed, based on respective political perspectives. that said, i will listen to your debate attentively. thank you for this opportunity.
first vice-president of the commission. – mr president, frankly, i prepared for a debate about the lisbon treaty. i was waiting for mr zanni to go onto the lisbon treaty. he went onto many things, very interestingly, but not onto the lisbon treaty. i will stick to my brief anyway. the treaty is a reflection of what member states collectively want and require from the eu. they have come together and pooled sovereignty because they see there are challenges that no one member state can address acting alone. as jean-claude juncker said in the state of the union address this year, and i quote: ‘european sovereignty is born of member states’ national sovereignty and does not replace it. sharing sovereignty – when and where needed – makes each of our nation states stronger.’ end of quote. too often the discussion on sovereignty is a pretext for the question of how we can stop things, but the real question we should ask is: how can we act effectively and legitimately in the face of challenges or crises? i also want to react to some of the things mr zanni said. there is no imposition. every single member state has signed and ratified the treaty. you can’t afterwards say, ‘i’ve signed and ratified, but i will not apply the treaty’. we are based on the rule of law and that means that this union can only function if we can accept that those who sign and ratify the treaty also apply the treaty. if we have a problem with that, the commission can intervene and at the end of the day, it’s the court of justice that will rule on this. that is the essence of how we constructed the european union after the second world war. creating the impression that this is an imposition from outside is, i think, fooling your own people and it is irresponsible. it can create a certain feeling of ‘we’re being dictated to from outside’, but it is not a reflection of reality, and it is also hiding away your own responsibility. you have a responsibility vis-à-vis your colleagues in the council, vis-à-vis the european parliament, vis-à-vis the commission, but you certainly have a responsibility vis-à-vis your own signature, your own ratification, and i think we should be talking about that more. i also believe that if you look at the number of treaty changes we have seen over the years, you see that, given the complexity of the challenges and the complexity of working together and having to tackle issues no member state can tackle on its own, the need for decision through qualified majority voting (qmv) has increased. i hope we can use the coming time to look into areas where qmv can be increased, especially in foreign policy. if you have to take decisions on international human rights, or if you have to take decisions on commercial issues, i think qmv could be a helpful instrument. again, and i come from a country where the temptation of thinking that the veto was the best thing you had has sort of waned. people understand that a veto can only give you the instrument to stop something temporarily, but it doesn’t give you the opportunity to act, to actually do something proactively, and so i think the discussion of qmv will have to move forward. on issues such as taxation, there is no way we can muster enough strength against the huge corporations that can negotiate tax deals with individual member states that de facto mean they don’t pay any taxes if we don’t do this at the european level. we need to do this at the european level, and for that i think qmv would be important as well. i also want to mention briefly the task force on subsidiarity and proportionality that jean-claude juncker created last year. yesterday in the commission work programme, we set out in broad terms how we believe the task force needs to respond to the questions that were put on the table by member states, by national parliaments and by other stakeholders such as regional and local authorities. we will prepare this for the bregenz conference that will take place soon. the core of the issue is that we need to make sure that all those who in the end have to work with legislation are also involved in preparing the legislation, helping us to evaluate the legislation, to make sure that we avoid duplication, to make sure that we avoid unnecessary complexity. it is now also my experience that the issue is not, in general, should competences be returned from the eu level to member states? we've challenged everyone to give us some examples, and the examples are few and far between, but not really very concrete. the real issue we need to tackle is: how do we legislate? how do we do this? can we not do this in a way that is less complex, that will help local and regional authorities to have ownership of these processes? where can we evaluate existing legislation to make sure that we take out what is no longer necessary etc.? we should get out of this logic that the only thing we could do is do less. we need to do things europe needs to do, and we need to stop doing things that are nice to do. we only need to do what we need to do. i believe that if we start from that premise, we will understand that there is a tremendous amount we still need to do, and i couldn’t end my intervention without referring to the momentous decision this parliament took today to embrace the plastic strategy. it is now up to the council to do something that a vast majority of europeans want us to do, and they want us to do it now.
mr president, the treaty of lisbon is rightly considered to be a turning point in european integration. in particular, it has enabled the eu to function after the historic enlargement of the former eastern bloc in 2004. it has also enabled new forms of association between member states, including enhanced cooperation in the field of defence. but now, almost 10 years after its real effectiveness, we have to set the mirror. have we really brought the union closer to the citizens? are we more accessible? do we involve more national parliaments in decision-making? are we really strictly adhering to the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality? for me personally, there is only one conclusion: europe, which has now gone through several crises, needs to reflect. we cannot continue to act as if nothing had happened, and we would be putting the whole european project in jeopardy. i would very much hate for the only real outcome of the lisbon treaty to be brexit.
mr president, the treaty of lisbon does not provide for the balance of power, the mutual control of institutions that we normally use at the level of associations, state institutions and international organisations. let us start with the commission: under article 17 (1) of the treaty of lisbon, 'the commission shall promote the general interest of the union'. notice that the whole of the lisbon treaty does not say that the union should serve the people, it serves itself. we can back that up with other quotes. article 17 (3) states that 'the members of the commission shall be chosen in such a way as to ensure their complete independence'. completely independent of the citizens of the member states, of ordinary people. it expressly states: 'the commission shall exercise its functions with complete independence. the members of the commission shall neither seek nor take instructions from any government, body, institution or other body. this means that the commission is completely cut off from the people. a political system that does not take into account the interests of the people is considered an authoritarian system. some argue that it is up to the member states to decide what the commission is allowed to do. council: article 4 (3) of the treaty of lisbon states: 'member states shall facilitate the achievement of the union's tasks and shall refrain from any measure which might jeopardise the attainment of the union's objectives.' so states cannot promote their own interests and the interests of their citizens in the union. i will quote the president of the european commission, mr juncker, at this point. he told the assembly of heads of government, "stop listening to your constituents". we could go on like this, we could prove that the lisbon treaty is a totalitarian system similar to those in the communist countries.
first vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i already said this morning that the origins of european integration is the wish of the european people to negotiate their differences around the conference table rather than on the battlefield. but if you look at europe today, the challenges we face are global challenges and the one question you can ask your citizens – and i am sure of the answer you’ll get – is, do you honestly think you can face the migration challenge on your own as a member state? do you honestly think nationalism is an answer to the challenges of economic change in the fourth industrial revolution? do you honestly believe that being on your own will make you stronger in facing the challenges of president putin or the challenges of a president trump, who wants to change the international system? do we honestly believe we will be stronger on our own? i don’t think there is a constituency anywhere in a member state that truly believes that, and i’m afraid the british people today are learning this the hard way. i believe that if we take that as a starting point, the way we transform our rules, the way we apply the treaty to face these challenges and to come up with real results for our citizens, is going to determine whether we’ll get support for this. i think debates about abstract notions such as sovereignty are distracting people or they are causing alarm. i do maintain that you can have a theoretical approach to sovereignty and to saying i want national sovereignty. theoretically, technically, yes, national sovereignty, but i think it was michael heseltine who said that ‘a man alone in the desert is sovereign [too]’. he dies of thirst but he’s sovereign. sovereignty should also be a material issue. does it give you the power to act and to change things the way your citizens want things changed? then the answer will be very clear: only if we act collectively can we shape the world according to the values we share in a world that is changing so fast. i wish that could be the starting point of our discussion about what we need in a treaty. there are still ample opportunities in the existing lisbon treaty to move ahead. let’s try and use those opportunities first, and if they are exhausted we can talk about treaty change. but we haven’t exhausted the possibilities that are on the table with the existing lisbon treaty. let’s do a great service to our citizens and use those opportunities to actually change the world in the direction they want.
i regret that not all of us have utilised the time for evaluation of the lisbon treaty, which was the topic of this topical debate, but of course there is freedom to approach it in one’s own way. the debate is closed. written statements (rule 162)
the next item is the debate on the court of auditors’ annual report – 2017 (2018/2760(rsp)).
president of the court of auditors. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. as a former member of this house, i know all too well that the european parliament is currently going through a very busy phase. parliament is currently working at full speed to get a number of other pieces of legislation in place before the elections. i should like to thank you all the more, as president of the european court of auditors, for the time you have taken to discuss our annual report in the plenary. we have presented our annual report to give you our assessment of the state of the european union's financial management in 2017. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the budgetary authority is able to meet the requirements of the budget. for 2017, as in previous years, we conclude that the eu accounts present a true and fair view of the financial position of the european union. as in the previous year, we are issuing a limited and non-failed audit opinion on the regularity of the payments underlying the 2017 annual accounts. in other words: a significant proportion of the expenditure we examined in 2017 does not have a material error rate. i am referring to expenditure based on entitlements, i.e. expenditure on programmes where beneficiaries receive payments if they meet certain conditions. these entitlement-based payments accounted for more than half of all payments from the eu budget in 2017. in addition, irregularities in eu spending have also continued to decline. the estimated error rate for payments in 2017 was 2,4%, after reaching 3,1% in 2016 and 3,8% in 2015. this shows that the financial management of the european union is clearly moving in the right direction, which - and i can say this very clearly - is due to the continuing joint efforts of the commission, the european parliament, the member states and the court of auditors. let me give a positive example of direct payments under the european agricultural guarantee fund, which accounts for a very large part of the corresponding mff heading. for these direct payments, we concluded that they no longer had a significant error rate. however, i would also like to point out that in many cases there is or has been sufficient information to prevent, detect and correct a significant proportion of the errors we have detected. if this information had been used by the commission or national authorities, an even larger proportion of the european budget would have had an error rate below the court's and the commission's materiality thresholds. in short, what we discovered could have been discovered by others. ladies and gentlemen, now it's time to take it a step further. the management and control systems have improved. for us as the court of auditors, this is now the opportunity to use these improvements for our audits. for 2017, we have tried a different approach in the area of cohesion. the most important change was that we reviewed and verified audits and controls that had previously been carried out by the bodies responsible for expenditure. with this new approach we want to provide you, the members of parliament, with better geographical and fund-specific knowledge for the whole of the european union in a cost-effective way. in the coming years we will extend this approach to other areas of expenditure, provided that - and i emphasize this - the necessary conditions are met, i.e. the data available to us are sufficiently reliable. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i have read with great interest the results of the 2018 parliamentarian survey that you recently published. i am certainly optimistic about the future to see that eu membership and the euro have strong support among the majority of european union citizens. at the same time, there are also large disparities in support between countries. both annual budgets and the union's long-term budget are instruments of distribution. the decisions taken in this area often involve political complications for member states which they consider to have 'gone badly'. whatever the political outcome of the decisions for us at the court of auditors, it is important to ensure that the citizens of the union whether in bucharest, helsinki, lisbon, berlin or paris can see for themselves and thus have confidence that eu funds have not only been used properly but also in a meaningful way. we call this process "business review" or "performance review", and i can assure you that you will see an increasing number of these reviews in the coming months and years. this is a direction which has been consistently pointed out to the court in the numerous conversations i have had in this house, but also in many national capitals, and i think this is a very important task. ladies and gentlemen, the court has shown that various member states have difficulties in making good and timely use of european structural and investment funds. in this respect, it is to be welcomed that the commission has proposed a number of measures for the next financial framework which should help to make better use of cohesion funds. in the coming days we will publish our detailed opinion on the relevant legislation. at the same time, the european budget remains under heavy pressure, given the amount of payments the eu has committed to make in the coming years. these so-called commitments to be settled or rals are expected to increase even further by the end of 2020. we believe that this growing volume of commitments still to be settled must be a major focus of the next multiannual financial framework. the total budget of the european union represents only about 1% of the gross national income of the european union as a whole. it is important that the european budget is used effectively. at the same time, however, we must be realistic about what can be achieved with the resources entrusted to us. the european union can only win or retain the trust of its citizens if it actually meets the expectations it has created. we must show our citizens that the european union can deliver on its promises efficiently and in a way that actually makes a difference.
member of the commission. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union. in the context of the annual accounts presented by the commission, the annual report of the european court of auditors plays an important role in the discharge procedure. we are grateful for the informed, objective assessment that the court presents to us today for the financial year 2017 and the commission's accounts. i believe that we can present parliament, the council and the court with good results in the implementation of the budget and the clearance of accounts, in the use of our budgetary resources. the court of auditors therefore also confirms improvements in budgetary management and its estimated error rate is lower than ever. i want to contribute to ensuring that we continue to learn from the experience and the constructive critical advice of the court and parliament and the council and that we are not at the end of a good development in budgetary management. the annual report shows that we are transparent in the implementation of the budget. the court's full audit opinion on the reliability of the annual accounts has now been issued for the eleventh consecutive time. the court recognises that the budget is an important instrument for achieving europe's political objectives and that it helps to bring real added value to the budget in the interests of our citizens. in the past year, we have added to our traditional long-standing tasks such as the common european agricultural policy and the still important cohesion policy, focused on infrastructure and the development of a high-performance crossborder infrastructure across the continent. we have funded more major research projects than ever before, and we have tried to tackle the migration problem, which has become a focus of attention in the last three years, not only politically but also financially. we believe that the european budget can deliver concrete and good results for our citizens, and we have done so in the past year. the commission also considers that controlling and further reducing the risk of error is a key element of its budgetary management. the commission has already made a number of proposals. our goal is to make mistakes avoidable in the first place so that we don't have to correct them in the first place. we are therefore working closely with the member states and with the beneficiaries. we provide assistance, training and helpdesks to ensure that shared management - beyond the european services on the ground - ensures the appropriate expertise in budget implementation. you can't avoid all mistakes. that's why we need robust correction systems when things go wrong. these corrections take place only after payment has been made, which is also due to the multiannual nature of the european programmes. we as the commission want to ensure that when we manage our budget, after the implementation of a programme, after the closure of a programme and after all the checks have been carried out, the remaining error rate is below 2%. we reached our target last year and estimate the remaining error rate at about 0.6%, i.e. well below 1%. i would add that mistakes can be traced back to fraud, waste and inefficiency. but there may be mistakes that are the result of negligence, that have nothing to do with fraudulent intent, corruption or disloyalty, and that still need to be corrected. the president of the court of auditors mentioned the commitments still to be settled and we also discussed payments at the interinstitutional meeting last week. we are monitoring the situation very closely, also because parliament expects it. we will make it clear in full transparency which commitments are still to be made. the sum of some eur 270 billion is not a surprise at the present stage of a multiannual budgetary framework, but is explainable. eu grants needed to implement programmes on the ground are disbursed to member states and other beneficiaries. we're not holding back money. but it should be borne in mind that the duration of many projects, from initial planning to construction, implementation and closure, extends over several years. this means that commitments may not be settled until a later financial year - and often after the end of the multiannual financial framework. this phenomenon also occurs in national budgets, but to a much lesser extent. the current state of commitments to be settled is not surprising, and the implementation of projects on the ground has picked up speed. i can tell you that the member states are now carrying out project selection on schedule, that the budget has now reached full speed, and this also applies to the implementation of the current 2018 budget. i am not going to go into detail. we are currently in the middle of negotiations with you on the next multiannual financial framework 2021-2027. i would like to thank the house - parliament - for its efforts to establish quickly a position which you intend to take in this house next month. the commission's proposals contain many elements which will bring about improvements in relation to some of the aspects identified by the court. the first is that the difference between the amount of commitment appropriations and the amount of payment appropriations should be reduced from the outset. secondly, more flexibility. third, n+2 instead of n+3 as it is now, so that the incentive to avoid delays increases, and then a stronger focus on performance and results. we need your support on these points and we want to include the opinion of the court. i would like to thank mr lehne, mr lazarou and your staff for the work the court has done to bring about further improvements in the management of european finances. it is my and our overall aim to have a budget that delivers results and real added value and is managed well and correctly. we need your constructive cooperation. we look forward to working constructively with parliament, the council and the court of justice in the forthcoming discussions on discharge for 2017.

the next item is the debate on the council and commission statements on the grave lack of implementation of the eu reach regulation and use of non-tested chemicals in the eu (2018/2887(rsp)).
mr president, reach requires industry to manufacture, import and use chemicals safely. to do so, sufficient information on hazardous chemicals must be available. it is a fact that many chemicals have been placed on the eu market, sometimes in very high quantities, without sufficient information on the risks they pose to human health and the environment. however, a wide range of test methods are currently used and efforts should be made to avoid testing on laboratory animals and to give preference to alternative methods of testing. in this context, it should be noted that the implementation of reach legislation, including the proper testing of chemicals, is a competence and responsibility of the member states. each member state is obliged to set up control mechanisms, including sanctions, for infringements of the provisions of reach. the european chemicals agency also has a role to play in this respect, liaising with the national supervisory authorities and alerting them to any deficiencies identified. that's the way the whole system is set up, and i'm convinced that it's set up well, i don't see any reason for revolutionary changes. the correct way is to apply the existing rules consistently. i want to speak in defence of reach, which i consider to be the most advanced and comprehensive chemical legislation in the world. the eu's example has also inspired many other countries around the world. eu industry now produces chemicals that are safer for citizens and the environment. our task now is rather to ensure that eu producers are not placed at a competitive disadvantage compared to non-eu producers.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank the speakers who have expressed concerns in relation to this issue, and to assure mr eickhout that we are not ping—ponging at all with the council. we are just out setting out the facts of the situation, mr eickhout. the issue of non—compliance of registration of dossiers is not unexpected and was foreseen by the decision—makers with the compliance check process. this is the reason why the second review was carried out and, arising from that review, there were recommendations made and i just want to tell you about some of them that have been implemented. so it’s not that there’s nothing happening. the european chemicals agency (echa), with the support of the member states and the commission, has been working on the effectiveness and the efficiency of the process. the commission has requested echa to significantly increase the efficiency of the evaluation procedures by 2019 – that’s not far away – by identifying reasons for non—compliance and developing the necessary remedies to improve the procedures and the work—sharing with the member states. this is not happening in the best possible way at the moment, but we want to ensure that the processes do work – and can work in parallel – and, importantly, to address the groups of substances where appropriate. so concrete changes in decision—making are already taking place and a first 14 substance group compliance check decisions are in the final adoption stage in echa. the commission has encouraged as well, of course, the updating of registration dossiers by the first quarter of 2019 to specify further the situations that trigger updates, as well as to set precise deadlines. we have an implementing regulation, supporting the implementation of reach article 22, being considered by the commission and this work is ongoing. enhanced enforcement is very important and you and other speakers have raised this. so, by the first quarter in 2019, the commission will consider further measures that are necessary in order to ensure that we have better enforcement. the objective of the action is wider, but we will also address the issue of registration dossier compliance. the work of the german institute is very welcome. i stress though that the german institute’s project was established to estimate the data quality. it was not the legal compliance of the dossiers that it was asked to do. this is a matter for echa. nevertheless, it is a very valuable contribution towards bringing this issue up the agenda and ensuring that we all have to do better in order to make sure that the dossiers are registered and that there is full compliance with what was intended in relation to the reach proposals in the past.
