for the group gue/ngl madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the implementation of the medical devices and in vitro medical devices regulations has many interconnections. unfortunately, there are now more questions than answers on the part of both manufacturers, notified bodies and patients, and i have to admit that many of their concerns are quite legitimate. it is already clear that the new legislation brings with it an undesirable reduction in the number of notified bodies. this will mean curbing the entry of new technologies and limiting the availability of older resources. for example, in the czech republic, where i come from, we have only two notified bodies left and recently there were five more. austria has none of those. the remaining notified bodies are already drowning in work and applications and it is not enough to deal with them, let alone take on new clients from countries where no notified body already exists, thus slowly pushing medical device manufacturers from these countries out of the market. of the implementing and delegated acts, only one has been adopted, as far as i know, and even if i have missed one, it is an absolutely scandalous balance at this stage. in particular, in a situation where the first regulation will enter into full force as early as may 2020. before a notification can be made to the nando database, it is essential that the notification procedure of each member state is published on the website. there was no notification procedure published there a year ago. overall, there is a slowdown at european level and the transitional period for medical device regulations no longer seems sufficient. if the situation is not resolved, the patients will feel the results. and not to mention, up to 40% of the current european certificates are from britain. however, the eu will lose its notified persons there completely, according to the explanatory documents from the european commission with brexit. in addition, it is estimated that around half of the authorised representatives in the medical device sector are based in the uk. some have already announced that they will move their headquarters to the eu, but not all will. for these reasons, the entry of devices on the market will be very slow. i therefore have three sub-questions: first, the newly tightened requirements of the medical device regulation are based on the evidence based principle and evidence based on scientifically correctly interpreted facts. i would therefore like to hear from the european commission some evidence of its current calm. do the representatives of the european commission consider that the implementation of both regulations is proceeding successfully according to the timetable and that there will therefore be no major problems in the market after may 2020? can the european commission guarantee that there will be no temporary withdrawal of more medical devices from the market due to a lack of notified bodies and their capacity? has the european commission carried out any specific studies or in-depth assessments to support this conclusion and what are the specific findings? what statistics can the european commission provide to support these conclusions? are these statistics publicly available? secondly, the capacity of notified bodies is currently a bottleneck for the entry of medical devices into the market. is it true, as we often hear in the news, that the european commission prefers to eliminate all smaller notified bodies in order to reduce their number to about 10 and to concentrate on a few large ones? can you provide evidence for future capacity analyses? last summer, 59 notified bodies were under the old legislation, so how many do the european commission consider to be optimal and sufficient to ensure the functioning of the market? thirdly, harmonised standards and common specifications are one of the cornerstones of the mdr. as regards standards and their harmonisation for medical device regulations, a considerable delay is already planned. some important standards, such as the assessment of biocompatibility, will be harmonised only in may 2024, 4 years after the entry into force of the first regulation. why has this happened, and what is the plan to address this delay? i firmly believe, commissioner, that today you have a unique opportunity to dispel not only my doubts about your inaction, or the commission's inaction, but above all the doubts of the people who are watching this point, the states who are worried about not having enough quality and registered medical devices, and the patients who are desperately complaining that they do not know how this child of legislation we have here will work after 2020.
madam president, i have to say, just on the basis of what we have heard, that my mother and my parents also remember very much the communist period, which mr sośnierz mentioned, when there was a lack of basic medical equipment in czechoslovakia and i very much doubt that there would be a lack of it in poland, so this is a bit of an expression in the context of what mr sośnierz said. however, new regulations on medical devices and in vitro diagnostic medical devices approved in 2016 help to ensure that all medical devices are safe and function well. this includes contact lenses, x-rays, breast implants, artificial joints, and so on. to this end, the new rules improve market surveillance and traceability and introduce stricter controls on medical devices. i am convinced that whether it is medical devices, cars or other products, there is a need to ensure effective supervision in the interests of our citizens. and from these new rules we should expect greater transparency, legal certainty for producers and importers, enhanced international competitiveness and also new innovation in this strategic sector. however, already during the three-year transition period ending in may 2020, it has become clear that the application of the mdr and the in vitro diagnostic medical devices regulation is facing problems and that some elements of the new system are indeed not being prepared optimally. this has been discussed here a lot, for example the problematic launch of the eudamed database, which is intended to integrate different electronic systems. the proper functioning of this database is very important, as it aims to improve access to information for the public as well as for healthcare professionals, for example to avoid multiple reporting requirements and to streamline or facilitate the flow of information between economic operators. i would therefore like to ask the commission, madam commissioner, whether the commission is really convinced that this database will work, whether it has created the necessary conditions for this and whether this database will serve the purpose i have outlined. this is particularly for healthcare professionals, but of course also for citizens who need to know whether, for example, their particular implant will be compatible with the airport's scanner. another problem worth mentioning is the lack of notified bodies responsible for the certification of medical devices. the commission should therefore address this situation without delay in order to avoid disrupting the supply of important medical devices to the market. i also expect the commission to improve coordination between the three dgs concerned so that the new system receives the necessary administrative support. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i welcome the agreement that has been reached, which allows for greater control over imported cultural goods. the european parliament has contributed to the final form of this regulation, for example by adapting the categories according to the unesco recommendations for the most valuable cultural goods, where a licence of origin clause is required for goods already over 100 years old. the aim of this regulation is therefore to restrict the financing of terrorism, in particular from areas of conflict. the european parliament also reduced a certain bureaucratic burden, so it is not a matter of restricting trade but of restricting the illegal import of cultural heritage for the purpose of financing terrorism.

the rapporteur. madam president, mr commissioner, when the european commission presented its winter package proposals in january 2016, it was clear that it had just started a long marathon of very demanding work. the challenge was to achieve a wording of the legislative proposals that would be acceptable to the european parliament, the european council and the european commission, while at the same time allowing the european energy and climate change objectives to be met. i am convinced that europe not only has its long-term share of responsibility for the global environment, but also, unlike many others, has the means and potential to be the main driver of these changes. i believe it is no coincidence that today's discussion of the clean energy package, and indeed today's plenary session as a whole, starts with the energy efficiency directive, because this is how we put the 'energy efficiency first' principle into practice, and i believe that most member states will follow this trend in their energy policy making. on the other hand, we didn't all share the same vision at the beginning about how ambitious we should be in energy efficiency. often, and it was by interpreting the results of impact studies differently, or using other sources of mathematical cost models for energy efficiency measures, we differed greatly. but the european parliament has always been among the leaders in promoting an ambitious european climate policy. as elected representatives of the people of the european union, we have a full responsibility to find effective measures which i believe the citizens expect from us. we entered into negotiations with the council in the spring with a requirement for a binding 30% energy efficiency target by the end of 2030, whereas the council supported the commission's proposal at 30% in a non-binding format. we had very long and technical discussions about the cost of the measures being introduced, their return and their impact on economic growth. i have always been convinced, and i remain convinced, supported by the results of impact studies and a number of analyses, that despite the initial costs, these energy efficiency measures will have a very positive impact on economic growth, employment and on reducing the european union's dependence on fossil fuel imports. i am pleased that after very difficult negotiations and many rounds of trialogue we have managed to reach a compromise of 32.5%. i would of course like to see that figure increased, after all i have clearly supported the 40% figure repeatedly in the european parliament, but let us remember that 32.5% was a big concession for the council. there was also a lot of discussion about the so-called article seven, where we finally reached a very good level of 0.8% of final energy consumption. this is very ambitious, more ambitious than the original proposal of the european commission. i would personally like to see transport included in this article, which in the end did not happen, but i trust that the member states will act responsibly in this respect. i would also like to conclude by mentioning the issue of energy poverty. as you know, our political group has been a long-standing supporter of the idea of fighting energy poverty on this soil. this has recently made it possible to observe that the problems of people who cannot pay their heating or energy bills are increasingly resonating among politicians at all levels. i am therefore very pleased and i consider it a great success that we have managed to significantly strengthen this aspect and to motivate the member states to prioritise investments in housing for the most vulnerable groups in their savings planning. the draft directive will be reviewed in 2023 in the light of the implementation of the climate agreements and also in the light of the progress in innovation and technology. we agreed that we would not be closed to higher ambitions, and i believe strongly in those higher ambitions. in the climate agreement, we committed to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by at least 40% by 2030, and increasing energy efficiency is the most cost-effective way to do that. i believe that by negotiating energy efficiency legislation we have indeed created a huge opportunity not only for our environment, but also for european industry, services and citizens, including the poorest groups, and for our competitiveness in a world market that is increasingly under threat. i would therefore be very pleased if the european parliament could approve this agreement by a large majority tomorrow, so that all our obligations under this directive are clearly defined.
madam president, commissioner, i am pleased that the trialogue has recognised what i believe to be overly ambitious goals for substantial change. i would particularly emphasize energy efficiency. in this case, acceptance of the originally programmed targets would imply incredibly high investment costs and huge price fluctuations. i am also pleased that we have managed to reduce the obligations arising from the so-called 'governance' and to keep the energy package as close as possible to the original package: that is what 'governance' is all about. it's not that much of a shift in the climate. it remains essential for me to ensure that the security and reliability of electricity supply are the responsibility of the member states. the member state must therefore also retain the necessary powers and decision-making powers to ensure the security and reliability of electricity supply. the role of regional or european organisations and their acts must remain advisory, without decision-making powers. the same goes for the energy mix. only the member state must retain the right to choose the energy mix and at the same time the ability to benefit from it. this includes the obligation to bear the disadvantages of that choice. a member state should not be obliged, but only able, to shoulder the costs of energy mix choices in neighbouring member states in solidarity.
madam president, the clean energy package is really one of the most important and significant pieces of legislation of our mandate and i’m very glad that i have been able to participate in the deliberations. it is absolutely necessary to modernise and shape the energy market, that is for sure, especially if we want to succeed in global competition, but at the same time, we need to focus on the other side of the coin: reducing consumption. it has dropped but, at the same time, we have new technologies, electro-mobility, other sources, renewables, and we obviously will have to focus on further reducing consumption. for me, the question is energy efficiency, an energy efficiency policy first, definitely. i would appreciate it if we could focus on that and i invite the member states to implement, as soon as possible, as thoroughly as possible, the clean energy package, with an emphasis not just on the opportunities in terms of technology, renewables, etc. but also on lowering consumption.
madam president, i would like to stress that europe is founded on pride in its diversity, on the diversity of cultures that enrich us all, on the sharing of our knowledge and, of course, on the wisdom and experience of our ancestors. respect for minorities within the european union is therefore one of the absolutely essential building blocks of our community. i therefore welcome the steps that will lead to an improvement in the status of minorities, the status of foreigners legally residing in our territory, and will effectively prevent discrimination. of course, we must not forget the importance of education, the importance of preventing attacks on these minorities, and at the same time we should also ask ourselves what we are doing to ensure that - each of us - minorities are treated in the sense of the law, in the sense of decent behaviour. not only us, but also the municipalities, regions, the state and of course the european union as a whole must be asked to cooperate.
madam president, i would like to thank my colleague, mr bogdan went, very much for this report. he pointed out that unfortunately not all member states were on the same page when it came to digitisation, even within the european union. this is even more true in developing countries. for example, sub-saharan africa is the worst off in terms of digitization and internet accessibility. and it is in these developing areas that digitisation presents a great opportunity for economic and social development. individual member states should therefore integrate this area of digitalisation into their national development cooperation strategies. many areas are doing very well, not the number of cell phones or the internet, but it's very geographically imbalanced, and that's where we need to make a change. i would like to draw attention to the danger of the misuse of personal data, the misuse of the internet to spread radicalism. this is another area where the european union can set a good example.
madam president, i would like to stress that the reduction in the amount of money spent on education is a bad trend that must be reversed. i agree with the proposal to increase the proportion of funding to this sector and consider it a step in the right direction. in addition to the priorities set out in the committee on development's proposal, i would like to highlight one more fact, namely the crucial and neglected importance of tertiary education. this education is really important for the development of any region, not just the disabled, but the disabled all the more. without quality higher education, particularly poorer regions will not be able to cope with the brain drain, as these brains will naturally move to places with higher education. without investment in tertiary education, european universities will not be attractive for researchers to work, which may mean that europe will no longer be able to compete with global players in technological development. i want to emphasize that we are already seeing this trend and there is a real need to confront this trend and prevent it from getting worse.
i support this report on european union development aid in the field of education. after all, education is a basic human right, education is the key to personal success, and it is also the best tool in the fight against poverty. so we have to work to make education available to all children, especially where it is not yet available, where it is not of high quality, where children cannot be educated, which is africa in particular. i also support the objective of increasing each member state's share of investment in education and training in development cooperation by 10% by 2024 and by 15% by 2030 if necessary. they're the right indicators. there is a lot of talk about the need to help the countries of origin in the fight against migration, and investing in education would be the best tool here. i also want to mention the long-distance adoption program, which many citizens contribute to.
madam president, these days we are commemorating the centenary of the end of the first world war. we commemorate this to ensure that the millions of dead, the millions of victims, are not forgotten and to remind the younger generation of the main reason why we have the european union as an area of peace. in 1914 people also did not believe that any war could be, in an age of rationality and progress. the royal families of europe were intertwined by kinship, and who would want to go to war against their own? but nationalism prevailed. in 1918, they were convinced that this war was the last, that nothing like this could ever happen again, and yet in 20 years it could happen again. today we live in a time of peace, but peace is not a given, and many young people tell me that they cannot imagine war, they are convinced that it is not possible. that's why we need to remember the millions who died in both world wars.
madam president, arcelormittal is buying the italian steel mill ilva. the european commission investigated this acquisition and ordered the company to sell some of its other plants. it has decided to sell the ostrava steelworks, which employs over 7,000 people in my moravian-silesian region. the risk of closure of the mills for the region associated with the sale is enormous, as it is currently facing the decline of coal mining and the insolvencies of many other companies, which have already led to the loss of thousands of jobs in recent years. all the trade unions in the ostrava steelworks strongly protest against the choice of the buyer of liberty steel. their main arguments include a breach of the conditions of sale under european legislation concerning the obligation for the buyer to have financial resources, proven knowledge of the field and the incentive to develop the company being sold as a viable competitive force. if you, as the commission, have decided that you want to intervene in this sale in any way, please do not let part of my republic, part of my region go bankrupt just because we sell it to someone who simply cannot afford it and will do nothing about it.

mr. chairman, i just have a technical note. my colleagues are not here because there is a vote on the terr special committee at the same time. and unfortunately, this vote, the vote on the 2,000 amendments, will be over in five minutes. so i'd like to ask you all if you could come out here after the colleagues arrive. commissioner, i would like to say on this subject that we all know that the european union has failed to respond effectively to the migration crisis. we have not been able to cope with the uncontrolled influx of migrants and refugees, who often enter the european union without proper documentation and checks. in 2015, i repeatedly visited refugee camps in greece, italy, and on my way through the balkans. it was clear that the european union was not able to effectively distinguish between those who were entitled to asylum and those who were not. after the entry into the european union of third-country nationals, all those who had a so-called syrian passport, even though it could be forged, were considered refugees. this situation must not happen again. the tool we were supposed to respond to the whole crisis was supposed to be humanitarian visas. i think this is a very good idea, but unfortunately we are halfway there again. this idea needs to be worked on and refined.
mr president, in the context of humanitarian visas, i think it is essential to emphasise one thing. in accordance with the judgment of the court of justice, member states are not obliged under european union law to issue humanitarian visas but are free to do so on the basis of their national law. so we're not talking about some mandatory mechanism, but about individual member states being able to analyze who is really a humanitarian refugee and who is just taking a chance to improve. this principle of voluntariness is refreshing and acceptable after years of embarrassing misrepresentation of mandatory quotas. the czech republic must decide for itself who it will grant a visa and who it will allow to enter the country. refugees must no longer support organised crime run by smugglers and be forced to cross european borders. they should apply for a humanitarian visa in the nearest safe country and await the decision of the sovereign state of a member state of the european union.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, allow me to begin by thanking my colleague, ms pilar del castilla, for her excellent work on the code. the code and berec are closely linked, and from the outset, i worked in coordination with ms pilar del castilla, and finally both proposals were successfully concluded in a joint trilogue. the code brings a number of new tasks for berec which needed to be reflected in this report. the issue of special voting rules in the board of regulators proved to be the most problematic point of the final trilogue and the commission was very helpful to us as a mediator. berec regulation as agreed by the hosts and the chapter originally agreed in the code on the regulation of calls within the european union. it would therefore not be fair to attribute this to the outcome of the berec report. in general, i appreciate the compromise on the code, i believe it will facilitate the development of high-speed networks as a prerequisite for 5g, as pilar del castillo mentioned here a moment ago. from the point of view of our political group, i am pleased that the resulting text leaves more flexibility to the member states in the area of spectrum and does not include single authorisations or the so-called double lock. back to the heart of the berec report the structure of the berec office and the berec support office was the result of a 2009 political agreement. now the commission has proposed to merge them into one large agency. as rapporteur, i initially tried to gather information from various sources on whether this dual structure was functional, and my colleagues and i also undertook a mission of the committee on industry, research and energy to riga. i'm not a fan of making major dramatic changes where something is working well, and positive evaluations here within berec have been strongly overwhelming. we also reflected concerns about the independence of the regulators' decision-making. we need real independence of regulators, both at the level of the member states and the european union. their distance from national governments and the european commission was therefore important. i therefore proposed to reject the merger of berec and the berec support office and to concentrate rather on minor improvements as discussed. nevertheless, the european parliament was divided on this fundamental issue, with some political groups favouring the merger proposed by the commission. finally, the new tasks for berec set out in the code, namely the newly introduced arbitration decision of the berec association, which would be legally binding, helped us to reach a compromise on the committee on industry, research and energy. consequently, berec would also receive legal personality. in the trialogue with the council and the commission, we have done the opposite there will be no legally binding decisions in the code, and we therefore maintain the existing structure and status of berec as an independent association of regulators. i am aware that not all colleagues are satisfied with this most fundamental point. my big thanks go out to all the shadow reporters, anyway. i think we have managed to agree on a reasonable modernisation of the rules so that berec can be efficient, can fulfil the new tasks of the code, contribute to the uniform application of the regulatory framework for telecommunications and open up the european union's single market.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. – madam president, today we are giving our citizens concrete achievements. this 500—page law will mean a future of cheaper calls, safer citizens, faster internet, better access for persons with disabilities, and a final confirmation that internet access is no longer a luxury but a right. from 15 may next year, everyone will be able to make cheaper calls. international eu rates have been unjustly high for too long. the end of roaming in europe was a great success, but we stopped half way. i am proud and humbled to have led parliament to complete the final step in creating an eu where country codes are obsolete and where consumers can call anyone without worrying about the price. also, every citizen will now have a right to an affordable internet connection. as more and more services, including government services, go online, it is unthinkable to be without internet access today. the internet must be seen as a utility like electricity and gas. finally, i want to highlight the introduction of the mobile public warning system. in the 21st century, citizens should not have to rely on rumours to know what is happening in the case of an attack or emergency. there is much more, but i’m running out of time, so let me just thank the rapporteur, pilar del castillo vera, and all my fellow shadow rapporteurs who worked on this proposal.
madam president, i believe that we have agreed with the council on useful adjustments to the functioning of the association of european regulators berec and the berec support office, which will increase their efficiency and protect the independence of the regulators. i am convinced that the reality of the telecommunications markets has not made it necessary to make any major changes, but this does not, of course, preclude a debate on the future, on the contrary. i would like to mention one more important issue related to berec. the next step is to ensure that this smallest agency of the european union is strengthened in terms of staffing to provide full support to telecoms regulators. i therefore believe that in the context of the 2019 budget negotiations, the council will accept the relevant proposal of the european parliament. let me thank once again the cooperation of my colleagues, the shadow rapporteurs and all of our parliamentary team, including the assistants, advisers and administrators of the committee on industry, research and energy, because the cooperation has been excellent and very complicated. i would like to thank my colleague pilar del castilla again, because working with her has been really excellent. i would like to thank the bulgarian presidency and, on behalf of the commission, commissioner gabriel and the director-general of the connect directorate-general, robert viola, because they have really made a very good proposal and a very viable one for the future, and i believe that it will be a very successful work for the future, which will bring very good and useful connectivity to the citizens of the european union as time goes on.
in writing. i welcome the adoption of the european code on electronic communications, which the european parliament has agreed with the member states. it not only updates existing legislation to adapt to new technologies in a rapidly changing environment, including the introduction of the 5g mobile standard, but also brings positive improvements for end-users. i am glad that the social democrats have succeeded in ending the long-standing effort to fix the price of calls from one eu member state to another, because the different price levels have no justification in the internal market, as was the case with the pointless roaming, which we have also managed to abolish. this is of course good news for consumers in our country, as czech operators are among the most expensive eu providers of electronic communications among the 28 eu member states, according to european surveys. i personally consider it equally important to strengthen consumer protection, including for the most vulnerable. the new code guarantees that consumers with disabilities will not be disadvantaged in terms of the quality of service provision, its price or access to telecommunications services in general.
madam president, i regret to have to criticize here again the emissions proposal from the workshop of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, but unfortunately, this time too, the committee has not presented a rational document. the proposed targets call for 50% of the sales of zero-emission, so-called electric, urban buses in 2025 and even 75% in 2030. however, these objectives completely ignore any elementary logic of public transport being different from both passenger and technical neutrality. the charging of a single heavy vehicle requires a minimum power of 40 kw. for comparison, the usual reserve of a single household is 3 kw. if electric buses are to become the backbone of urban public transport, the garages of a typical transport undertaking will have a consumption comparable to the neighbourhood of the city in which they are located. another thing that we prefer not to talk about here is the safety of electric vehicles. the batteries contain two components necessary for combustion and are difficult to extinguish by conventional techniques. but in every heavy-duty vehicle, unlike a personal battery, there are several tons. the proposed emission targets in the environment, public health and food safety committee text are twice what analyses suggest is realistic and will lead to higher transport costs, higher consumer prices or an increase in the age of the fleet.
member of the commission. – madam president, i have listened carefully to the debate today and i have heard a broad range of positions, in particular on the level of ambition and on zero— and low—emission vehicle incentives. i would like to stress once more that the commission proposal is balanced, ambitious and fully in line with our climate objectives. some interventions are calling for higher ambition levels in 2025. with 15% in 2025, the commission proposal strikes the right balance between being realistic and ambitious. we have based it on a solid impact assessment because a 15% target can be achieved using technologies that are already available on the market but not yet widely deployed. today we have also heard calls for setting a binding and higher target for 2030. let me please remind you that while the 2025 target can be met by deploying readily available cost—effective technologies, achieving a more ambitious 2030 target would require the implementation of new technologies that are not yet on the market. there is currently a lack of robust information on how the performance and costs of those more innovative and prospective technologies might evolve in the medium term, and this creates a risk that a binding 2030 target would not be set at the most cost—effective level right now. the commission has therefore proposed to review the 2030 aspirational co2 emission targets in 2022 once sufficient certified co2 emission data has been collected and more information is available. some interventions also called for incentives for the deployment of zero— and low—emission vehicles, an objective that the commission supports. a crediting system based on a benchmark was considered in the impact assessment. however, it was not retained, in particular in view of the very low penetration of zero— and low—emission vehicles in the fleet and of the scarcity of robust data on such markets. adding a malus would mean that manufacturers meeting the co2 targets would actually be penalised if they did not meet the benchmark. instead, the commission proposal contains a super—credit system which also preserves the environmental integrity of the co2 target. co2 emissions standards cannot be set yet for buses, as the co2 emissions are not yet certified. however, as set out in the 2022 review clause, buses will be included in future standards. but, at the same time, this proposal can help to speed up the market uptake of zero—emission bus technologies by including then in the super—credit scheme. there is a competition distortion between manufacturers which is addressed through a cap. the proposed revision of the clean vehicle directive will be a key tool to steer public authorities’ decisions in purchasing zero—emission buses by creating a larger market for those vehicles. a platform has already been created to help cities make joint public procurement, and the european union budget will also continue to support the financing of a new fleet of clean buses. however, certain zero—emission buses mandates for manufacturers will not be proposed. we heard today some interventions calling for credits for alternative fuels. the commission proposal does not include such credits. our approach is based on the principle of clear responsibilities for clear actors. manufacturers are responsible for designing vehicles so that they achieve the co2 reductions required under the legislation. however, they cannot be held responsible for the type of fuel that is actually used in its vehicle or take advantage of it. more precisely, one vehicle is not necessarily linked to one fuel type as in many cases the same vehicle can use several fuels, either conventional, biofuels or other low carbon fuels. this being said, it does not mean that we are not taking action to decarbonise emissions from fuels. the eight—year reform will ensure decarbonisation of electricity production and the renewable energies directive will ensure that more low—carbon fuels will enter the market. in this context we need to apply the principle of better regulation and avoid double regulation and double accounting for incentivising renewable fuels. some interventions today raised concerns about the proposed level of the penalties, considering them too high and possibly putting at risk the economic situation of a manufacturer exceeding its targets. firstly, ensuring compliance is absolutely necessary for the effectiveness of any regulatory requirements. secondly, to be in line with the objectives of the regulation, financial penalties have to be set at a level that exceeds the marginal cost of meeting the targets. the penalties need to be a real deterrent. it should not be less expensive to pay penalties than to invest in technologies to meet targets. thirdly, the approach taken by the commission is to set an equivalent level of stringency from an environmental point of view, as compared with cars and buses. finally, this issue will be part of the review of the effectiveness of the legislation in 2022. to conclude, i would like to thank all of you for the interesting debate. i am looking forward to the upcoming vote. the commission is also confident that the council, under the leadership of the austrian presidency, will be able to reach swiftly a position so that the discussion between the two co—legislators can start this year. it is absolutely essential that the final political agreement between the co—legislators can be found before the end of the term of office of this parliament. i trust that this will be possible thanks to the dedication and hard work of the two co—legislators. again, my thanks to the rapporteur for a good, fast and ambitious job.
president of south africa. – mr president, it is indeed a great honour and a privilege to have this opportunity to address this gathering, particularly in the centenary year of the birth of nelson mandela, the founding father of our democratic nation, a revered statesman and a committed internationalist. we celebrate the centenary knowing well that nelson mandela does not belong to us as south africans. he belongs to all of us, and indeed to the world. (applause)he is one person who, through his life and deeds, proved to all the people across the world that championing human rights and doing what is right in terms of embracing universal values was the right thing to do, and that is precisely what he dedicated his life to. he belongs not only to those who lived through the tumultuous events of the last century, but he also belongs to the generations that are going to follow all of us. we live in the world that nelson mandela and many other great leaders of his age made. we are grateful to the european union and to the people, as well as the leaders, of europe for the tributes they have paid in many different ways to nelson mandela on the occasion of his birth, and for this we thank you from the bottom of our hearts. we say thank you for honouring nelson mandela in the way that you have. (applause)it is indeed a testament to the universal appeal of his vision that he is as revered on this continent as he is across the african continent. we remember with the nations of the world the centenary of the armistice that marked the end of the first world war. we pay tribute to all those who lost their lives in that great human tragedy, including the many south africans of all races who also fought in that war and who perished on these foreign shores. (applause) twenty—six years ago, in the city of maastricht, the leaders of europe signed the historic treaty that would mark a new era in the continent’s history. now, more than a quarter of a century later, that affirmation of democracy, of liberty, of respect for human rights, of fundamental freedoms and the rule of law continues to endure. these timeless values are indeed beacons of hope in our troubled times, for – despite our greatest efforts – the world today is blighted by conflict, poverty and underdevelopment in a number of poor countries. there is still a lack of meaningful social progress, unrest and displacement. the spectre of the resurgence of racism and xenophobia, cloaked in the mantle of nationalism, is rising and is causing a great deal of concern in a number of places. unilateralism is on the rise and threatens, in many ways, to undermine our collective commitment to democratic values and respect for human rights. now, more than ever, we turn to our great leaders for strength and inspiration as we seek to resolve the most pressing challenges of our times. we recall their words and their actions as we reaffirm our commitment to democratic values and upholding the rights of every human being. it was the great statesman and friend of the anti-apartheid cause, olof palme, the former prime minister of sweden, who said, ‘for us, democracy is a question of human dignity. this includes political liberties, the right to freely express our views, the right to criticise and to influence opinion. it embraces the right to health, work, to education and social security’. this view of democracy resonates with nelson mandela’s vision of a global community of nations that affirms the centrality of human dignity and strives through all means available to improve the human condition. just four months after his release from prison in 1990, nelson mandela paid his first visit to the european parliament to receive the sakharov prize for freedom of thought. he used this occasion to thank the leaders and the people of europe for their contribution to the liberation of south africa. i also stand here, like nelson mandela, and thank the people of europe for the contribution that they made to end apartheid, and we say thank you for all the support you gave us during the dark days of apartheid. thank you very much. (applause) nelson mandela also used the opportunity, as he addressed this european parliament, to call on the nations of europe to continue to stand by south africa as we sought to rebuild our country. we are here today to confirm that, indeed, the nations of europe answered the call that nelson mandela made with conviction and generosity. we are here today, following in the footsteps of nelson mandela, to say that we would urge you and we would call on you to continue supporting south africa as it has embarked on a journey, not only to reform itself, but to build a new south african nation from the ashes of apartheid. we thank you for continuing to support us. (applause)the european union was born out of the need to end the historic divisions on the continent, to create a firm base for the construction of a new europe rooted in solidarity and cooperation, and to contribute to global peace, security and development. the countries of africa have similarly embarked upon a new path of peace, development and transformation. the african union’s agenda 2063 provides a programme for integration and prosperity on a continent that has been wracked by division, exploitation and strife. nelson mandela was an ardent advocate for pan—africanism, and he believed that the continent could only reach her full potential if her peoples were united. in pursuing this goal, there are many lessons we can draw from the european union’s path to integration, to economic and political union, and to achieving social progress. south africa notes the tireless work of the european parliament to ensure that the provisions of the lisbon treaty on the eradication of poverty continue to underpin the eu’s approach to external development cooperation. since 2007, south africa and the eu have enjoyed the mutual benefits of a strategic partnership that continues to play a meaningful role in our country’s economic growth. it covers over 20 sectoral policy dialogues on diverse issues from development cooperation, science and technology, health, climate change, trade, education, skills development to peace migration, security and human rights as well. an integral part of this partnership are the interparliamentary meetings between the south african parliament and the european parliament. these meetings have indeed played a crucial role in strengthening south africa’s, as well as european, relations, contributing to a strategic partnership – a partnership that is based on equality, shared values and issues of mutual interest. allow me to express our sincere appreciation for the contribution that the european union – and in this case the european parliament – have made not only to the development of south africa, but also towards the achievement of progress across the african continent. nelson mandela led south africa in its peaceful transition to democracy, but he had a bigger dream: an africa at peace with itself and the world. in his lifetime, he supported the african union’s effort towards conflict prevention and resolution, peaceful building, as well as post-conflict reconstruction. he would have joined us in applauding the eu’s support for the au peace and security architecture and its steadfast support for efforts to silence the guns on the african continent for good. he always argued that lasting peace and security on the continent will not be possible without economic development. without opportunity, without investment, without thriving economies that enable us all to enjoy our place in the sun, we cannot hope to have political and social stability on the african continent. we therefore welcome the support of the european union for africa’s development needs through initiatives such as the european external investment plan, the economic partnership agreement and the new africa—european alliance for sustainable investment and jobs. we should strive to ensure that these approaches are developed and implemented in line with agenda 2063 of the african union. we should seek to ensure that these developmental initiatives support the establishment of the african continental free trade area and africa’s overall industrialisation infrastructure development, as well as economic transformation. we need to work together, as the countries of europe and africa, to develop sustainable and humane responses to the challenge of irregular migration. this is a challenge that europe has had to deal with in recent years. so, too, has south africa, and several african countries have had to deal with this challenge as well in a number of ways. we need to work together to address the root causes of irregular migration, which include poverty, inequality, unemployment, economic exclusion and competition for scarce resources. this should be informed by the principles of solidarity, compassion and an unwavering commitment to the human rights of all the people of the world. africa is the cradle of humankind. it is rich in land and water, abundant in minerals, and blessed with a young and vibrant population. it is the next frontier of growth, of innovation and of social progress. to realise this potential, the people of africa are in the process of uniting themselves. africa will continue to strengthen the bonds of partnership and friendship with the citizens of her sister continents and, in this case, her sister continent europe. it is therefore a matter of great satisfaction and promise that our partnership with the european union is as strong as it has been enduring. it is grounded in responsibility, respect and mutual accountability. the nations of the eu are a source of investment, trade, skills and knowledge, which has worked with us as south africa in our quest to grow our economy and improve the lives of our people. as we look to the future, we continue to count on the strength of this partnership between our countries and our peoples. as we advance forward to address the many challenges that our country faces – the challenges of unemployment, of poverty, and of inequality – we rely on europe to be our partner. right now, our country is in the process of transforming and reforming itself. we are seeking to create a south africa that is conducive for investment flows. a few weeks ago, we held an investment conference that attracted a number of european—based companies to come to south africa, and we were particularly overjoyed when many of them declared that they will continue to invest in south africa. we were able, at that conference – and another conference that followed – to raise usd 26 billion that will flow into south africa, and we are grateful for the support that we have enjoyed also from europe, for that vote of confidence in our economy. (applause)it is this collaboration and partnership that we seek – a collaboration that has bound south africa and the countries of europe together in a pact to never again return to the excesses and divisions of the past. as south africa, we are dealing with a number of issues that have to do with policies, policies that will consolidate south africa and make our country more stable. we are in the process of dealing with the question of land reform. land reform is one of those challenges that our country has had to face over hundreds of years, where the indigenous people of south africa were denied access to land, and have over the years been yearning to have access to land. we are now at a stage where we are engaging in a dialogue – an overarching dialogue among the people of south africa, those who own land and those who don’t own land – to see how best this important resource can be utilised to develop our country. through this, the dialogue is leading us to a point where we are seeking to find solutions that will be inclusive and that will enable all south africans, as we continue to consolidate our nation and to work together to resolve this problem. we have said very clearly that this problem of land will be resolved through adherence to the rule of law, through adherence to our constitution, and we will utilise the lessons that nelson mandela taught us with regard to resolving problems. we will resolve this problem in an inclusive basis, respecting each other’s human rights, respecting each other’s hopes and aspirations, and making sure – like we did when we resolved the apartheid problem – that we resolve it with a view to building a nation from the ashes of apartheid. this we shall do, because that is what nelson mandela taught us. i can assure you that we will resolve the land issue in line with the values that are enshrined in our constitution and, when we do so, we will have an all—inclusive solution that will make all the people of south africa work together and move together into the promised future that we see ahead of us. (applause) unity and partnership, as well as solidarity, are the principles that have underpinned our cooperation across two vast and different continents out of dark histories and into a promising new dawn. south africa, africa and the european union are bound by shared values of democracy and respect for human rights. we share a commitment to the ideals of free and prosperous societies, societies in which all persons live together in harmony with equal opportunities. we owe it to our great forebears, leaders of the calibre of nelson mandela, to focus on what unites us and not that which divides us, and to focus also on what benefits all of us and not what enriches and benefits just a few. we owe it to the leaders who are our forebears to build a strategic partnership that endures, that flourishes, and that – above all else – advances the cause of humanity. we thank you for your friendship, we thank you for your solidarity, we thank you for your partnership, and we thank you all for your support and for your continued help in everything that we are doing to rebuild the south african nation so that we, too, can enjoy the type of progress that you, as europeans, have achieved. thank you very much. mr president, i voted in favour of the european parliament's position on the negotiation of the next financial framework of the european union. i consider the overall budget level of 1.08 % of gdp of the european union to be realistic and moderate. i believe that citizens do not expect us to increase the volume of funds dramatically, but rather to spend them effectively on real priorities such as border protection, security and defence. i also support the idea of tripling the funding for the erasmus programme, and i agree that there should be no reduction in the funding for cohesion policy. on the contrary, i am opposed to the inclusion of the so-called child guarantee in the european social fund plus. as regards the revenue side, i agree with the requirement to tax large digital companies operating across the european union market.
mr. chairman, i did not vote for this press for several reasons. i'll name at least two. one of those reasons is the question of the european union's own resources. it is perhaps no surprise that our group is fundamentally opposed to creating more of our own budgetary resources, whether by any new taxation or otherwise. the european union is not a state, so it does not have its own budgetary resources and its budget is to be strictly, directly and closely linked to contributions, in particular those from the national states, from the member states. the second reason why i did not vote in favour is the overall structure of the european budgets. if we are serious about protecting the external borders and if we are serious about increasing expenditure on this chapter, this must also be taken into account in the overall budget restructuring, and unfortunately this has not happened. so i'm down and out.
mr. chairman, georgia is very important strategically and geopolitically. my political family has friends in the georgian political scene, we have partners even in the current georgian government, and we think that the european union should pay due attention to georgia, not least because this country has become a kind of model example of russian pressure some time ago, and it is good to continue to monitor and monitor the situation closely. that is why it is good that we are monitoring the implementation of the association agreement, that is why it is good that progress is being made here. when you look at the path georgia has taken since the collapse of the soviet union, it's truly remarkable what this country has accomplished. they certainly deserve the support of the european union and all of us for the future.
mr president, i was in moldova recently. this country is expected to hold parliamentary elections very soon after the new year. it is a country that is plagued by a number of ills, especially various corruption scandals and affairs that need to be carefully investigated. i have to say, from my own experience, from my own observation, that i feel that there is a lack of consistency, that in some cases the affairs are simply swept under the carpet, while in other cases, for political reasons, the exponents are subjected to great pressure. it's also a country that faces, let's call it, russian meddling, media manipulation. moldova has already taken some measures to counter this pressure, but this needs to be continued and, again, developments in the country need to be closely monitored.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, this is a very serious issue, and i am very happy to see that, judging so far by the speakers that have spoken, we have a very broad consensus here in parliament. the fight against disinformation, from my point of view, involves two main aspects. one is enhancing our resilience against those that are spreading disinformation and the other is appropriate education awareness, in order to be able to differentiate between what is really disinformation and what is not. if i look at these two main aspects, i need to say that on the first one, one should also emphasise the regulatory framework, and i did listen very carefully to you, madam state secretary and the council’s attitude. to be honest, i use that as an opportunity to say i don’t see it, for example, in the trilogue on the cyber security act. i am not talking about the presidency. i know it is a very tough task to achieve, but the mindset in some member states that co-decide on the common position has not changed. that brings me to the second aspect, and that is that education awareness is not just about citizens; it is about politicians. i very much appreciate what commissioner jourová has said and that is that the main threat is russia. if we don’t use the proper words then we will be failing, and i must say that, once again, i don’t hear that clear language from the council. there is clear evidence of that in terms of scale, coordination and also the stated willingness of the russians to do so, and it is increasingly dominant. the european elections are behind doors. they are absolutely key. they are also a test on what we have just spoken about and what we are tackling. a final remark, on the east stratcom. once again, i do appreciate what the commissioner said. it is understaffed, but it is doing excellent work. it needs a permanent budget and i would appreciate it if we could see more emphasis from the commission – i must say i was never impressed by the attitude of high representative mogherini in this respect: she can provide better assistance – but also emphasis from the council. that is key. they are excellent and we are failing to support them.
mr. chairman, free elections are not just about being able to choose from more than one candidate, but also about elections being free from misinformation and manipulation. and we are in a disinformation war with russia, a war that threatens our democracies. it is only in this context that we appreciate the vision of the schuman declaration, which states, among other things, that 'world peace could not be maintained without creative efforts commensurate with the dangers which threaten it.' that means in our situation, we have to put at least as much effort and resources into fighting disinformation as the adversary does on the other side of the barricade. so we're talking at least hundreds of millions of euros. but democracy is worth it. lies are cheap, but truth is expensive. now this disinformation is threatening the european elections. this is therefore a europe-wide challenge requiring continent-wide cooperation. i am glad that the european commission's proposals are geared to what we really need. in addition to data protection and financing controls, we need above all this: quality technical monitoring tools must be available in all member states. the european union must be able to coordinate these instruments. the national teams must have sufficient capacity at european level to disseminate news to counter fake news. this could be paid advertising on the internet or cooperation with public media. the dissemination of these verification reports must be in real time within the range of hours, this is crucial. we need special units with good algorithms and with a single task to detect, report and block disinformation. assistance through platforms like facebook or twitter is essential. but above all, we need the will, the collective will to fight against misinformation. i see that will in the european parliament, with certain exceptions, and i am glad of it.
mr president, the fight against disinformation should be a priority for political and security reasons for the member states and the european union as a whole. in the area of security, counter-intelligence expenditure should therefore also be a national priority, as interference in internal affairs, including election interference, is also a security threat to member states. i would like to point out that the czech republic, for example, has set up a centre against terrorism and hybrid threats to, among other things, detect disinformation campaigns, and i believe that this will be worthwhile for the czech republic. the forthcoming european parliament elections will be a test of our resilience, and there will undoubtedly be an attempt to influence their outcome in the same way as in the united states or in the case of brexit. russia will support an anti-european disinformation campaign. this hybrid warfare is more effective than violence in today's digital age, and we need to confront it by teaching critical thinking, media literacy and supporting public media. in this context, i would like to call on the european external action service to increase financial support for its component called stratcom. it came into force in 2015, and has uncovered three and a half thousand cases of pro-russian disinformation. however, this activity needs more financial support. unfortunately, stratcom's spending is incomparably smaller than russia's media spending.
mr. chairman, as early as 2013, the chief of the general staff of the russian armed forces, general valery gerasimov, outlined the principles of hybrid warfare. the gerasimov doctrine emphasizes that nonmilitary means can be more effective than traditional weapons. a word, properly chosen and applied, can be more destructive than tanks or missile systems. one of the divisions of this gerasimov hybrid army is influencing the electoral process. for example: the dutch referendum on the ratification of the association agreement between the european union and ukraine, the referendum on the withdrawal of the united kingdom from the european union, the german elections in 2017, the french presidential elections in the same year. disinformation has flooded and affected the presidential elections in my country, the czech republic. the victory of pro-russian president zeman helped greatly. stopping the artificial erosion of trust in democratic institutions must finally become a central point of the agenda of the member states, but also of the european union itself. i therefore strongly urge the heads of state or government and the high representative for foreign affairs and security policy to make a fundamental shift in the perception of this strategic danger at the december summit. i expect to see a very ambitious action plan with concrete steps. the next major target of disinformation campaigns and possible cyber-attacks will be the european parliament elections in may 2019. i would hope that we're more prepared than we have been in the past.
mr president, as the representative of a country which has one of the most dense rail networks in the world, i am very pleased to see the further deepening of passenger rights in rail transport. passengers should now be entitled to access to information and compensation in the event of delay or cancellation. they should be informed of their rights, the connections involved and other related matters before, during and after the journey itself. i am also pleased that we have succeeded in making rail transport more accessible to disabled people or those with reduced mobility. if we are to convince the citizens of the european union that train travel is a tradition worth continuing, it is necessary to raise the level of this transport and the rights of passengers. the european parliament should set an example in this area.
madam president, if it were a month ago, i probably would have structured my intervention today differently. i would focus on some of the provisions, highlight something and support some things, but in fact, i’m going to say something different. i have a relatively fresh experience. after the very long vote in the last plenary on thursday, i was to take a train from here to karlsruhe and then to munich and catch a plane. because we had a long vote, an unnecessarily long vote, i didn’t manage. then i missed the tgv, so i had to take several trains before i could get to karlsruhe, then stuttgart and then to munich – and you can already guess what happened with my plane connection in munich. of course, i spent some extra money. of course, my journey to the balkans was much longer, but i do still understand that my position is somewhat more favourable than that of thousands or millions of european consumers when they arrive, or when they end up in a similar position. i really believe that what we will be voting on is not perfect. i’m sure that many of us, on a number of provisions, would like to go further. i’m sure that there are still some question marks, but it is a major step in the right direction to provide railway passengers with completely different quality rights, and not just in the terms that i’ve mentioned – accessibility, etc. i think this is a good indication of what european legislation can bring a consumer.
madam president, i have often reflected on this report, what my severely disabled trainee, who uses an electric wheelchair, told me about her train journey. let me quote her experience: "for me, ordering a shipment means planning my activities 24 hours in advance. i live in a rural area, so i cannot decide, for example, to go to a nearby city in the morning to see my doctor. i can't schedule an evening movie in the morning and stuff. it would also welcome a reduction in the time needed to order transport, to keep platforms in good condition, to improve the organisation of transport for wheelchairs, to improve staff training and to facilitate access to information. in the end of his letter, he writes: "the right to travel freely and independently should be a right of each of us. i wholeheartedly agree with this and thank mr bogusław for taking these comments seriously and for supporting a number of my proposals, such as the reduction of the time needed to order assistance services.
for the group gue/ngl madam president, the proposed regulation implements the european union's commitments under the stockholm convention on persistent organic pollutants and the protocol thereto. the fundamental objective of these international treaties and, subsequently, of the regulation is to protect health and the environment from the harmful effects of this group of chemicals. unfortunately, some of the major amendments were not sent by the committee on the environment, public health and food safety and the proposal was therefore weakened considerably, which is why i have read them again in plenary. so i'm asking you, along with environmental and consumer organizations, to vote in favor of stricter limits for some other substances. there is a need to ban proven toxic substances as soon as possible, globally, in both new and recycled products. and it's the recycled plastics that are going to make these things disappear faster. i would like to specifically ask you to support the amendments that will ensure that european consumers are protected from exposure to these substances, in particular from recycled products, and thus promote a clean circular economy. i propose to update the limits for short-chain chlorinated paraffins as proposed in a 2015 study initiated by the german environment agency and for the amount of pbde that is in line with international obligations to limit persistent organic pollutants under the stockholm convention. it is absolutely essential, ladies and gentlemen, that the revised version of the regulation be adopted as soon as possible. it is absolutely essential if we are to protect european citizens.
for several weeks, i have been warning colleagues about the public health implications of the legislative gaps and that i do not agree that this is a mere technical adjustment to international commitments. on the contrary, it is a decision with a significant social impact, the consequences of which will be felt for decades to come. the eu's current approach to recycling and limiting persistent organic pollutants is toxic to the point of being toxic. especially if there are safer alternatives. if we want to guarantee the safety of the materials we recycle, we must first eliminate the amount of hazardous substances in the primary products, not put them back into circulation. and certainly not in the form of contaminated kitchen utensils or children's toys. for those of you who don't know, pops are substances that accumulate in the environment, from where they get into our food chain. in humans, they interfere with the functioning of the hormonal, nervous, and immune systems. unfortunately, it takes too long for us in the eu to ban these dangerous substances completely, which means that we will have to deal with their legacy for a long time to come. for example, next year the five-year transition period for hbcd will end. but we'll have this toxic substance built into our homes for decades to come. i think we can all agree that this is not a legacy we should be passing on to our children.
mr. chairman, many academics today are talking about us being in what's called a post-factual age. i mean, in a time when you can say almost anything and not even put the facts on the table. i think donald trump is a beautiful example of this phenomenon. not only do i often get the impression that he's a dream come true, but now it's primarily about what he does and what he says. in october, he announced that the united states would withdraw from the intermediate-range nuclear forces treaty with russia, claiming that moscow was violating it. by presenting evidence or calling for an investigation, trump is not bothered and he threatens. they say the road to hell is paved with good intentions. all too often, however, it is patched up by the actions of selfish political amateurs who overestimate their role in the international arena. no one can threaten peace and international agreements. peace is not a gamble, it is a care. and that is the signal that we as the european union must send.
mr president, the field of care services is undoubtedly important and will become even more important in the future in view of demographic trends. this sector is primarily the responsibility of the member states, which have an interest in improving the conditions for all workers in these services, whether women or men. it is the member states that must put the emphasis on the quality and accessibility of all care services. i see the connection between this report and the directive on reconciling private and professional life. in this area, i also support the inclusion of caring leave in the work permit, so that carers have the best possible working conditions. but the member states' competence in this matter must be respected. the gender perspective is only limited to care services. the report in this respect, i dare say, is not balanced and omits a number of factors that should be taken into account, because care services are really complex, they touch on other aspects such as age, such as vulnerable groups and other factors. i consider problematic, among other things, the calls for separate taxation of spouses, the widest possible concept of the family, the commission to regulate and control the quality of services, the establishment of a binding framework for minimum standards of working conditions in this sector and the guarantee of financial support for carers. these and other points, in my view, significantly interfere with the member states' competence. i would like to say that not only in my opinion, but also in the opinion of some of the group of the european people's party. but thank you for making this report a source of inspiration for our future work.
mr president, i would first of all like to thank my colleague, mrs merja kyllönen, for bringing this subject to the plenary, and as it turns out, it is a subject which goes across europe, which goes across political groups. it's a subject we all want to address. we can, of course, address it by saying in europe that we know it exists, but we should address it primarily by linking our healthcare capacities, and primarily by changing the fact that there is still no consensus in the european union on how to treat, diagnose and screen for lyme disease. the problem is, the diagnosis of the disease is not happening at the time we need it. patients are very often left with some other trivial treatment that doesn't work, and then the really expert treatment happens when there's really not much help and there's a lot of other problems. i would therefore like to ask, and thank the commissioner very much for saying that we will generally be looking at better research into this disease, better searching for causes, but also better searching for treatment. i'm asking her to do it, and we're putting the money into it, and we can't do it without it, and if we don't find it, we have no chance of changing it.
author. mr president, vietnam is a dynamic developing country with a promising future. as in china, however, there is a massive violation of human rights and freedoms under the rule of the communist party. we also see a paradox similar to other so-called communist systems - the absence of trade unions, the massive violation of workers' rights or the reliance of the media on political actors. reporters without borders index reports that in 2018, the media's reliance on the communist party even increased. there is virtually no free press, and any independent expression in the media is punishable by imprisonment in most cases. i also want to mention that the death penalty is still a hot topic in vietnam and it is all the more alarming that vietnam keeps the number of those sentenced to death secret from the international community. these are grave facts that we cannot remain silent about. getting china to do something is difficult, but in the case of vietnam, we have a tool in our hands. the signing of the free trade agreement between the european union and vietnam is due to take place. in this resolution, the european parliament shows that it is not indifferent to the situation of political prisoners, bloggers, activists, christian clergy and the masses of the population, who are monitored by the regime with the help of modern technologies. the european parliament will insist on improvements by vietnam, or it will not give its consent to the trade agreement. vietnam is a country that is paradoxically very close to me, because there are 100,000 vietnamese people in the czech republic, which is the largest community in europe. i would therefore like the european union to have good economic relations with vietnam. but it cannot be done without conditions, and i am also glad that there is a broad consensus across the political parties in the european parliament on this.
mr president, i would like to stress that vietnam has indeed made substantial progress in the past period, but it is important to remember that it is not really a free state, that it is by no means democratic. if we talk about one side, where there is no opposition, there are no unions, if we talk about the lack of freedom of the media, the lack of pluralism of opinion, on the contrary, even for a different opinion there are political prisoners, then it is clear that this is not the democratic character of the state, and nobody even doubts it. we have not only political prisoners here, but also religious prisoners. what more can we do? we have the tool of the trade agreement here, i think we should put more pressure on the vietnamese government. of course, our diplomatic efforts, our delegations going to vietnam, must also address these fundamental reservations. we can talk, and therefore we should really talk about these pressing issues in these delegations, and i of course support this resolution.
the author. mr president, the european parliament has already awarded the sakharov prize to cuban human rights defenders in 2002, 2005 and 2010. according to today's resolution, cuba remains a totalitarian state with a single communist party that does not bother to respect basic human rights. defenders of these rights, or just those who want to express themselves or assemble freely, are intimidated or illegally imprisoned. in october alone, more than 200 cubans were imprisoned in this way simply for exercising their rights under the universal declaration of human rights. the state authorities do not allow the existence of human rights organizations and prevent their representatives from traveling. religious freedom in cuba is minimal, and church leaders are under the supervision of state authorities. cuba has a strict censorship policy and does not allow any media other than state media to exist. this situation also holds us, the european union and its member states, partly responsible. we were the ones who did not insist on a clear conditionality of the application of the new agreement with cuba with respect for fundamental rights. however, even this shortcoming does not diminish our obligation to insist on respect for these rights in all the external relations of the european union, as required by article 21 (1) of the treaty on european union. the eu must therefore take a clear stand on the fundamental rights of the cuban people and seize all the economic opportunities that the new treaty with cuba has opened up. we can't be fooled into holding elections where independent candidates can't run. the maximum possible contact with civil society must be strengthened and all repressive methods used by the cuban state must be constantly pointed out.
